# PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition & PMP® Exam — Complete Study Guide

**A self-study companion for the *PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition* (PMI, 2025) and the PMP® Examination Content Outline that took effect in July 2026**

> **Read this first.** This is an independent study aid written to help you understand, remember and *apply* the content you will be tested on. It explains the concepts of the *PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition* in plain language and connects them to the way PMP® questions are written. It does not reproduce the PMBOK® Guide, so keep your copy of the Guide next to you and use this book to make sense of it. Exam logistics (number of questions, time, fees, eligibility) can change, so confirm them in PMI's current *PMP Certification Handbook* before you book your exam. PMI, PMP, CAPM and PMBOK are registered marks of the Project Management Institute, Inc.

## How this study package is organised

| File | What it is | When to use it |
|---|---|---|
| **01 – Study Guide** (this file) | 21 chapters + glossary. Each chapter ends with review questions; answers with explanations are in Appendix B. | Weeks 1–6: read one chapter, then answer its questions *before* looking at the key. |
| **02 – Comprehensive Question Bank** | Questions covering the whole body of knowledge, grouped in parts, each part followed by its answers and explanations. | After you finish the guide, to find weak areas. |
| **03 – Demo Exams 01–10** | The ten demo exams as documents (180 questions, 240 minutes each) with answer keys, explanations and a score-analysis sheet. | If you prefer to practise on paper. |
| **04 – Exam Simulator** | The exam platform: the ten demo exams plus mixed exams drawn from all of them, with a timer, breaks, flagging, scoring by domain and task, and a PDF of your corrections after every attempt. | Weeks 7–8 (and beyond): one exam per sitting, under real conditions. |

## How to study with this guide

1. **Read actively.** Each chapter starts with what you should be able to do afterwards. Keep a notebook of terms you could not explain to a colleague.
2. **Test yourself honestly.** Answer the review questions without looking back. Mark any question you guessed as "wrong" even if you guessed right.
3. **Close the gaps.** If you score below 80% on a chapter, re-read the parts you missed before moving on.
4. **Integrate.** After the last chapter, take the Comprehensive Question Bank. It mixes topics the way the exam does.
5. **Simulate.** Take the demo exams under exam conditions: 240 minutes, no notes, breaks only after questions 60 and 120. Review *every* explanation — including those for questions you got right.
6. **Think like PMI.** Chapter 21 explains the mindset behind the "best" answer. Re-read it before every demo exam.

### Suggested 8-week plan (about 10–12 hours per week)

| Week | Read | Practise |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Ch. 1–4 (exam, PMBOK 8 structure, value delivery, principles) | Chapter questions |
| 2 | Ch. 5–7 (life cycles, focus areas & processes, Governance) | Chapter questions |
| 3 | Ch. 8–11 (Scope, Quality, Schedule, Finance) | Chapter questions + formula drills (Ch. 20) |
| 4 | Ch. 12–14 (Stakeholders, Resources & procurement, Risk) | Chapter questions |
| 5 | Ch. 15–17 (Agile & hybrid, People, Business environment) | Chapter questions |
| 6 | Ch. 18–21 (Tailoring, toolbox, formulas, exam strategy) | Comprehensive Question Bank |
| 7 | Revise weak chapters | Demo Exams 01–04 |
| 8 | Revise weak domains; re-read Ch. 21 | Demo Exams 05–10 (retake any below 75%) |

### Conventions used in this guide

- **Exam tip** boxes tell you how a concept tends to appear in questions.
- **Trap** boxes describe a wrong answer that looks attractive.
- **What changed** boxes compare the Eighth Edition with the Sixth and Seventh Editions — useful if you have older materials.
- Process names follow the Eighth Edition; when an older name is still common in prep materials it is given in brackets, e.g. *Develop Scope Structure (formerly Create WBS)*.

# Chapter 1 — The PMP® Exam (2026) and How the PMBOK® Guide Fits

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe the format of the PMP® exam that has been delivered since July 2026;
- list the three exam domains, their weights and the 26 tasks;
- explain the relationship between the exam content outline and the *PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition*;
- plan your preparation and know what "good enough" looks like on practice exams.

## 1.1 Which certification does this guide target?

PMI offers several certifications. The two that rely most heavily on the PMBOK® Guide are:

| | **PMP® (Project Management Professional)** | **CAPM® (Certified Associate in Project Management)** |
|---|---|---|
| Who it is for | Practitioners who have led projects | Entry-level and aspiring practitioners |
| Experience required | Yes (see 1.6) | No |
| Question style | Mostly situational ("What should the project manager do next?") | More knowledge-based ("Which artifact…?") |
| Based on | PMP Examination Content Outline (ECO) | CAPM Examination Content Outline |

This guide is written for the **PMP**, because it is the certification most people mean when they study the PMBOK® Guide. Everything you need for the CAPM is also here: the CAPM tests the same vocabulary, processes, life cycles, agile frameworks and business-analysis basics, only at a lower level of difficulty.

## 1.2 Exam format (from 9 July 2026)

PMI introduced a new PMP Examination Content Outline (ECO) in July 2026. The most widely reported format is:

| Element | Detail |
|---|---|
| Number of questions | **180** — of which **170 are scored** and **10 are unscored pretest items** (you cannot tell which ones) |
| Time | **240 minutes** |
| Breaks | Two optional 10-minute breaks. Once you start a break you cannot go back to the previous block of questions |
| Delivery | Pearson VUE test centre or online proctored |
| Item types | Multiple choice (one answer), multiple response (choose two or three), matching / drag-and-drop, hotspot (click on part of a graphic), limited fill-in-the-blank (usually a calculation), and **case-study sets** in which several questions share one scenario that may include tables or charts |
| Approaches | About **40% predictive**, about **60% agile or hybrid** |

> **Exam tip.** Pace yourself at roughly **80 seconds per question**. With 240 minutes for 180 questions you have 1.33 minutes per question. A good checkpoint plan is: question 60 at about 80 minutes, question 120 at about 160 minutes, leaving a few minutes at the end to revisit flagged items.

> **Trap.** Some websites still show the pre-2026 format (230 minutes, 175 scored + 5 unscored questions). Always check the current PMP Certification Handbook on pmi.org.

## 1.3 The three domains

The exam is organised into three **domains** (areas of practice). The 2026 outline moved a lot of weight from People and Process into the Business Environment:

| Domain | 2021 outline | **2026 outline** | What it covers |
|---|---|---|---|
| **People** | 42% | **33%** | Leading and developing the team, conflict, stakeholder engagement and expectations, communication, knowledge transfer |
| **Process** | 50% | **41%** | Planning and managing scope, schedule, finance, resources, procurement, quality, value delivery, status and closure |
| **Business Environment** | 8% | **26%** | Governance, compliance, change control, impediments and issues, risk, continuous improvement, organisational change, external environment |

On 180 questions this is roughly **59 People, 74 Process and 47 Business Environment** questions.

## 1.4 The 26 tasks

Each domain contains tasks. Each task has **enablers** — examples of the work involved. You do not need to memorise task numbers, but you should know *where* a topic lives, because it tells you what the question is really testing.

### Domain I — People (33%)

| # | Task | What questions typically test |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Develop a common vision | Helping the team and stakeholders share one understanding of the goal: vision statements, project/product vision, team charter, alignment workshops |
| 2 | Manage conflicts | Recognising the source and stage of conflict, choosing an appropriate resolution approach, facilitating rather than imposing |
| 3 | Lead the project team | Servant leadership, empowerment, motivation, coaching, team development, psychological safety, performance |
| 4 | Engage stakeholders | Identifying and analysing stakeholders, building relationships, choosing engagement strategies |
| 5 | Align stakeholder expectations | Surfacing expectations early, facilitating agreement among stakeholders with different interests |
| 6 | Manage stakeholder expectations | Monitoring satisfaction, adjusting engagement, keeping expectations consistent with what can be delivered |
| 7 | Help ensure knowledge transfer | Lessons learned, documentation, mentoring, handovers, transfer of knowledge to operations and to future projects |
| 8 | Plan and manage communication | Communication requirements, methods, frequency, channels, feedback, information radiators, reporting |

### Domain II — Process (41%)

| # | Task | What questions typically test |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Develop an integrated project management plan and plan delivery | Choosing the approach, integrating subsidiary plans, planning releases and iterations |
| 2 | Develop and manage project scope | Requirements, scope statement, WBS/backlog, validating deliverables, preventing scope creep |
| 3 | Help ensure value-based delivery | Identifying value with stakeholders, prioritising by value, incremental delivery, measuring benefits |
| 4 | Plan and manage resources | Team and physical resources, acquisition, allocation, resource constraints |
| 5 | Plan and manage procurement | Make-or-buy, contract types, selecting sellers, administering contracts |
| 6 | Plan and manage finance | Estimates, budgets, reserves, earned value, forecasting, tracking spend |
| 7 | Plan and optimise quality of products/deliverables | Quality planning, acceptance criteria, definition of done, quality assurance and control |
| 8 | Plan and manage schedule | Estimating, sequencing, critical path, compression, release planning, velocity |
| 9 | Evaluate project status | Performance measurement, metrics, dashboards, reporting, forecasts |
| 10 | Manage project closure | Formal acceptance, transition, contract closure, lessons learned, archiving, releasing resources |

### Domain III — Business Environment (26%)

| # | Task | What questions typically test |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | Define and establish project governance | Decision rights, escalation paths, phase gates, roles (sponsor, steering committee, PMO, CCB) |
| 2 | Plan and manage project compliance | Regulatory, legal, contractual and organisational requirements; audits; consequences of non-compliance |
| 3 | Manage and control changes | Integrated change control, change requests, the change control board, backlog-based change in agile |
| 4 | Remove impediments and manage issues | Issue logs, escalation, servant-leader impediment removal |
| 5 | Plan and manage risk | Risk planning, identification, analysis, responses, monitoring, reserves |
| 6 | Continuous improvement | Retrospectives, lessons learned, process improvement, Kaizen, PDCA |
| 7 | Support organisational change | Readiness, change management models, adoption, resistance, transition to operations |
| 8 | Evaluate external business environment changes | Regulations, market, technology, economic and social changes and their effect on the project |

> **What changed.** Risk and change control now sit in the **Business Environment** domain, and the **Process** domain has separate tasks for *finance*, *resources* and *procurement*. Expect more questions on governance, compliance, sustainability, artificial intelligence and organisational change than in older practice tests.

## 1.5 How the PMBOK® Guide relates to the exam

The PMP exam is built from the **Examination Content Outline**, not from a single book. PMI uses several references when it writes questions, and the *PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition* is the core one. Questions rarely ask you to recite the Guide. Instead they describe a situation and ask what a competent project manager would do. To answer them you need three things:

1. **Vocabulary and concepts** from the PMBOK® Guide (principles, performance domains, processes, artifacts, techniques).
2. **Judgment** — knowing which action comes *first*, which is *best* and which is merely possible.
3. **Context awareness** — recognising whether the scenario is predictive, agile or hybrid, because the right action can differ.

## 1.6 Eligibility and scoring (summary)

To apply for the PMP you need **35 contact hours** of project management education (or an active CAPM) and experience leading projects within the last eight years:

- **36 months** with a four-year (bachelor's) degree or global equivalent;
- **60 months** with a high-school diploma or associate's degree;
- **24 months** with a bachelor's or postgraduate degree from a GAC-accredited programme.

Your application may be selected for **audit**, in which case you must provide evidence (diplomas, training certificates, supervisor signatures).

**Scoring.** PMI does not publish a fixed pass mark. A panel of experts determines the passing standard using psychometric methods, and your report shows **Pass/Fail** plus a rating per domain: **Above Target, Target, Below Target, Needs Improvement**. As a practical rule, if you consistently score **75% or more** on good-quality full-length practice exams, you are in a strong position.

## 1.7 What the exam rewards — a first look at the "PMI mindset"

You will see this in detail in Chapter 21. In short, the best answer usually:

- **analyses before acting** — understand the situation, check the plan, assess the impact;
- **addresses the root cause** rather than a symptom;
- **involves the right people** — talk directly to the person concerned, collaborate with the team, engage the sponsor or product owner when it is their decision;
- **follows the agreed process** (for example, a change request in predictive work, backlog prioritisation by the product owner in agile work);
- **serves the team** — remove impediments, coach, protect focus, empower decisions;
- **keeps value, ethics and sustainability in view.**

Answers that ignore a problem, escalate too early, blame people, bypass governance or let the project manager make decisions that belong to others are usually wrong.

# Chapter 2 — PMBOK® Guide 8 at a Glance: Structure, Evolution and Core Vocabulary

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe how the Eighth Edition is organised and why it changed;
- explain the "why – what – how" logic of principles, performance domains and processes;
- define the core terms used everywhere in the exam (project, program, portfolio, operations, value, outcome, benefit…);
- translate older knowledge-area vocabulary into Eighth-Edition terms.

## 2.1 What the PMBOK® Guide is

The *PMBOK® Guide – Eighth Edition* (published by PMI in late 2025) is a single volume containing two parts:

1. **The Standard for Project Management** — a short, consensus-based (ANSI-accredited) description of *what* project management is: the system for value delivery, the project environment and the **six principles** that guide behaviour.
2. **A Guide to the Project Management Body of Knowledge** — the larger part, describing the **seven performance domains**, the **40 processes** grouped in **five focus areas**, **tailoring**, and the **models, methods and artifacts** practitioners use.

It closes with appendices. Those most relevant to the exam are **X2 – Project Management Office (PMO)**, **X3 – Artificial Intelligence (AI)** and **X4 – Procurement**, plus a glossary. (X1 lists contributors and X5 describes the evolution of the Guide.)

> **Exam tip.** The PMBOK® Guide is *descriptive and non-prescriptive*: it describes good practice that is applicable to most projects most of the time, and the project team **tailors** it. An answer that says "always follow every process in the Guide" is suspicious.

## 2.2 How we got here — the evolution of the Guide

| Edition | Year | Organising idea |
|---|---|---|
| 1st–5th | 1996–2013 | Process-based: process groups × knowledge areas, inputs/tools & techniques/outputs (ITTOs) |
| **6th** | 2017 | **5 process groups, 10 knowledge areas, 49 processes**; agile content added in each knowledge area; *Agile Practice Guide* bundled |
| **7th** | 2021 | Radical shift to **principles**: **12 principles**, **8 performance domains**, tailoring, **models, methods and artifacts**; processes moved to PMIstandards+ |
| **8th** | 2025 | Synthesis: **6 principles, 7 performance domains, 5 focus areas, 40 processes**, tailoring, models/methods/artifacts, new appendices on PMO, AI and procurement |

Why the change? Practitioners found the Sixth Edition too process-heavy for adaptive work, and the Seventh Edition too abstract to apply ("we know the principles, but *how* do we do it?"). The Eighth Edition keeps the principles-and-domains foundation of the Seventh Edition, simplifies it, and **reintroduces processes** — but as non-prescriptive guidance that applies to predictive, adaptive and hybrid work alike.

### The three layers: why, what and how

| Layer | Eighth-Edition element | Question it answers |
|---|---|---|
| **Why** | **6 principles** | What beliefs and behaviours should guide every decision? |
| **What** | **7 performance domains** | Which areas of activity must be managed effectively for the project to deliver its outcomes? |
| **How** | **40 processes in 5 focus areas** + tailoring + models, methods and artifacts | What do we actually do, in which order, with which tools? |

### The six principles (Chapter 4)

1. **Adopt a holistic view**
2. **Focus on value**
3. **Embed quality into processes and deliverables**
4. **Be an accountable leader**
5. **Integrate sustainability within all project areas**
6. **Build an empowered culture**

### The seven performance domains (Chapters 7–14)

| Domain | Main concern | Number of processes |
|---|---|---|
| **Governance** | Decision structures, oversight, integration of plans, execution, quality assurance, knowledge, change, closure | 9 |
| **Scope** | Requirements, scope definition and structure, validation and control (including quality control) | 6 |
| **Schedule** | Planning, developing and controlling the schedule | 3 |
| **Finance** | Financial planning, cost estimates, budget, financial control and funding | 4 |
| **Stakeholders** | Identification, engagement and communications | 7 |
| **Resources** | Team and physical/virtual resources, acquisition, leadership, resource control | 5 |
| **Risk** | Risk planning, identification, analysis, responses and monitoring | 6 |
| | **Total** | **40** |

### The five focus areas (Chapter 6)

**Initiating → Planning → Executing → Monitoring and Controlling → Closing.** These are the former *process groups*. They describe the *nature* of the work, not phases in time; they repeat in every phase, release or iteration.

## 2.3 What happened to the ten knowledge areas?

If you studied with Sixth-Edition material, this translation table will save you time:

| Sixth Edition knowledge area | Where it lives in the Eighth Edition |
|---|---|
| Integration Management | **Governance** domain (charter, integrated plan, execution, knowledge, monitoring, change control, closure) |
| Scope Management | **Scope** domain |
| Schedule Management | **Schedule** domain (activity definition, sequencing and estimating are folded into *Develop Schedule*) |
| Cost Management | **Finance** domain (broadened to funding and financial value) |
| Quality Management | Spread out: quality **planning** inside *Define Scope*, quality **assurance** in Governance (*Manage Quality Assurance*), quality **control** inside *Monitor and Control Scope* — reflecting the principle *Embed quality* |
| Resource Management | **Resources** domain (*Develop Team* and *Manage Team* merge into *Lead the Team*) |
| Communications Management | **Stakeholders** domain |
| Risk Management | **Risk** domain (qualitative and quantitative analysis merge into *Perform Risk Analysis*) |
| Procurement Management | Sourcing strategy in **Governance** (*Plan Sourcing Strategy*); acquisition and control through the **Resources** domain; strategic detail in **Appendix X4** |
| Stakeholder Management | **Stakeholders** domain |

And the Seventh Edition's eight domains map like this: *Stakeholders → Stakeholders; Team → Resources; Development Approach & Life Cycle → Governance and tailoring; Planning → spread across all domains; Project Work → Governance and Resources; Delivery → Scope (plus value and quality); Measurement → Governance (monitoring) and each domain's control process; Uncertainty → Risk.* Schedule and Finance became domains in their own right.

## 2.4 Core vocabulary

You must be able to use these terms precisely — many distractors depend on confusing them.

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Project** | A **temporary** endeavour undertaken to create a **unique** product, service or result. Temporary = definite beginning and end (not short); unique = something not done exactly before. |
| **Progressive elaboration** | Developing plans and details iteratively as more information becomes available. |
| **Project management** | The application of knowledge, skills, tools and techniques to project activities to meet project requirements and deliver value. |
| **Operations** | Ongoing, repetitive work that sustains the organisation (e.g., running payroll). Projects often *change* operations. |
| **Program** | Related projects, subsidiary programs and program activities managed in a **coordinated** way to obtain **benefits** not available from managing them individually. |
| **Portfolio** | Projects, programs, subsidiary portfolios and operations managed **as a group** to achieve **strategic objectives**. Components need not be related to each other. |
| **Product** | An artifact that is produced, quantifiable, and can be an end item or a component. Products often outlive the projects that create or change them. |
| **Deliverable** | Any unique and verifiable product, result or capability required to complete a process, phase or project. |
| **Output** | What the project produces (a deliverable). |
| **Outcome** | The end result or consequence of using the output (e.g., faster order processing). |
| **Benefit** | A gain realised by the organisation and beneficiaries from the outcome (e.g., lower costs, higher customer satisfaction). |
| **Value** | The worth, importance or usefulness of something — financial or non-financial. Projects exist to create value. |
| **Organisational project management (OPM)** | A framework that integrates portfolio, program and project management with organisational enablers to achieve strategic objectives. |
| **Stakeholder** | An individual, group or organisation that may affect, be affected by, or perceive itself to be affected by a decision, activity or outcome of the project. |
| **Sponsor** | The person or group that provides resources and support for the project and is accountable for enabling success (and usually for benefits). |
| **Baseline** | The approved version of a work product (scope, schedule, cost) that can be changed only through formal change control and is used for comparison. |
| **Enterprise environmental factors (EEFs)** | Conditions *not under the immediate control of the team* that influence, constrain or direct the project (culture, regulations, market, infrastructure). |
| **Organisational process assets (OPAs)** | Plans, processes, policies, procedures and knowledge bases *specific to and used by* the performing organisation (templates, lessons learned, historical data). |
| **Tailoring** | Deliberate adaptation of approach, governance and processes to suit the context. |
| **Model / Method / Artifact** | A model is a thinking strategy to explain a process or framework; a method is a means of achieving an outcome (a technique); an artifact is a template, document, output or deliverable. |

### Project vs operations vs program vs portfolio

| Aspect | Project | Program | Portfolio | Operations |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Objective | Deliver specific outputs and outcomes | Deliver benefits through coordination | Achieve strategic objectives; optimise investment | Sustain the business |
| Duration | Temporary | Temporary (often long) | Ongoing | Ongoing |
| Change | Controlled through the plan | Expected; managed to optimise benefits | Monitored in the broader environment | Minimised; continuous improvement |
| Success measured by | Product and project quality, timeliness, budget, satisfaction, value | Benefits realised | Aggregate investment performance and benefits | Efficiency, KPIs |

### Why projects are started

Projects are vehicles for change. Common drivers:

- **Regulatory, legal or social requirements** (new safety law, accessibility rules);
- **Stakeholder requests or needs** (a customer asks for a new feature, a community requests a facility);
- **Business or technological strategies** (digital transformation, entering a new market, adopting AI);
- **Creating, improving or fixing products, processes or services** (fixing defects, improving performance).

> **Exam tip.** Questions often hide a portfolio or program concept inside a project scenario. If two projects compete for the same scarce resources and the decision affects strategy, the right level for the decision is usually **portfolio** governance, not the project manager.

## 2.5 Reading the rest of this guide

Chapters 3–5 cover the Standard (value delivery, environment, principles) and life cycles. Chapter 6 introduces focus areas and all 40 processes. Chapters 7–14 go domain by domain. Chapters 15–19 cover agile/hybrid practice, people skills, the business environment, tailoring and the toolbox of models, methods and artifacts. Chapter 20 collects every formula, and Chapter 21 prepares you for the exam itself.


## Chapter 2 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 2](#ans-2) in Appendix B.*

**2.1** Which combination correctly describes the structure of the PMBOK Guide – Eighth Edition?

- **A.** 5 process groups, 10 knowledge areas and 49 processes
- **B.** 6 principles, 8 performance domains, 5 process groups and 49 processes
- **C.** 6 principles, 7 performance domains, 5 focus areas and 40 processes
- **D.** 12 principles, 8 performance domains and models, methods and artifacts

**2.2** Which of the following is NOT one of the seven performance domains of the Eighth Edition?

- **A.** Resources
- **B.** Finance
- **C.** Quality
- **D.** Governance

**2.3** A new IT system is delivered (output). Customer orders are now processed in four hours instead of two days (outcome). What is the best example of the resulting benefit?

- **A.** The trained help-desk team
- **B.** The project closure report
- **C.** Higher customer retention and lower processing costs
- **D.** The installed and tested IT system, accepted by the customer

**2.4** What distinguishes a program from a portfolio?

- **A.** A program is ongoing, while a portfolio is temporary
- **B.** A program coordinates related components for added benefits; a portfolio groups components to meet strategy
- **C.** A portfolio contains only projects, while a program contains only operations and maintenance work of the organisation
- **D.** There is no difference; the terms are interchangeable

**2.5** Which of the following is an organisational process asset rather than an enterprise environmental factor?

- **A.** The lessons learned repository from previous projects
- **B.** New government safety regulations
- **C.** Current market exchange rates
- **D.** The organisation's culture and its appetite for risk in new projects

**2.6** In the Eighth Edition, where does Communications Management (a Sixth Edition knowledge area) primarily reside?

- **A.** Resources performance domain
- **B.** Governance performance domain
- **C.** It was removed from the Eighth Edition
- **D.** Stakeholders performance domain

**2.7** A colleague says, "A project is temporary, so it must be short." What is the best response?

- **A.** Temporary means a definite beginning and end, not a short duration
- **B.** Temporary means that the team is always disbanded after each phase of the project
- **C.** Correct; long-duration work is always operations
- **D.** Correct; projects longer than one year are programs

**2.8** Why did PMI reintroduce processes in the Eighth Edition?

- **A.** Because the PMP exam is now based only on ITTOs
- **B.** To remove agile and hybrid approaches from the Guide and return to a purely predictive model
- **C.** To make the Guide mandatory for all projects
- **D.** Principles alone were too abstract, so processes returned as non-prescriptive guidance

**2.9** Which two appendices of the Eighth Edition are newly added or significantly expanded and frequently referenced for exam preparation? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Business Analysis for Practitioners
- **B.** Artificial Intelligence
- **C.** Procurement
- **D.** Earned Value Management standard
- **E.** Agile Manifesto

**2.10** A company replaces its payroll software, then continues to run payroll every month with the new system. Which statement is correct?

- **A.** Both activities are operations because payroll is recurring
- **B.** Replacing the software is a project; running monthly payroll is operations
- **C.** Replacing the software is a portfolio, and running the monthly payroll is a program
- **D.** Both activities are projects because they involve software

**2.11** Match each layer of the Eighth Edition with the question it answers.

*(Matching: pair every numbered item with one lettered option.)*

| Item | Option |
|---|---|
| **1.** Principles | **A.** What — which areas of activity must be managed effectively? |
| **2.** Performance domains | **B.** How — which work is performed, in what order, with which tools? |
| **3.** Processes and focus areas | **C.** Why — which beliefs and behaviours should guide decisions? |
| **4.** Tailoring | **D.** Fit — how should the approach be adapted to this context? |

**2.12** A government ministry passes a new law on data protection that affects a software project. For the project, this law is best classified as:

- **A.** An organisational process asset of the ministry
- **B.** An enterprise environmental factor
- **C.** A work performance report
- **D.** A project deliverable

# Chapter 3 — The Standard: A System for Value Delivery, Governance and the Project Environment

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- explain how portfolios, programs, projects, products and operations work together to create value;
- distinguish organisational governance from project governance and describe the main governance roles;
- classify internal and external environmental factors and organisational process assets;
- compare organisational structures and their effect on the project manager's authority;
- describe the business documents that justify and authorise a project (needs assessment, business case, benefits management plan, charter);
- describe the project manager's role and the PMI Talent Triangle.

## 3.1 Value is the reason projects exist

A project is not successful just because it delivered on time and on budget. It is successful when it contributes to **value** — the worth, importance or usefulness that stakeholders obtain. Value can be:

- **tangible**: revenue, cost savings, market share, assets, equity;
- **intangible**: reputation, brand recognition, public benefit, strategic alignment, employee goodwill, sustainability.

The chain that connects work to value is:

**Activities → Outputs (deliverables) → Outcomes (changes in behaviour or capability) → Benefits (gains for the organisation) → Value**

> **Exam tip.** When a question asks how to measure success, prefer answers that refer to **outcomes, benefits and value** (customer adoption, business KPIs, benefit realisation) over answers that refer only to outputs or to the triple constraint.

## 3.2 The system for value delivery

Organisations do not deliver value through isolated projects. They use a **system for value delivery**: a collection of strategic business activities aimed at building, sustaining and advancing the organisation. Its components are:

| Component | Role in value delivery |
|---|---|
| **Portfolios** | Select and prioritise the right work to execute strategy; balance investments and risks |
| **Programs** | Coordinate related projects and activities to realise benefits that would not be obtained individually |
| **Projects** | Create the outputs that enable outcomes and benefits |
| **Products** | Deliver value continuously over their life cycle; may be created or changed by many projects |
| **Operations** | Run the business and *sustain* the value once deliverables are transitioned |

**Information flows in both directions.** Strategy, priorities and constraints flow *down* from leadership through portfolios and programs to projects. Performance data, lessons, risks and benefit realisation information flow *up*, so leadership can adjust strategy and investments. A project manager who reports only "green" status is breaking this feedback loop.

### Value is delivered continuously

In adaptive and product-oriented organisations, value is delivered in increments: each release is used, measured and fed back into the backlog. Value delivery does not wait for project closure. Predictive projects may deliver most value at the end, but benefits are still tracked after the transition to operations.

### Products and product management

The Eighth Edition gives explicit attention to **product management considerations**:

- A **product life cycle** (introduction, growth, maturity, decline, retirement) is longer than any single project.
- Organisations increasingly fund **long-lived product teams** rather than temporary project teams ("project-to-product" shift).
- Projects may create a new product, add capabilities, or retire one.
- A product focus emphasises customer outcomes, continuous discovery, roadmaps and backlog management — vocabulary you will meet again in Chapter 15.

## 3.3 Governance: organisational and project

**Governance** is the framework of authority, accountability, policies, decision rights and oversight that directs and controls work. Two levels matter:

| Level | Examples | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Organisational governance** | Board, executive committees, portfolio governance, policies, compliance rules | Sets strategy, risk appetite and rules for the whole organisation |
| **Project governance** | Sponsor, steering committee, phase gates, change control board, escalation paths, PMO oversight | Guides and oversees the specific project within the organisation's framework |

A good governance framework answers: *Who decides what? Within what limits (thresholds)? How are issues escalated? How is performance reviewed? How are changes approved? How are compliance and ethics ensured?*

### Key governance roles

| Role | Typical responsibilities |
|---|---|
| **Sponsor** | Champions the project, provides funding and resources, approves the charter, makes decisions above the PM's authority, removes organisational obstacles, is usually accountable for benefits |
| **Steering committee / project board** | Provides strategic direction, approves major changes and phase gates, resolves escalated issues |
| **Portfolio manager / portfolio board** | Prioritises investments, allocates scarce resources across projects, decides on start/stop |
| **Program manager** | Coordinates related projects, manages interdependencies and benefits |
| **PMO** | Standardises governance, provides methods, templates, training, reporting and sometimes direct management of projects |
| **Change control board (CCB)** | Reviews, approves, defers or rejects change requests according to authority thresholds |
| **Product owner** (adaptive) | Owns the product backlog, maximises value, decides priorities and accepts work |
| **Project manager** | Leads the team to achieve objectives, integrates the work, operates within delegated authority |
| **Functional / resource managers** | Provide people and expertise, often hold administrative authority over team members |

## 3.4 Functions needed in every project

Regardless of job titles, every project needs certain **functions** to be fulfilled. One person may perform several functions and one function may be shared:

- **Provide oversight and coordination** — plan, monitor, integrate (often the PM, PMO or scrum master/team facilitator).
- **Present objectives and feedback** — articulate needs, priorities and acceptance (customer, product owner, users).
- **Facilitate and support** — encourage participation, consensus, removing impediments (PM, scrum master, coach).
- **Perform work and contribute insights** — create the deliverables (team members, contractors).
- **Apply expertise** — subject-matter experts, specialists.
- **Provide business direction and insight** — clarify value and priorities (sponsor, product owner, business analyst).
- **Provide resources and direction** — sponsor, functional managers, governance bodies.
- **Maintain governance** — approve, oversee and ensure compliance (steering committee, PMO, CCB).

## 3.5 The project environment

Projects exist inside an environment that influences them. The Guide distinguishes **internal** and **external** factors.

| Internal environment | External environment |
|---|---|
| Organisational culture, structure and governance | Marketplace conditions (competitors, market share, brand) |
| Geographic distribution of facilities and people | Social and cultural influences, political climate, ethics |
| Infrastructure, IT systems and software | Regulatory and legal environment (laws, standards, data protection, safety) |
| Resource availability and employee capability | Commercial databases and benchmarking data |
| Process assets (templates, procedures, policies) | Academic research and industry standards |
| Data assets and knowledge assets (lessons learned, historical data) | Financial considerations (exchange rates, interest rates, inflation, tariffs) |
| Security and safety policies | Physical environment (weather, working conditions) |

Two traditional labels are still widely used in exam questions:

- **Enterprise environmental factors (EEFs)**: conditions *not under the team's immediate control* (internal or external) that influence, constrain or direct the project.
- **Organisational process assets (OPAs)**: plans, processes, policies, procedures and knowledge bases *of the performing organisation*. They are of two kinds: **processes, policies and procedures** (usually not updated by the project) and **organisational knowledge repositories** (updated by the project — e.g., lessons learned, historical information).

> **Trap.** An organisation's *culture* is an EEF, not an OPA, even though it is internal. Templates and lessons learned are OPAs.

## 3.6 Organisational structures and the project manager's authority

| Structure | PM authority | Resource availability | Who controls the budget | PM role |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Organic / simple** | Little or none | Little or none | Owner/operator | Part-time, coordinator |
| **Functional (centralised)** | Little or none | Little or none | Functional manager | Part-time; often called *project coordinator* or *expediter* |
| **Multi-divisional** | Little or none | Little or none | Functional manager | Part-time |
| **Weak matrix** | Low | Low | Functional manager | Part-time; coordinator/expediter |
| **Balanced matrix** | Low to moderate | Low to moderate | Mixed | Full-time |
| **Strong matrix** | Moderate to high | Moderate to high | Project manager | Full-time, with admin staff |
| **Project-oriented (projectized)** | High to almost total | High to almost total | Project manager | Full-time |
| **Virtual / networked** | Low to moderate | Low to moderate | Mixed | Full- or part-time |
| **Hybrid** | Mixed | Mixed | Mixed | Mixed |
| **PMO-led** | High | High | Project manager | Full-time |

- A **project expediter** acts as a staff assistant and communications coordinator with no decision authority.
- A **project coordinator** has some limited authority and usually reports to a higher-level manager.
- In a **matrix**, team members have two bosses: the functional manager (administrative, career, often technical) and the project manager (project work). Conflicts over priorities and resources are common — the PM must **negotiate** and use influence rather than formal power.

## 3.7 Business documents: justifying and authorising the project

| Document | Owner | Content | Purpose |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Needs assessment** | Business / business analyst | Problem or opportunity, current state, desired future state, gaps | Understand the business need before investing |
| **Business case** | Sponsor (prepared with business analysts) | Business need, situation analysis, options (do nothing, do minimum, do more), recommended option, cost-benefit analysis, financial measures (NPV, IRR, payback), risks, success criteria | Justify the investment; reviewed at phase gates to confirm the project is still worthwhile |
| **Benefits management plan** | Sponsor / benefit owner | Target benefits, strategic alignment, timeframe for realisation, **benefit owner**, metrics, assumptions, risks | Describe how and when benefits will be delivered and measured |
| **Project charter** | Issued by the **sponsor** (or initiator); developed with the PM | Purpose, measurable objectives and success criteria, high-level requirements, boundaries and key deliverables, overall risk, summary milestones, pre-approved financial resources, key stakeholders, approval and exit criteria, **assigned PM and authority level**, sponsor's name and authority | **Formally authorises the project** and gives the PM authority to apply organisational resources |

> **Exam tip.** The **charter** authorises the project; the **business case** justifies it; the **benefits management plan** describes how value will be realised and measured. If a question asks "the project no longer seems worthwhile — what should be reviewed?", the answer is usually the **business case** (and the benefits plan), often at a phase gate.

### Agreements and contracts

When the project is performed for an external customer, an **agreement** (contract, memorandum of understanding, letter of intent, service-level agreement) defines the initial intentions and is an input to initiating the project.

## 3.8 The project manager's role and competencies

The project manager is the person assigned by the performing organisation to lead the team responsible for achieving the project objectives. A common metaphor is the **conductor of an orchestra**: the PM does not play every instrument but integrates and directs. The PM's spheres of influence include the **project** (team, sponsor, stakeholders), the **organisation** (other PMs, PMO, executives), the **industry** (trends, standards, regulations) and the **profession** (knowledge transfer, continuing education, mentoring).

### The PMI Talent Triangle®

| Side | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Ways of Working** | Mastery of predictive, agile, hybrid, design thinking and other approaches; selecting and tailoring methods |
| **Power Skills** | Collaborative leadership, communication, innovative mindset, for-purpose orientation, empathy, negotiation, problem-solving |
| **Business Acumen** | Understanding of the organisation's strategy, finance, markets, competition, regulations; linking the project to value |

> **What changed.** Earlier versions of the Talent Triangle used *Technical Project Management*, *Leadership* and *Strategic and Business Management*. You may still see these labels in older materials.

### Integration — the PM's core job

Integration happens at several levels: **process** (making the pieces of the plan fit together), **cognitive** (applying knowledge and judgment), **context** (adapting to the environment and technology) and **complexity** (managing the system behaviour of the project). The PM, not the team, is ultimately accountable for integrating the project as a whole.

# Chapter 4 — The Six Principles of Project Management

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- state the six principles of the Eighth Edition and explain each in your own words;
- recognise behaviours that apply (or violate) each principle in a scenario;
- relate the six principles to the twelve principles of the Seventh Edition and to the PMI Code of Ethics.

## 4.1 What a principle is (and is not)

A **principle** is a fundamental norm, truth or value that guides behaviour and decisions. Principles are **not prescriptive**: they do not tell you which template to use. They apply to every project, every development approach, every industry. When a scenario seems to have no "process" answer, ask yourself which principle the best action would honour.

The principles are consistent with the **PMI Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct** (responsibility, respect, fairness and honesty — see Chapter 16). They also overlap: one good action usually honours several principles at once.

## 4.2 Principle 1 — Adopt a holistic view

**Idea in one sentence:** see the project as a system of interacting parts that lives inside larger systems (the organisation, the market, society), and make decisions with the whole in mind.

**Key ideas**
- **Systems thinking.** A change in one area ripples elsewhere: compressing the schedule may raise cost, risk and team stress; a supplier change may affect quality and compliance.
- **Integration across domains.** Scope, schedule, finance, resources, risk and stakeholders are interdependent — the seven domains are *interactive, interrelated and interdependent*.
- **Life-cycle perspective.** Consider the product's whole life — operation, maintenance, support, disposal — not only the project's delivery.
- **Complexity awareness.** Many sources of complexity (human behaviour, system behaviour, ambiguity, technological innovation) cannot be removed by planning harder; they require sensing, adapting and feedback.
- **Context.** The organisation's strategy, culture, governance and environment shape what "good" looks like.

**In practice:** impact analysis before approving changes; cross-functional reviews; considering downstream users and operations; tracking interdependencies with other projects and programs.

**Violations to recognise:** optimising one constraint at the expense of the whole ("we met the date, the help desk can deal with the defects"); ignoring effects on other projects that share resources.

## 4.3 Principle 2 — Focus on value

**Idea in one sentence:** continually evaluate and adjust the project so that it delivers the intended outcomes, benefits and value — not just outputs.

**Key ideas**
- **Value is the ultimate success indicator.** It may be financial or non-financial, and may be realised during or after the project.
- **Clarify value early** with stakeholders: what problem are we solving, for whom, and how will we know?
- **Prioritise by value.** In adaptive work the product owner orders the backlog by value; in predictive work the business case and benefits plan anchor decisions.
- **Deliver early and often** where possible, so value is realised sooner and assumptions are validated by real feedback.
- **Re-evaluate continuously.** If the business case is no longer valid, the right decision may be to change direction — or to stop the project.

**In practice:** minimum viable products (MVPs); value-based prioritisation (MoSCoW, cost of delay, weighted shortest job first); benefit KPIs; phase-gate reviews of the business case.

**Violations:** continuing a project that no longer makes sense because "we already spent so much" (sunk-cost thinking); measuring success purely by "on time, on budget".

## 4.4 Principle 3 — Embed quality into processes and deliverables

**Idea in one sentence:** quality is designed and built in from the beginning — in the way work is done and in what is produced — rather than inspected in at the end.

**Key ideas**
- **Two dimensions of quality:** the *process* (how we work) and the *product* (what we deliver). Both must meet the expectations of stakeholders and the acceptance criteria.
- **Prevention over inspection.** Preventing a defect is cheaper than finding it; finding it early is cheaper than finding it late.
- **Quality is defined by requirements and fitness for use**, including non-functional requirements (performance, security, usability, sustainability).
- **Everyone owns quality.** Teams use definitions of done, peer reviews, test automation and standards; leaders create conditions for doing it right.
- **Continuous improvement.** Retrospectives, lessons learned, root-cause analysis and PDCA cycles improve both process and product.

**Why it matters for the Eighth Edition structure:** quality is no longer a separate domain. **Quality planning** is part of *Define Scope*, **quality assurance** is the Governance process *Manage Quality Assurance*, and **quality control** is part of *Monitor and Control Scope*. The principle explains why: quality is everyone's responsibility in every domain.

**Violations:** skipping testing to meet a date; adding extra features nobody asked for (gold plating — more is not "higher quality"); treating quality as the QA department's problem.

## 4.5 Principle 4 — Be an accountable leader

**Idea in one sentence:** take ownership of decisions, actions and results; lead with integrity and transparency, whatever your formal role.

**Key ideas**
- **Leadership is not a title.** Anyone on the team can demonstrate leadership behaviours: setting direction, motivating, facilitating, taking ownership.
- **Accountability and ownership.** Leaders own outcomes, admit mistakes, report status honestly and follow through on commitments.
- **Stewardship.** Act as a responsible steward of the organisation's resources and of the trust of stakeholders: integrity, care, trustworthiness and compliance with laws and policies.
- **Ethics.** Follow the PMI Code of Ethics; act transparently; avoid conflicts of interest; protect confidential information.
- **Adapt the style.** Use servant leadership to empower teams, directive leadership in a crisis, coaching for less experienced members (situational leadership).
- **Decision-making.** Make timely, informed decisions at the right level, and know when a decision belongs to someone else (product owner, sponsor, CCB).

**In practice:** honest status reports (including bad news, early); escalating through the agreed path when a decision exceeds your authority; protecting the team from inappropriate pressure; modelling the behaviours you expect.

**Violations:** hiding problems ("watermelon" status — green outside, red inside); blaming team members publicly; accepting gifts that create conflicts of interest; making decisions that belong to the customer or product owner.

## 4.6 Principle 5 — Integrate sustainability within all project areas

**Idea in one sentence:** consider the long-term economic, environmental and social impacts of the project and its product in every decision.

**Key ideas**
- **The triple bottom line:** **people** (social), **planet** (environmental) and **prosperity/profit** (economic). Many organisations frame this as **ESG** — environmental, social and governance.
- **Across all domains:** sustainable sourcing (Resources/Governance), life-cycle costs and green finance (Finance), environmental and social risks (Risk), community and stakeholder impacts (Stakeholders), sustainable requirements and acceptance criteria (Scope), energy-efficient scheduling and logistics (Schedule).
- **Product life cycle:** design for reuse, recycling and minimal waste (circular economy); consider operation and disposal.
- **Social sustainability includes the team:** sustainable pace, well-being, fair labour practices, diversity and inclusion.
- **Compliance and beyond.** Meet legal requirements (environmental permits, reporting rules) and the organisation's sustainability commitments (e.g., net-zero targets, UN Sustainable Development Goals).

**In practice:** including sustainability criteria in supplier selection; measuring the carbon footprint of a solution; engaging affected communities; choosing options with lower life-cycle impact even when initial cost is slightly higher, when the business case supports it.

**Violations:** ignoring environmental regulations to save time; selecting a supplier with known unethical labour practices because it is cheapest; consistently demanding overtime ("death marches").

## 4.7 Principle 6 — Build an empowered culture

**Idea in one sentence:** create an environment of trust, psychological safety and shared ownership in which people and stakeholders can contribute their best, make decisions and adapt.

**Key ideas**
- **Empowerment.** Push decisions to the people closest to the work; give teams autonomy within clear boundaries; self-organising teams decide *how* to do their work.
- **Psychological safety.** People can raise problems, admit mistakes and challenge ideas without fear.
- **Collaboration and shared ownership.** Team agreements (team charter, ground rules), collective accountability for results.
- **Engaged stakeholders.** Stakeholders participate actively rather than receiving reports passively.
- **Diversity, equity and inclusion.** Diverse perspectives improve decisions; inclusive practices ensure all voices are heard.
- **Learning and adaptability.** A culture that experiments, learns from failure, and adapts to change is more resilient.

**In practice:** servant leadership; facilitating rather than dictating; recognising contributions; retrospectives with real follow-through; coaching; delegating decisions with clear guardrails.

**Violations:** micromanagement; punishing people who report bad news; assigning tasks to self-organising teams; excluding remote team members from decisions.

## 4.8 How the twelve principles of the Seventh Edition live on

This mapping is an interpretive study aid (the Eighth Edition does not require you to memorise it), but it helps if your other materials still use the Seventh-Edition list:

| Seventh Edition principle | Mainly reflected in (Eighth Edition) |
|---|---|
| Be a diligent, respectful and caring steward | Be an accountable leader; Integrate sustainability |
| Create a collaborative project team environment | Build an empowered culture |
| Effectively engage with stakeholders | Build an empowered culture; Stakeholders domain |
| Focus on value | Focus on value |
| Recognise, evaluate and respond to system interactions | Adopt a holistic view |
| Demonstrate leadership behaviours | Be an accountable leader |
| Tailor based on context | Tailoring (Guide); Adopt a holistic view |
| Build quality into processes and deliverables | Embed quality into processes and deliverables |
| Navigate complexity | Adopt a holistic view |
| Optimise risk responses | Risk performance domain |
| Embrace adaptability and resiliency | Build an empowered culture; Adopt a holistic view |
| Enable change to achieve the envisioned future state | Focus on value; Build an empowered culture (and organisational change, Chapter 17) |

## 4.9 Using principles to answer exam questions

When two answers both look reasonable, check which one better reflects the principles:

| If the scenario is about… | Prefer the answer that… |
|---|---|
| A change with ripple effects | analyses the impact across scope, schedule, cost, risk, quality and other projects (holistic view) |
| Continuing or stopping work | revisits value, the business case and benefits (focus on value) |
| Defects found late | addresses process causes and prevention, not just rework (embed quality) |
| A mistake or bad news | is transparent, owns the problem and acts ethically (accountable leader) |
| Environmental or social impacts | considers long-term impacts and stakeholders affected (sustainability) |
| Team decisions or morale | empowers, facilitates and builds safety (empowered culture) |


## Chapter 4 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 4](#ans-4) in Appendix B.*

**4.1** A project manager proposes to compress the schedule by adding a night shift. Before approving, the PM asks the team to analyse effects on quality, safety, costs, team well-being and the neighbouring community. Which principle is most directly demonstrated?

- **A.** Build an empowered culture
- **B.** Adopt a holistic view
- **C.** Embed quality into processes and deliverables
- **D.** Focus on value

**4.2** Market research shows that the product being built will no longer meet customers' needs. The project is 60% complete and on budget. What does the principle "Focus on value" suggest?

- **A.** Reduce testing to finish faster
- **B.** Ask the team to add more features to increase value
- **C.** Finish the project, because 60% of the budget has already been spent and must not be wasted
- **D.** Re-evaluate the business case with the sponsor and consider changing direction

**4.3** Which practice best reflects the principle "Embed quality into processes and deliverables"?

- **A.** Inspecting all of the deliverables thoroughly at the end of the project, before handover
- **B.** Adding features beyond the requirements to delight the customer
- **C.** Agreeing on a definition of done and automated tests before development starts
- **D.** Letting the quality department own all quality decisions

**4.4** A project manager discovers that a status report sent last week understated a cost overrun. What should the project manager do, consistent with being an accountable leader?

- **A.** Ask the finance analyst to take responsibility for the error
- **B.** Inform the sponsor promptly, correct the report and explain the planned actions
- **C.** Wait until the overrun has been recovered, so that the issue never needs to be reported to anyone
- **D.** Remove cost information from future reports

**4.5** During supplier selection, the lowest bidder uses a manufacturing process with significant environmental damage, while the second-lowest bidder meets the organisation's environmental commitments at a 3% higher price. Which principle should most influence the recommendation?

- **A.** Build an empowered culture
- **B.** Adopt a holistic view, so the cheapest option is preferred
- **C.** Integrate sustainability within all project areas
- **D.** Focus on value, so price is the only criterion

**4.6** An agile team asks the project manager to decide how to split a feature into technical tasks. What best reflects the principle "Build an empowered culture"?

- **A.** Encourage the team to decide the breakdown and offer support
- **B.** Ask the sponsor to decide the breakdown
- **C.** Postpone the feature until the project manager has time to analyse it in detail
- **D.** Prepare the task list and assign tasks to each developer

**4.7** Which two situations violate the principle of being an accountable leader? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Escalating a decision that exceeds the PM's authority through the agreed path
- **B.** Reporting a project as green although critical milestones are slipping
- **C.** Accepting a valuable gift from a bidder during an open tender
- **D.** Asking the team for input before an important decision
- **E.** Admitting a planning error in the team retrospective

**4.8** In the Eighth Edition, why is quality not a separate performance domain?

- **A.** Because quality is fully covered by the risk domain
- **B.** Because quality is only relevant to predictive projects
- **C.** Because quality is no longer considered important in modern, value-focused projects of any type
- **D.** Because embedding quality makes it part of every domain, especially Governance and Scope

**4.9** A team has worked overtime for six consecutive sprints and morale is falling. Which principle is most at risk?

- **A.** Integrate sustainability, including the team's social sustainability and a sustainable pace
- **B.** Focus on value
- **C.** Adopt a holistic view only
- **D.** Embed quality into processes, because overtime always improves the quality of the work produced by teams

**4.10** Which Seventh Edition principle is now primarily addressed through the Risk performance domain rather than as a separate principle?

- **A.** Focus on value
- **B.** Optimise risk responses
- **C.** Demonstrate leadership behaviours
- **D.** Create a collaborative team environment

**4.11** A product owner wants to measure the success of a new self-service portal. Which metric best reflects the principle of focusing on value?

- **A.** Number of status meetings held
- **B.** Number of lines of code written
- **C.** Lower call-centre volume and higher customer satisfaction after launch
- **D.** Percentage of the approved budget that has been spent by the end of the project

**4.12** Which statement about the six principles is correct?

- **A.** They replace the PMI Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct
- **B.** They are mandatory procedures that must be documented in the project management plan
- **C.** They apply only to adaptive projects

# Chapter 5 — Life Cycles and Development Approaches

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- distinguish project life cycle, product life cycle, phases and phase gates;
- compare predictive, iterative, incremental, adaptive and hybrid approaches;
- select a development approach using product, project and organisational factors;
- recognise common hybrid patterns and delivery cadences in exam scenarios.

## 5.1 Key definitions

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Project life cycle** | The series of phases a project passes through from start to completion (e.g., feasibility → design → build → test → deploy → close). |
| **Phase** | A collection of logically related activities that culminates in the completion of one or more deliverables. |
| **Phase gate** (also stage gate, kill point, phase review, decision gate) | A review at the end of a phase where performance and documents (e.g., the business case) are assessed and a decision is made: **continue, continue with modifications, repeat the phase, or end the project/program**. |
| **Product life cycle** | The series of phases that represents the evolution of a product from concept through delivery, growth, maturity and retirement. A product life cycle may contain many projects. |
| **Development approach** | The method used to create and evolve the product, service or result (predictive, iterative, incremental, adaptive, hybrid). |
| **Delivery cadence** | The timing and frequency of deliveries: single delivery, multiple deliveries, periodic deliveries, or continuous delivery. |

## 5.2 The spectrum of development approaches

Think of development approaches as a **continuum** from predictive to adaptive, not as two boxes.

| | **Predictive** (plan-driven, "waterfall") | **Iterative** | **Incremental** | **Adaptive / agile** |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Requirements | Defined up front and stable | Refined through repeated cycles (prototypes, feedback) | Defined for each increment | Elaborated frequently during delivery |
| Activities | Performed once for the whole project, in sequence | Repeated until correct | Performed once per increment | Repeated in short cycles (iterations/sprints) or continuous flow |
| Delivery | Single final delivery | Usually single final delivery (after iterations improve the solution) | Frequent smaller deliverables | Frequent, small, customer-valued increments |
| Change | Constrained as much as possible; managed by change control | Incorporated at periodic intervals | Incorporated at increment boundaries | Welcomed; incorporated in real time via the backlog |
| Stakeholder involvement | At specific milestones | Regular feedback on iterations | Regular, at each increment | Continuous |
| Risk and cost control | Detailed planning of mostly known considerations | Progressive elaboration with new information | — | Controlled as requirements and constraints emerge |
| Best when | Requirements are clear, technology is known, regulations demand documentation, changes are expensive (construction, manufacturing) | Complexity is high, frequent changes are expected, feedback improves the solution | Customers benefit from early partial value | High uncertainty, rapid change, innovative products, fast feedback is possible |

- **Iterative** = improve the product through repeated cycles (e.g., prototype → feedback → refine). Optimises for *learning*.
- **Incremental** = deliver the product in usable pieces that add up (e.g., release module 1, then module 2). Optimises for *speed of delivery*.
- **Agile** approaches are both iterative *and* incremental.

> **Trap.** "Waterfall" does not mean "no change". Predictive projects handle change through **integrated change control**. Adaptive projects do not mean "no planning" — planning happens continuously and at several levels (vision, roadmap, release, iteration, daily).

### Hybrid approaches

A **hybrid** approach combines predictive and adaptive elements. Common patterns:

1. **Agile development followed by predictive rollout** — software built in sprints, then a planned, phased deployment across 40 branches.
2. **Predictive project with adaptive components** — a construction project uses agile sprints for the building-management software.
3. **Mostly agile with a predictive component** — the product is built iteratively, but a regulatory submission follows a fixed, documented sequence.
4. **Phase-gated governance with agile delivery** — funding and governance use stage gates; work inside each stage is delivered in sprints.
5. **Iterative design, predictive build** — prototypes and user research early, then a plan-driven build.

> **Exam tip.** In hybrid scenarios, look for the answer that applies the *right* mechanism to the *right* part: change control for the baselined predictive component, backlog refinement for the adaptive component, and integrated reporting across both.

## 5.3 Delivery cadence

| Cadence | Example |
|---|---|
| **Single delivery** | A bridge opened at the end of the project |
| **Multiple deliveries** | A regulatory programme delivering separate submissions at different times |
| **Periodic deliveries** | A fixed release every month or every quarter |
| **Continuous delivery** | Features released to users as soon as they are ready (DevOps pipelines) |

## 5.4 Choosing a development approach

The Guide groups the considerations into three families:

**1. Product, service or result**
- *Degree of innovation* — novel products favour adaptive; well-understood products favour predictive.
- *Requirements certainty* — clear and stable → predictive; uncertain → adaptive.
- *Scope stability* — stable scope → predictive.
- *Ease of change* — if changes are cheap (software), adaptive works well; if changes are costly (concrete), predictive.
- *Delivery options* — if the product can be delivered in usable pieces, incremental/adaptive is possible.
- *Risk* — high-risk products benefit from incremental learning *or* from rigorous up-front analysis, depending on the nature of the risk.
- *Safety requirements* and *regulations* — strict documentation and verification often push toward predictive (or hybrid).

**2. Project**
- *Stakeholders* — availability of customer/product owner for frequent feedback.
- *Schedule constraints* — the need to deliver something early favours incremental.
- *Funding availability* — uncertain funding favours incremental releases that deliver value early (and can stop).

**3. Organisation**
- *Structure* — hierarchical, bureaucratic organisations often favour predictive; flatter ones support adaptive.
- *Culture* — trust, empowerment and tolerance of experimentation support adaptive.
- *Capability* — experience with agile, availability of coaches.
- *Team size and location* — adaptive approaches work best with small (often 3–9 people), dedicated, co-located or well-connected teams.

### Helpful models

**Stacey matrix** (agreement vs certainty):

| | Requirements agreed and clear | Requirements far from agreement |
|---|---|---|
| **Technology certain** | *Simple* → predictive | *Complicated* (political) |
| **Technology uncertain** | *Complicated* (technical) | *Complex* → adaptive; far corner = *chaotic* |

**Cynefin framework**: *clear* (best practice; sense–categorise–respond), *complicated* (good practice via experts; sense–analyse–respond), *complex* (emergent practice; probe–sense–respond — use experiments and agile), *chaotic* (novel practice; act–sense–respond — stabilise first), and *confusion* (not yet known which domain applies).

**Agile suitability filter** (from the *Agile Practice Guide*): assess **culture** (buy-in, trust in the team, decision-making powers), **team** (size, experience, access to the customer/business) and **project** (likelihood of change, criticality of the product, possibility of incremental delivery). Results fall in the agile, hybrid or predictive zones.

## 5.5 Life cycles in practice — examples

| Situation | Suitable approach | Why |
|---|---|---|
| Building a hospital wing | Predictive (with hybrid elements for IT systems) | Scope is defined by design drawings; changes after construction starts are very expensive; regulations require inspections |
| Developing a new mobile app for an uncertain market | Adaptive | High uncertainty; frequent feedback; low cost of change |
| Implementing an ERP package in 30 countries | Hybrid | Configuration can be iterative; data migration, training and go-lives are planned and phased |
| Running a one-off international conference | Predictive with iterative elements | Fixed date; marketing campaigns can be iterated |
| Developing a new drug | Predictive, phase-gated | Clinical trial phases and regulatory approvals require strict sequence and documentation |

## 5.6 Planning in each approach

| Level | Predictive | Adaptive |
|---|---|---|
| Strategic | Business case, charter | Product vision, roadmap |
| Overall plan | Integrated project management plan with baselines | Release plan (features targeted per release) |
| Detailed plan | Detailed schedule, rolling-wave refinement of later work | Iteration (sprint) plan, created by the team |
| Daily | Status updates against schedule | Daily stand-up (daily scrum) |

**Rolling wave planning** — plan the near term in detail and the far term at a higher level, refining as information emerges — is a form of progressive elaboration used in predictive and hybrid projects.
