# Chapter 6 — Focus Areas and the 40 Processes

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe the purpose of the five focus areas and how they repeat during a project;
- place each of the 40 processes in its performance domain and focus area;
- translate Sixth-Edition process names into Eighth-Edition names;
- explain inputs, tools and techniques, and outputs (ITTOs) and the most common ones;
- list the components of the project management plan, the main project documents and the baselines;
- distinguish work performance data, information and reports.

## 6.1 The five focus areas

The Eighth Edition calls the former *process groups* **focus areas**. A focus area is a logical grouping of processes by the *nature of the work* — not a phase of the project. All five can occur within each phase, release or iteration, and they overlap.

| Focus area | Purpose | Typical key outputs |
|---|---|---|
| **Initiating** | Define a new project or phase, obtain authorisation, align stakeholders on purpose | Project charter, assumption log, stakeholder register |
| **Planning** | Establish the scope, refine objectives and define how the work will be done | Project management plan, baselines, subsidiary plans, project documents |
| **Executing** | Perform the work defined in the plan, lead people, manage engagement and communications | Deliverables, work performance data, issue log, change requests |
| **Monitoring and Controlling** | Track, review and regulate progress and performance; identify needed changes; validate and control deliverables | Work performance information and reports, approved/rejected changes, accepted deliverables, forecasts |
| **Closing** | Formally complete the project, phase or contract | Final product transition, final report, archives, lessons learned |

> **Exam tip.** **Monitoring and Controlling runs in parallel with all other focus areas** — from the first day to the last. And in adaptive approaches all five focus areas occur inside every iteration: planning (sprint planning), executing (development), monitoring and controlling (daily scrum, sprint review), closing (iteration closure) — and initiating when a new release or phase starts.

How many processes per focus area? **Initiating 2 · Planning 19 · Executing 8 · Monitoring and Controlling 10 · Closing 1 = 40.**

## 6.2 The 40 processes — domain × focus area

| Domain | Initiating | Planning | Executing | Monitoring & Controlling | Closing |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Governance (9)** | Initiate Project or Phase | Integrate and Align Project Plans · Plan Sourcing Strategy | Manage Project Execution · Manage Quality Assurance · Manage Project Knowledge | Monitor and Control Project Performance · Assess and Implement Changes | Close Project or Phase |
| **Scope (6)** | | Plan Scope Management · Elicit and Analyze Requirements · Define Scope · Develop Scope Structure | | Validate Scope · Monitor and Control Scope | |
| **Schedule (3)** | | Plan Schedule Management · Develop Schedule | | Monitor and Control Schedule | |
| **Finance (4)** | | Plan Financial Management · Estimate Costs · Develop Budget | | Monitor and Control Finances | |
| **Stakeholders (7)** | Identify Stakeholders | Plan Stakeholder Engagement · Plan Communications Management | Manage Stakeholder Engagement · Manage Communications | Monitor Stakeholder Engagement · Monitor Communications | |
| **Resources (5)** | | Plan Resource Management · Estimate Resources | Acquire Resources · Lead the Team | Monitor and Control Resourcing | |
| **Risk (6)** | | Plan Risk Management · Identify Risks · Perform Risk Analysis · Plan Risk Responses | Implement Risk Responses | Monitor Risks | |

Patterns that help you memorise the table:

- Every domain except Governance and Stakeholders has a **"Plan … Management"** process that creates its management plan (Plan Scope Management, Plan Schedule Management, Plan Financial Management, Plan Resource Management, Plan Risk Management); Stakeholders has *Plan Stakeholder Engagement* and *Plan Communications Management*, and Governance has *Integrate and Align Project Plans* (the integrated plan) and *Plan Sourcing Strategy*.
- Every domain has at least one **monitoring and controlling** process.
- Governance is the only domain with processes in **all five** focus areas.
- Only **Initiate Project or Phase** and **Identify Stakeholders** are initiating processes; only **Close Project or Phase** is a closing process.

## 6.3 From the Sixth Edition's 49 processes to the Eighth Edition's 40

| Sixth Edition process(es) | Eighth Edition process |
|---|---|
| Develop Project Charter | **Initiate Project or Phase** |
| Develop Project Management Plan | **Integrate and Align Project Plans** |
| Plan Procurement Management | **Plan Sourcing Strategy** |
| Direct and Manage Project Work | **Manage Project Execution** |
| Manage Quality | **Manage Quality Assurance** |
| Manage Project Knowledge | Manage Project Knowledge |
| Monitor and Control Project Work | **Monitor and Control Project Performance** |
| Perform Integrated Change Control | **Assess and Implement Changes** |
| Close Project or Phase | Close Project or Phase |
| Plan Scope Management · Collect Requirements | Plan Scope Management · **Elicit and Analyze Requirements** |
| Define Scope + Plan Quality Management | **Define Scope** (now includes quality planning) |
| Create WBS | **Develop Scope Structure** |
| Validate Scope | Validate Scope |
| Control Scope + Control Quality | **Monitor and Control Scope** (now includes quality control) |
| Plan Schedule Management | Plan Schedule Management |
| Define Activities · Sequence Activities · Estimate Activity Durations · Develop Schedule | **Develop Schedule** (one process) |
| Control Schedule | **Monitor and Control Schedule** |
| Plan Cost Management · Estimate Costs · Determine Budget · Control Costs | **Plan Financial Management** · Estimate Costs · **Develop Budget** · **Monitor and Control Finances** |
| Identify Stakeholders · Plan Stakeholder Engagement · Manage Stakeholder Engagement · Monitor Stakeholder Engagement | Same names |
| Plan Communications Management · Manage Communications · Monitor Communications | Same names (now in the Stakeholders domain) |
| Plan Resource Management · Estimate Activity Resources | Plan Resource Management · **Estimate Resources** |
| Acquire Resources (+ Conduct Procurements) | **Acquire Resources** (obtaining internal and external resources; procurement detail in Appendix X4) |
| Develop Team · Manage Team | **Lead the Team** |
| Control Resources (+ Control Procurements) | **Monitor and Control Resourcing** |
| Plan Risk Management · Identify Risks | Same names |
| Perform Qualitative Risk Analysis · Perform Quantitative Risk Analysis | **Perform Risk Analysis** |
| Plan Risk Responses · Implement Risk Responses · Monitor Risks | Same names |

Arithmetic check: 49 − 3 (schedule merges) − 1 (risk analysis merge) − 1 (team merge) − 1 (quality planning) − 1 (quality control) − 2 (conduct and control procurements absorbed) = **40**.

> **What changed.** The Eighth Edition states that its processes are **non-prescriptive**: they describe work that is commonly needed, but the team decides — through tailoring — which processes to use, how formally, and how often. Don't expect the exam to ask you to recite inputs and outputs; expect it to test whether you know what a process is *for* and what it *produces*.

## 6.4 Anatomy of a process: ITTOs

Each process is described by:

- **Inputs** — what you need to perform it (documents, plans, data, environmental factors);
- **Tools and techniques** — how you transform the inputs (methods, analyses, meetings);
- **Outputs** — what the process produces (artifacts, deliverables, decisions, updates).

Outputs of one process are often inputs to another — this is how processes **interact**.

### The most common inputs

| Input | Notes |
|---|---|
| **Project management plan** (or a component of it) | The integrated plan and its subsidiary plans and baselines |
| **Project documents** | Logs, registers, estimates, schedules — documents that are *not* part of the plan |
| **Enterprise environmental factors** | Culture, market, regulations, infrastructure |
| **Organisational process assets** | Policies, templates, historical information, lessons learned |
| **Business documents** | Business case, benefits management plan (mainly in initiating) |
| **Agreements** | Contracts with customers or suppliers |
| **Work performance data/information** | Status data from execution and control |

### The most common tools and techniques (by family)

| Family | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Expert judgment** | Advice from individuals or groups with specialised knowledge |
| **Data gathering** | Brainstorming, interviews, focus groups, questionnaires and surveys, benchmarking, checklists |
| **Data analysis** | Alternatives analysis, root-cause analysis, variance analysis, trend analysis, earned value analysis, what-if scenario analysis, reserve analysis, SWOT, assumption and constraint analysis |
| **Data representation** | Charts and diagrams, matrices, mind maps, affinity diagrams, stakeholder grids, histograms |
| **Decision-making** | Voting (unanimity, majority, plurality), autocratic decision, multicriteria decision analysis |
| **Communication skills** | Feedback, presentations, active listening |
| **Interpersonal and team skills** | Facilitation, conflict management, negotiation, influencing, leadership, emotional intelligence, meeting management |
| **Estimating** | Analogous, parametric, bottom-up, three-point |
| **Meetings** | Kickoff, planning, status, review, retrospective, change control board meetings |
| **Project management information system (PMIS)** | Scheduling software, dashboards, configuration management tools, AI-assisted analytics |

## 6.5 The project management plan and project documents

The **project management plan** describes how the project will be executed, monitored and controlled, and closed. It integrates:

**Subsidiary management plans** — scope management plan, requirements management plan, schedule management plan, financial (cost) management plan, quality management plan (quality planning is part of defining scope in the Eighth Edition, but a quality plan is still a component), resource management plan, communications management plan, risk management plan, sourcing (procurement) strategy plan, stakeholder engagement plan.

**Baselines** — scope baseline, schedule baseline, cost baseline (together the **performance measurement baseline, PMB**).

**Additional components** — change management plan, configuration management plan, performance measurement baseline, project life cycle description, development approach, management reviews.

**Project documents** (not part of the plan, updated freely as the project progresses — no formal change control needed): activity attributes and list, assumption log, basis of estimates, change log, cost estimates, duration estimates, issue log, lessons learned register, milestone list, physical resource assignments, project calendars, project communications, project schedule, network diagram, project scope statement*, project team assignments, quality control measurements, quality metrics, quality report, requirements documentation, requirements traceability matrix, resource breakdown structure, resource calendars, resource requirements, risk register, risk report, schedule data, schedule forecasts, stakeholder register, team charter, test and evaluation documents.

\* The project scope statement is also part of the scope baseline once approved.

> **Trap.** Updating the **issue log**, **risk register** or **lessons learned register** does **not** require a change request — they are project documents. Changing a **baseline** or the **project management plan** does.

### The baselines

| Baseline | Components | Changed only through |
|---|---|---|
| **Scope baseline** | Approved project scope statement + WBS (scope structure) + WBS dictionary | Formal change control |
| **Schedule baseline** | Approved version of the schedule model with baseline start and finish dates | Formal change control |
| **Cost baseline** | Approved time-phased budget, *excluding management reserves*, including contingency reserves | Formal change control |
| **Performance measurement baseline (PMB)** | Integrated scope-schedule-cost baseline used for earned value management | Formal change control |

## 6.6 Data → information → reports

| Term | Created in | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Work performance data** | Executing (Manage Project Execution and other executing processes) | Raw observations: "Activity 4.2 is 60% complete; actual cost $12,000; 3 defects found" |
| **Work performance information** | Monitoring and controlling processes | Data compared with the plan and analysed: "CPI = 0.86; activity 4.2 is 5 days late; defect rate above threshold" |
| **Work performance reports** | Monitor and Control Project Performance | Compiled for decisions and communication: status reports, dashboards, heat maps, forecasts |

> **Exam tip.** A question that says "the team reports that 70% of the work is complete" is describing **data**. Once compared with the plan ("we are 10% behind"), it becomes **information**. Assembled into a dashboard for the steering committee, it becomes a **report**.

## 6.7 Change requests: the four types

Executing and monitoring processes produce **change requests**, which go through *Assess and Implement Changes*:

| Type | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **Corrective action** | Realign *current* performance with the plan (e.g., add a tester because testing is behind) |
| **Preventive action** | Ensure *future* performance stays aligned with the plan (e.g., train the team before a new technology is used) |
| **Defect repair** | Modify a non-conforming product or component |
| **Update** | Change formally controlled documents or plans to reflect modified or additional ideas or content |

# Chapter 7 — Governance Performance Domain

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- explain what project governance is and what good governance looks like;
- describe the purpose, key inputs and outputs of the nine Governance processes;
- choose an appropriate contract type and sourcing approach;
- apply integrated change control in predictive, adaptive and hybrid contexts;
- close a project or phase correctly, including early termination.

## 7.1 What the Governance domain covers

The Governance domain addresses the **framework, functions and processes that guide project activities** so the project delivers its intended outcomes in line with organisational strategy, policies and obligations. It absorbs most of the former *Integration Management* knowledge area, plus quality assurance and the sourcing (procurement) strategy.

Good governance provides:

- **Clear decision rights** — who can decide what, and the thresholds (for example, the PM can approve schedule changes up to five days; the CCB beyond that; the steering committee for anything affecting the business case).
- **Accountability and oversight** — sponsor, steering committee, PMO and audits.
- **Escalation paths** — how issues, risks and decisions move up when they exceed authority.
- **Alignment** — the project stays linked to strategy and the business case through phase gates and reviews.
- **Integration** — plans, work and changes fit together as a coherent whole.
- **Compliance** — legal, regulatory, contractual and policy obligations are met (see Chapter 17).

**Signs of effective governance (desired outcomes):** decisions are timely and made at the right level; the project remains aligned with objectives; stakeholders trust the information they receive; changes are controlled without paralysing delivery; knowledge is captured and reused; the project or phase closes cleanly with value transitioned.

> **Exam tip.** Governance is **not** bureaucracy for its own sake. The best answer tailors governance to the context: lightweight and empowering for small adaptive teams, more formal for regulated, high-risk or contract-based work.

## 7.2 Initiate Project or Phase *(Initiating)*

**Purpose:** formally authorise the project (or the next phase), define its purpose and success criteria, and give the PM authority.

| Key inputs | Key tools & techniques | Key outputs |
|---|---|---|
| Business case, benefits management plan, agreements, EEFs, OPAs | Expert judgment, data gathering (brainstorming, focus groups, interviews), interpersonal skills (facilitation, conflict management, meeting management), meetings | **Project charter**, **assumption log** |

**Charter contents:** project purpose; measurable objectives and success criteria; high-level requirements; high-level description, boundaries and key deliverables; overall project risk; summary milestone schedule; pre-approved financial resources; key stakeholder list; project approval requirements (what constitutes success, who signs off); exit criteria; assigned PM, responsibility and authority level; name and authority of the sponsor.

**Assumption log:** records high-level strategic and operational assumptions and constraints identified at initiation; later refined with detailed assumptions.

**In adaptive contexts** the equivalent artifacts are a **product vision** (often an "elevator pitch"), a **project/product charter** or **team charter**, a **product roadmap** and an initial **product backlog**. Phase or release initiation re-confirms the vision and business value.

> **Trap.** The PM should be **assigned as early as possible** — ideally during charter development — but the PM never authorises their own project. The **sponsor** issues the charter.

## 7.3 Integrate and Align Project Plans *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** define, prepare and coordinate all plan components and consolidate them into an **integrated project management plan** aligned with governance, the development approach and stakeholder expectations.

- **Outputs:** the **project management plan** (subsidiary plans, baselines, change and configuration management plans, life cycle and development approach).
- **Techniques:** expert judgment, data gathering (checklists, brainstorming), interpersonal skills (conflict management, facilitation), meetings — especially the **kickoff meeting**.
- **Kickoff meeting:** marks the end of planning and the start of execution in predictive projects (small projects may hold it right after initiation). It communicates objectives, gains commitment, explains roles and responsibilities. In adaptive projects a similar launch (often called *iteration zero*, *chartering workshop* or *inception*) aligns the team on vision, ways of working and the initial backlog.
- **Progressive elaboration:** the plan is refined as the project progresses; once baselined, changes to it go through change control.
- **Plan delivery:** choose how the product will be delivered — releases, increments, milestones — and integrate adaptive release plans with predictive milestones in hybrid projects.

## 7.4 Plan Sourcing Strategy *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** decide **what to obtain from outside** the organisation, **how** (contract type, selection method) and **from whom**, and document it in the **sourcing strategy plan** (the Sixth Edition's procurement management plan). Appendix X4 (Procurement) expands on strategic implications, make-or-buy and the project manager's role.

### Make-or-buy analysis

Compare the cost, risk, capability, schedule and strategic implications of producing internally versus buying. Consider total cost (purchase price + indirect costs + life-cycle costs), core competence, intellectual property, confidentiality, capacity, and the effect on the team.

*Example:* Renting a machine costs $500/day; buying costs $18,000 plus $100/day to operate. Break-even: 18,000 + 100d = 500d → d = 45 days. If you need it for more than 45 days, buying is cheaper.

### Contract types

| Family | Type | How the seller is paid | Who carries the cost risk |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Fixed price (FP)** | **Firm fixed price (FFP)** | Fixed total price | **Seller** (most) |
| | **Fixed price incentive fee (FPIF)** | Fixed price + incentive for meeting targets; ceiling price; cost sharing ratio | Seller (shared up to the ceiling) |
| | **Fixed price with economic price adjustment (FP-EPA)** | Fixed price adjusted for defined changes (inflation, commodity prices) — for multi-year contracts | Seller (with protection against market changes) |
| **Cost-reimbursable (CR)** | **Cost plus fixed fee (CPFF)** | Allowable costs + fixed fee (usually % of *estimated* cost) | **Buyer** |
| | **Cost plus incentive fee (CPIF)** | Costs + fee that depends on performance against targets; cost overrun/underrun shared by ratio (e.g., 80/20) | Buyer (shared) |
| | **Cost plus award fee (CPAF)** | Costs + award fee based on the buyer's subjective evaluation of performance | Buyer |
| **Time and materials (T&M)** | — | Rate per hour/day + materials at cost; often a not-to-exceed limit | Buyer (mid-way between FP and CR) |

**How to choose:**
- **Scope well defined and stable** → fixed price.
- **Scope uncertain or evolving** → cost-reimbursable or T&M (with controls such as not-to-exceed caps).
- **Small amounts of staff augmentation, or work starting before a statement of work is complete** → T&M.
- **Buyer wants to motivate performance** → incentive or award fee.

**Agile contracting options** (from the *Agile Practice Guide*): fixed-price increments (per user story or sprint), not-to-exceed T&M, graduated fixed price (rate varies with early/late delivery), early cancellation option, dynamic scope option (swap features of equal size), team augmentation, multi-tiered structures (master agreement + statements of work).

### Source selection methods

Least cost; qualifications only; quality-based/highest technical score; **quality and cost-based** (weighted scoring); sole source (single supplier, with justification); fixed budget (scope adapted to a stated budget).

### Procurement documents

| Document | Use |
|---|---|
| **Request for information (RFI)** | Gather information on what sellers can offer — early market research |
| **Request for quotation (RFQ)** | Obtain prices for well-defined, standard items |
| **Request for proposal (RFP)** | Obtain proposals for complex work where sellers propose a solution |
| **Procurement statement of work (SOW)** | Describes the procured item in enough detail for sellers to determine if they can provide it (performance, functional or design SOW) |
| **Terms of reference (TOR)** | SOW equivalent for services |
| **Source selection criteria** | How proposals will be evaluated (technical approach, price, capability, past performance, financial stability, sustainability, IP rights) |
| **Independent cost estimate** | Buyer's own estimate to benchmark proposals |

> **Exam tip.** Procurement decisions are governed by **organisational policies and legal requirements**. The PM usually works *with* the procurement or contracts department, which holds the authority to sign contracts. A PM who promises a supplier the contract informally is creating legal and ethical problems.

## 7.5 Manage Project Execution *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** lead and perform the work defined in the project management plan, and implement **approved** changes, to achieve the project's objectives.

- **Outputs:** **deliverables**, **work performance data**, **issue log**, **change requests**, updates to plans and documents.
- **Approved change requests** (corrective actions, preventive actions, defect repairs) are *implemented* here.
- The **issue log** records current problems, owners, due dates and status.
- The PMIS and meetings support execution.

> **Trap.** A team member who implements a change the customer asked for — without approval — is creating **scope creep**. In a predictive context, the request must go through *Assess and Implement Changes*; in an adaptive context, it goes to the **product owner** for backlog prioritisation.

## 7.6 Manage Quality Assurance *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** translate the quality requirements and policies into executable quality activities and **confirm that the processes being used will produce deliverables that meet requirements**. Focus is on the *process* (assurance), whereas inspecting deliverables is quality *control* (done within *Monitor and Control Scope*).

Key techniques: **quality audits**, **process analysis**, checklists, **root-cause analysis**, design for X, problem solving, cause-and-effect diagrams, flowcharts, test and evaluation documents. Key outputs: **quality reports**, test and evaluation documents, change requests (process improvements). Details in Chapter 9.

## 7.7 Manage Project Knowledge *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** use existing knowledge and create new knowledge to achieve objectives and contribute to organisational learning.

| Explicit knowledge | Tacit knowledge |
|---|---|
| Easily codified in words, pictures, numbers (manuals, lessons learned, procedures) | Personal, hard to express (experience, insights, "know-how") |
| Shared through documents and repositories (information management) | Shared through **conversation and interaction**: communities of practice, mentoring, job shadowing, storytelling, workshops, pairing, knowledge fairs |

- **Key output:** **lessons learned register** — created early and updated *throughout* the project (not only at the end). At closure it is transferred to the organisational **lessons learned repository** (an OPA).
- **Knowledge transfer** to operations and to future projects is an explicit PMP task (People domain, task 7).
- In agile teams, retrospectives, pairing, mob programming and shared code ownership spread knowledge continuously.

## 7.8 Monitor and Control Project Performance *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** track, review and report overall progress toward the objectives defined in the plan, and recommend corrective or preventive actions.

- Combines work performance information from every control process (scope, schedule, finance, resources, risk, stakeholders, communications).
- Techniques: **variance analysis**, **trend analysis**, **earned value analysis**, root-cause analysis, alternatives analysis, cost-benefit analysis, decision-making, meetings.
- Outputs: **work performance reports** (status reports, dashboards, forecasts), change requests.
- Agile/hybrid equivalents: burnup/burndown charts, cumulative flow diagrams, velocity trends, information radiators, sprint reviews.

## 7.9 Assess and Implement Changes *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** review all change requests; approve, defer or reject them; manage changes to deliverables, project documents and the project management plan; and communicate decisions. This is **integrated change control**.

### The change control flow (predictive)

1. **Identify** the need for a change (anyone can raise it).
2. **Document** it in a change request (written; verbal requests are captured in writing).
3. **Evaluate the impact** on scope, schedule, cost, quality, risk, resources, stakeholders and the business case — *before* deciding (holistic view).
4. **Explore options** (alternatives, trade-offs).
5. **Decide** — by the person or body with the authority (PM within thresholds, CCB, sponsor, steering committee, customer for contract changes).
6. **Update** the plan, baselines and documents; record in the **change log**.
7. **Communicate** the decision to affected stakeholders.
8. **Implement** (in Manage Project Execution) and **verify** the change was implemented correctly.

**Configuration management** supports change control by managing the *specifications of the product and documents*: **configuration identification** (what items are controlled), **configuration status accounting** (recording and reporting their state), and **configuration verification and audit** (confirming the composition is correct and changes were implemented as approved).

### Change in adaptive contexts

- Change is expected and welcomed. New ideas and changed requirements become **backlog items** that the **product owner** prioritises; the *current* sprint goal is protected (the team and PO negotiate if something truly urgent arises).
- Changes to the **overall** constraints (release date, budget, contract, fundamental vision) still follow governance, and may need sponsor or steering approval.

> **Exam tip — the classic sequence for a change request in a predictive project:** *evaluate impact → explore options → submit to CCB → update plan and log → communicate → implement.* The wrong answers usually skip impact analysis, go straight to the sponsor, implement before approval, or refuse to consider the change at all.

## 7.10 Close Project or Phase *(Closing)*

**Purpose:** finalise all activities for the project, phase or contract; archive information; release resources; and ensure value is transitioned.

**Closing activities:**
- Confirm all work is completed and deliverables are **formally accepted** (accepted deliverables come from *Validate Scope*).
- **Transfer** the final product, service or result to operations or the customer (with training, documentation, support arrangements).
- **Close procurements**: verify all contract work, settle claims, make final payments, issue formal written notice of contract completion, archive procurement records.
- Measure stakeholder satisfaction; complete the **final report** (summary of performance against objectives: scope, quality, cost, schedule, benefits, risks/issues).
- Finalise and transfer **lessons learned**; update OPAs; **archive** project documents.
- **Release resources** (team and physical) and celebrate/recognise contributions.
- Hand over **benefit tracking** to the benefit owner.

**Early termination:** if the project is cancelled, the PM still performs closure: document the reason, the status of deliverables (completed and incomplete), transfer partial work, close contracts per their termination clauses, capture lessons learned, release resources.

> **Trap.** "The customer has verbally accepted the product; release the team" — wrong. Obtain **formal written acceptance**, complete administrative closure and lessons learned **before** releasing resources.

## 7.11 Governance in adaptive and hybrid environments

- **Lightweight, value-based governance:** funding by increment or by product (rather than one big up-front approval), with continuation decisions based on demonstrated value.
- **Guardrails instead of gates:** clear boundaries (budget, compliance rules, architecture standards) within which teams decide.
- **Frequent transparency:** sprint reviews and information radiators replace some formal status reports.
- **Hybrid governance:** phase gates for investment decisions; sprints inside phases; a single integrated view of progress and risk for the steering committee.
- **The PMO** may evolve into a **value delivery office (VDO)** or agile centre of excellence that coaches teams rather than policing them (Appendix X2; see Chapter 17).


## Chapter 7 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 7](#ans-7) in Appendix B.*

**7.1** A project's scope is unclear, and the buyer wants the seller to start immediately while requirements are being developed. Which contract type is most appropriate?

- **A.** Firm fixed price
- **B.** Time and materials with a not-to-exceed limit
- **C.** Fixed price with economic price adjustment
- **D.** Fixed price incentive fee with a ceiling price

**7.2** Under which contract type does the seller bear the greatest cost risk?

- **A.** Firm fixed price
- **B.** Cost plus award fee
- **C.** Cost plus fixed fee
- **D.** Time and materials

**7.3** A customer emails a developer directly asking for an extra report. The developer adds it without telling anyone. In a predictive project, what does this represent and what should have happened?

- **A.** A defect repair that needs no approval
- **B.** Scope creep; it should go through integrated change control
- **C.** Gold plating; the developer should be disciplined immediately for agreeing to it
- **D.** Good customer service that should be encouraged

**7.4** A change request has been submitted to alter a key deliverable in a predictive project. What should the project manager do first?

- **A.** Submit it directly to the change control board without any analysis, to save time
- **B.** Reject it because the scope baseline is approved
- **C.** Implement it to keep the customer satisfied
- **D.** Evaluate its impact on scope, schedule, cost, quality, risk and value

**7.5** What is the main difference between explicit and tacit knowledge?

- **A.** Explicit knowledge cannot be shared outside the team
- **B.** There is no difference; both terms describe documentation
- **C.** Explicit knowledge can be codified; tacit knowledge is personal and shared through interaction
- **D.** Tacit knowledge is always written down in the lessons learned register, while explicit knowledge is not

**7.6** When should the lessons learned register be updated?

- **A.** Only after a major failure
- **B.** Only during the final closure meeting, when the whole team can reflect on the project
- **C.** Only when the sponsor requests it
- **D.** Throughout the project, then transferred to the organisation's repository at closure

**7.7** The customer has informally said they are happy with the final product. The functional managers want their people back immediately. What should the project manager do first?

- **A.** Start the next project with the same team
- **B.** Release all team members immediately
- **C.** Obtain formal acceptance before completing closure
- **D.** Archive the documents and close the project, since the customer is happy

**7.8** Which process confirms that the project's processes will produce deliverables that meet quality requirements, for example through quality audits?

- **A.** Manage Project Knowledge
- **B.** Validate Scope
- **C.** Manage Quality Assurance
- **D.** Monitor and Control Scope

**7.9** A buyer has a well-defined scope but wants to motivate the seller to finish early and under budget, sharing savings according to an agreed ratio up to a ceiling price. Which contract type fits?

- **A.** Fixed price incentive fee
- **B.** Time and materials
- **C.** Firm fixed price
- **D.** Cost plus fixed fee

**7.10** The procurement department is preparing to buy standard laptops in large quantities, where price is the main differentiator. Which procurement document is most appropriate?

- **A.** Request for proposal
- **B.** Request for quotation
- **C.** Terms of reference for consulting services
- **D.** Request for information

**7.11** Which two activities are part of closing a procurement? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Holding a bidder conference
- **B.** Creating the procurement statement of work
- **C.** Issuing a new request for proposal
- **D.** Settling open claims and making final payments
- **E.** Providing formal written notice that the contract is complete

**7.12** A sponsor cancels a project halfway through because of a strategic change. What should the project manager do?

- **A.** Ask the team to finish the most important deliverables without funding
- **B.** Perform closure: document the status, transfer completed work, close contracts and capture lessons
- **C.** Continue working until the original end date to recover the investment
- **D.** Stop all work immediately and leave the project documentation exactly as it is, since the project has been cancelled

**7.13** In an adaptive project, a stakeholder requests a new feature in the middle of a sprint. What is the most appropriate handling?

- **A.** Ask the developers to add it to the current sprint immediately, since the stakeholder is important
- **B.** Submit a formal change request to the change control board
- **C.** Refuse the request because scope is fixed
- **D.** Add it to the product backlog for the product owner to prioritise

**7.14** Which element of configuration management confirms that the product's composition is correct and that approved changes were implemented?

- **A.** Configuration verification and audit
- **B.** Configuration status accounting
- **C.** Configuration identification
- **D.** Analysis of the change log by the PMO

**7.15** Renting equipment costs $400 per day. Buying costs $12,000 plus $100 per day for operation. Beyond how many days of use does buying become cheaper?

- **A.** 24 days
- **B.** 30 days
- **C.** 40 days
- **D.** 120 days

# Chapter 8 — Scope Performance Domain

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- explain the six Scope processes and their key outputs;
- elicit, analyse, prioritise and trace requirements;
- build a WBS (scope structure) and a product backlog, and know when each is appropriate;
- distinguish validating scope from controlling quality, and scope creep from gold plating;
- manage scope in predictive, adaptive and hybrid projects.

## 8.1 What the Scope domain covers

The Scope domain addresses **defining, structuring, validating and controlling the work and the deliverables** required to achieve the project's objectives — and in the Eighth Edition it also carries **quality planning** (in *Define Scope*) and **quality control** (in *Monitor and Control Scope*).

Two kinds of scope:

| **Product scope** | **Project scope** |
|---|---|
| The features and functions that characterise the product, service or result | The work performed to deliver the product with the specified features and functions |
| Measured against the **requirements** | Measured against the **project management plan** (scope baseline) |

**Desired outcomes:** stakeholders share an understanding of what will be delivered; requirements are clear, prioritised and traceable; deliverables meet acceptance criteria; scope changes are controlled; delivered scope produces the expected value.

## 8.2 Plan Scope Management *(Planning)*

Creates the **scope management plan** (how scope will be defined, structured, validated and controlled; how the WBS/backlog will be created and maintained; how formal acceptance will be obtained) and the **requirements management plan** (how requirements will be elicited, analysed, documented, prioritised, traced, and how changes to them are handled; configuration management of requirements).

## 8.3 Elicit and Analyze Requirements *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** determine, document and manage stakeholder needs and requirements. (Formerly *Collect Requirements*; the new name emphasises that requirements are *drawn out* and *analysed*, not just collected.)

### Types of requirements

| Type | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Business** | Higher-level needs of the organisation | "Reduce customer churn by 10%" |
| **Stakeholder** | Needs of a stakeholder or group | "Branch managers need a weekly churn report" |
| **Solution — functional** | Behaviours of the product | "The system sends a renewal reminder 30 days before expiry" |
| **Solution — non-functional** | Environmental conditions or qualities (performance, security, usability, reliability, sustainability) | "Pages load in under 2 seconds for 10,000 users" |
| **Transition and readiness** | Temporary capabilities to move from current to future state | Data conversion, training |
| **Project** | Actions, processes or conditions the project must meet | Milestone dates, contractual obligations |
| **Quality** | Conditions or criteria for successful completion of a deliverable | Test coverage, acceptance thresholds |

### Elicitation techniques

| Technique | Best for |
|---|---|
| **Interviews** | Confidential or detailed information from individuals and experts |
| **Focus groups** | Prequalified stakeholders and SMEs discussing expectations of a product |
| **Facilitated workshops** (e.g., **JAD** in software, **QFD** / *voice of the customer* in manufacturing) | Cross-functional sessions that define requirements quickly and reconcile differences |
| **Questionnaires and surveys** | Large, dispersed audiences; quick statistics |
| **Brainstorming** | Generating many ideas |
| **Benchmarking** | Comparing with best-in-class practices |
| **Observation / job shadowing** | When people struggle to articulate their needs |
| **Prototypes, wireframes, storyboards** | Getting early feedback on a tangible model |
| **Document analysis** | Existing contracts, regulations, process descriptions |
| **Context diagrams** | Showing the system's scope, inputs, outputs and actors |
| **Affinity diagrams, mind mapping** | Grouping ideas into themes |
| **Nominal group technique** | Brainstorming followed by ranking/voting |
| **Story mapping** (adaptive) | Arranging user stories along the user journey and by release |

### Prioritisation techniques

- **MoSCoW** — Must have, Should have, Could have, Won't have (this time).
- **Kano model** — basic (must-be), performance (one-dimensional), excitement (delighters), indifferent and reverse features. Over time delighters become expected.
- **Cost of delay / WSJF** (weighted shortest job first = cost of delay ÷ job size).
- **100-point method**, **paired comparison**, **multicriteria weighting**.

### Requirements traceability matrix (RTM)

A grid linking each requirement to its **origin** (business need, objective) and forward to the **deliverables, design, tests and acceptance**. It ensures each requirement adds business value and is delivered and tested, and helps assess the impact of changes.

> **Exam tip.** If stakeholders disagree about requirements, the best first move is usually to **facilitate a workshop** to reach agreement — not to choose the requirement of the most senior stakeholder, and not to add everything.

## 8.4 Define Scope *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** develop a detailed description of the project and product — what is included and what is **excluded** — and (in the Eighth Edition) plan how quality will be achieved and verified.

**Project scope statement** contents:
- product scope description (progressively elaborated);
- **deliverables**;
- **acceptance criteria** (conditions that must be met before deliverables are accepted);
- **project exclusions** (what is out of scope — prevents misunderstandings);
- **constraints** and **assumptions** (also in the assumption log).

Techniques: expert judgment, **product analysis** (product breakdown, systems analysis, value engineering, requirements analysis), **alternatives analysis**, multicriteria decision analysis, facilitation.

**Quality planning within Define Scope:** identify quality requirements and standards, define **quality metrics** (what is measured and the tolerance), prepare the **quality management plan** elements, plan **cost of quality** trade-offs, design test and evaluation approaches. See Chapter 9.

## 8.5 Develop Scope Structure *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** subdivide deliverables and work into smaller, more manageable components. In predictive projects the classic structure is the **work breakdown structure (WBS)**; in adaptive projects it is a **product backlog** organised into themes, epics, features and user stories (often visualised as a story map).

### WBS essentials

- **Deliverable-oriented** hierarchical decomposition of the **total scope** of work.
- **100% rule**: the WBS includes all the work — and only the work — of the project, including project management work. The sum of the children equals the parent.
- **Work package**: the lowest level of the WBS, for which cost and duration can be estimated and managed. (A common heuristic is the 8/80 rule: between 8 and 80 hours of effort.)
- **Control account**: a management control point where scope, budget and schedule are integrated and compared with earned value; contains one or more work packages.
- **Planning package**: below a control account, with known work content but not yet detailed activities (supports rolling wave planning).
- **Code of accounts**: unique identifiers for each WBS element (e.g., 1.2.3).
- Decomposition can be organised by **phases**, **major deliverables**, subcomponents, or organisational units; different branches may be decomposed to different levels.
- Too much decomposition wastes effort; too little loses control.

**WBS dictionary**: detailed description of each WBS element — code of account, description of work, assumptions and constraints, responsible organisation, schedule milestones, associated activities, resources required, cost estimates, quality requirements, acceptance criteria, technical references, agreement information.

**Scope baseline** = approved scope statement + WBS + WBS dictionary.

### Product backlog essentials (adaptive)

- An ordered list of everything that might be needed in the product; the single source of work for the team.
- Owned and ordered by the **product owner**, by value, risk, dependencies and learning.
- Items near the top are small and detailed ("ready"); items further down are larger and less detailed (**epics** to be split later) — the backlog is **progressively elaborated** through **backlog refinement**.
- **User story** format: *"As a <role>, I want <capability>, so that <benefit>."* Good stories are **INVEST**: Independent, Negotiable, Valuable, Estimable, Small, Testable. A story has the **3 Cs**: Card, Conversation, Confirmation (acceptance criteria).
- **Definition of ready (DoR)**: conditions a backlog item must meet before the team pulls it into a sprint.
- **Definition of done (DoD)**: shared quality criteria that every increment must meet (coded, tested, reviewed, documented, deployable…). **Acceptance criteria** are specific to each story; the **DoD** applies to all.
- **MVP** (minimum viable product): the smallest product that lets you learn whether the solution delivers value. **MMF** (minimum marketable feature): the smallest piece of functionality that delivers value to users.

## 8.6 Validate Scope *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** **formalise acceptance** of completed deliverables by the customer or sponsor.

- Input: **verified deliverables** (deliverables that passed quality control).
- Technique: **inspection** with the customer (reviews, walkthroughs, demonstrations), decision-making (voting).
- Output: **accepted deliverables** (signed off), change requests for those not accepted, work performance information.
- Performed periodically throughout the project — at the end of each phase or major deliverable, not just once.
- In adaptive projects, the **sprint review** (demonstration to stakeholders) and the product owner's acceptance of stories against acceptance criteria serve this purpose.

| **Quality control** (part of Monitor and Control Scope) | **Validate Scope** |
|---|---|
| Checks **correctness** — does the deliverable meet the quality requirements? | Checks **acceptance** — does the customer formally accept it? |
| Usually performed by the team/quality staff, **first** | Performed with the customer/sponsor, **after** quality control |
| Output: **verified** deliverables | Output: **accepted** deliverables |

## 8.7 Monitor and Control Scope *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** monitor the status of project and product scope, manage changes to the scope baseline, and — in the Eighth Edition — **control quality** of deliverables.

- **Variance analysis**: compare actual scope delivered with the baseline; determine cause and degree of difference.
- **Trend analysis**: is performance improving or deteriorating?
- **Quality control**: inspection, testing, statistical sampling, control charts, checklists — producing **quality control measurements** and **verified deliverables**.
- Outputs: work performance information, change requests, updates.

### Scope creep vs gold plating

| **Scope creep** | **Gold plating** |
|---|---|
| Uncontrolled expansion of scope **without adjustments** to time, cost and resources — often driven by customer or stakeholder requests | The **team** adds extra features or quality beyond what was required, on its own initiative, often to impress the customer |
| Prevent with clear scope statements, exclusions, change control, backlog discipline | Prevent with clear acceptance criteria, DoD, culture of delivering exactly what is valued |

Both are undesirable. Gold plating increases cost and risk, and may introduce defects, without agreed value.

## 8.8 Scope in hybrid projects

- Use a **WBS for the overall structure** (phases, major deliverables, predictive components) and **backlogs** for the adaptive components — for example, the WBS element "Customer portal" is delivered through a backlog of user stories.
- Keep **one source of truth for each component** and a traceability link between requirements, backlog items and contractual deliverables.
- The **scope baseline** may define *outcomes and capacity* (e.g., "a portal meeting these objectives within six sprints") while detailed features remain flexible.


## Chapter 8 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 8](#ans-8) in Appendix B.*

**8.1** What does the 100% rule state for a WBS?

- **A.** The WBS includes all of the project's work, and only that work
- **B.** Each work package must take exactly 100 hours
- **C.** Every deliverable must have 100% test coverage
- **D.** The WBS must be 100% complete before the project charter can be approved by the sponsor

# Chapter 9 — Quality: Embedded Across Governance and Scope

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- explain where quality planning, assurance and control live in the Eighth Edition;
- distinguish quality from grade, and precision from accuracy;
- calculate and classify the cost of quality;
- use the seven basic quality tools and interpret a control chart;
- apply quality practices in adaptive teams (definition of done, test automation, continuous integration).

## 9.1 Quality in the Eighth Edition

**Quality** is the degree to which a set of inherent characteristics fulfils requirements. Two classic definitions summarise it: **conformance to requirements** (Crosby) and **fitness for use** (Juran). Quality must satisfy both the *stated* requirements and the *real needs* of stakeholders.

There is no Quality performance domain. Following the principle **Embed quality into processes and deliverables**:

| Quality activity | Eighth Edition home | Focus |
|---|---|---|
| **Quality planning** — identify standards, requirements, metrics; plan cost of quality; design tests | **Define Scope** (Scope domain) | Decide what "good" means and how it will be achieved and verified |
| **Quality assurance** — confirm processes are adequate and followed; improve them | **Manage Quality Assurance** (Governance domain) | The **process** |
| **Quality control** — inspect and test deliverables; measure results | **Monitor and Control Scope** (Scope domain) | The **product** |

The PMP exam task *Plan and optimise quality of products/deliverables* (Process domain) covers all three.

## 9.2 Key concepts

**Quality vs grade**
- **Quality** = how well the product meets requirements. Low quality is **always a problem**.
- **Grade** = a category assigned to products with the same functional use but different technical characteristics (e.g., economy vs business class). Low grade **may be acceptable** if it meets the requirements.
- *Example:* a low-grade software product with few features and no defects is acceptable; a high-grade product full of defects is not.

**Precision vs accuracy**
- **Precision** = repeated measurements are clustered closely (consistency).
- **Accuracy** = the measured value is close to the true value (correctness).
- Precise measurements are not necessarily accurate, and vice versa.

**Prevention over inspection** — quality is planned, designed and built in, not inspected in. The cost of preventing mistakes is generally much lower than the cost of correcting them when found by inspection or by customers.

**Continuous improvement** — the **Plan-Do-Check-Act (PDCA)** cycle (Shewhart, popularised by Deming) underlies modern quality management. **Kaizen** = continuous small improvements by everyone.

**Management responsibility** — success requires participation of all team members, but management provides the resources and environment for quality (Deming estimated most quality problems originate in the system, not in individual workers).

**Mutually beneficial partnership with suppliers** — quality of procured items affects quality of the whole.

### Quality theorists you may meet in questions

| Name | Key idea |
|---|---|
| **W. Edwards Deming** | PDCA, 14 points for management, most problems are system problems; reduce variation |
| **Joseph Juran** | Fitness for use; quality trilogy (planning, control, improvement); Pareto principle (80/20) applied to quality |
| **Philip Crosby** | Conformance to requirements; zero defects; "quality is free" (cost of non-conformance) |
| **Walter Shewhart** | Statistical process control; control charts; Plan-Do-Study/Check-Act |
| **Kaoru Ishikawa** | Cause-and-effect (fishbone) diagram; quality circles |
| **Genichi Taguchi** | Quality loss function; design of experiments |

### Quality methodologies

- **Total Quality Management (TQM)** — organisation-wide, customer-focused, continuous improvement.
- **Six Sigma** — reduce variation and defects to 3.4 defects per million opportunities; **DMAIC** (Define, Measure, Analyse, Improve, Control) to improve existing processes; DMADV for new designs.
- **Lean** — eliminate waste and maximise flow (see Chapter 15).
- **ISO 9000 family** — quality management system standards.

## 9.3 Cost of quality (COQ)

| **Cost of conformance** (money spent to *avoid* failures) | **Cost of non-conformance** (money spent *because of* failures) |
|---|---|
| **Prevention costs** — build a quality product: training, documenting processes, equipment, time to do it right, design reviews | **Internal failure costs** — failures found *by the project*: rework, scrap |
| **Appraisal costs** — assess the quality: testing, destructive testing loss, inspections | **External failure costs** — failures found *by the customer*: liabilities, warranty work, lost business, reputation |

> **Exam tip.** Spending on prevention and appraisal is usually justified because external failures are the most expensive. A question that asks "which is the most expensive cost of quality?" is pointing at **external failure**. Also note: **warranty claims** = external failure; **inspection** = appraisal; **training** = prevention; **rework before delivery** = internal failure.

## 9.4 Quality metrics and the quality management plan

A **quality metric** describes a product or project attribute and how it is measured, with an allowable variation — the **tolerance**. Examples: defect density, failure rate, on-time performance, availability (99.9%), customer satisfaction score, test coverage, mean time between failures.

The **quality management plan** (planned within Define Scope) describes: quality standards adopted; quality objectives; roles and responsibilities; deliverables and processes subject to review; quality control and assurance activities; tools; procedures for handling non-conformance, corrective actions and continuous improvement.

## 9.5 The seven basic quality tools

| Tool | What it shows | Typical use |
|---|---|---|
| **Cause-and-effect (fishbone, Ishikawa) diagram** | Possible causes grouped in categories (e.g., people, methods, machines, materials, measurement, environment) leading to an effect | Root-cause analysis; often combined with the **5 Whys** |
| **Flowchart (process map)** | Steps, decisions and flow of a process (SIPOC: suppliers, inputs, process, outputs, customers) | Find inefficiencies and failure points |
| **Check sheet (tally sheet)** | Counts of occurrences by category | Collect data on defects efficiently |
| **Pareto diagram** | Vertical bars ordered by frequency, with a cumulative line | Focus on the **vital few** causes that produce most problems (80/20 rule) |
| **Histogram** | Distribution of data (bar chart of frequencies) | Show shape and spread of measurements |
| **Control chart** | Process data over time against control limits | Determine whether a process is **stable/predictable** |
| **Scatter diagram** | Relationship between two variables | Identify **correlation** (not proof of causation) |

Other useful tools: **affinity diagrams**, **matrix diagrams**, **mind maps**, **logical data models**, **design of experiments (DOE)**, **statistical sampling**, **benchmarking**, **audits**, **checklists**.

### Control charts in detail

- **Mean** — the centre line of the process.
- **Upper and lower control limits (UCL/LCL)** — typically set at **±3 sigma** from the mean; they reflect the *voice of the process* and are calculated from process data.
- **Specification limits** — set by the *customer* or requirements (voice of the customer). A process can be in control but still not meet specifications, if the specification limits are tighter than the control limits.
- **Out of control** when:
  - a single point falls **outside** a control limit; or
  - the **rule of seven**: seven consecutive points fall on one side of the mean (a non-random trend), even if all are within limits.
- **Common-cause (random) variation** is inherent in the process; **special-cause (assignable) variation** has a specific, identifiable cause that should be investigated.

### Statistical sampling

- **Attribute sampling** — the result conforms or does not (pass/fail).
- **Variable sampling** — the result is rated on a continuous scale measuring the degree of conformity.
- Sampling is used when inspecting everything is too costly or destructive.

### Sigma levels (for reference)

| ± sigma | % of values within limits |
|---|---|
| 1σ | 68.27% |
| 2σ | 95.45% |
| 3σ | 99.73% |
| 6σ | 99.9999998% (Six Sigma programmes target 3.4 defects per million, allowing a 1.5σ shift) |

## 9.6 Quality assurance in practice (Manage Quality Assurance)

- **Quality audits** — structured, independent reviews to determine whether project activities comply with policies, processes and procedures; identify good practices, gaps and non-conformities; share good practices; support process improvement.
- **Process analysis** — identify improvements, non-value-adding activities, constraints.
- **Design for X (DfX)** — design to optimise a particular characteristic (cost, reliability, manufacturability, serviceability, sustainability).
- **Problem solving** — define the problem, identify root cause, generate solutions, choose, implement, verify.
- Output: **quality reports** (issues, recommendations for corrective actions), **test and evaluation documents**, change requests.

## 9.7 Quality in adaptive teams

- **Definition of done** — a shared, explicit quality standard for every increment.
- **Acceptance criteria** for each story, often written as examples (behaviour-driven development: *Given – When – Then*).
- **Test-driven development (TDD)**, **automated testing**, **continuous integration and delivery (CI/CD)** — build quality in and get fast feedback.
- **Pair programming**, **mob programming**, **code reviews** — real-time inspection and knowledge sharing.
- **Refactoring** — continuously improve the design to keep technical debt low.
- **Retrospectives** — inspect and adapt the process every iteration (continuous improvement).
- **Small batch sizes** — reveal problems early; reduce the cost of fixing them.
- **Escaped defects** — a key quality metric: defects found after release.

> **Trap.** "The team is behind; skip testing this sprint and catch up later" is never the right answer. Lowering the definition of done creates technical debt and violates the principle of embedding quality. The right answer usually involves the product owner adjusting **scope**, not quality.

# Chapter 10 — Schedule Performance Domain

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe the three Schedule processes and the schedule management plan;
- define activities, sequence them with dependencies, leads and lags, and estimate durations (including PERT);
- calculate the critical path, total float and free float with forward and backward passes;
- choose between crashing, fast tracking, resource levelling and resource smoothing;
- plan and track schedules in adaptive work (release planning, velocity, burn charts, flow metrics).

## 10.1 What the Schedule domain covers

The Schedule domain addresses **planning, developing and controlling the timing of the work** so the project delivers its outcomes when they are needed. It has three processes:

| Process | Focus area | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Plan Schedule Management** | Planning | Establish policies, procedures and documentation for planning, developing, managing, executing and controlling the schedule |
| **Develop Schedule** | Planning | Define activities, sequence them, estimate durations and resources, and build the schedule model and baseline (or release/iteration plans) |
| **Monitor and Control Schedule** | Monitoring and Controlling | Monitor status, manage changes to the schedule baseline, forecast completion |

## 10.2 Plan Schedule Management

The **schedule management plan** specifies: the scheduling methodology and tool; schedule model development and maintenance; level of accuracy (rounding of durations, contingency); **units of measure** (hours, days, story points); **organisational procedure links** (WBS framework); **control thresholds** (e.g., ±10% variance before action); **rules of performance measurement** (EVM rules, % complete methods); reporting formats; release and iteration lengths for adaptive work.

## 10.3 Develop Schedule — step by step

### Step 1: Define activities

Decompose **work packages** into **activities** (the work needed to produce the deliverables). Outputs: **activity list**, **activity attributes** (ID, description, predecessors, successors, lead/lag, resources, constraints, location), **milestone list** (milestones have **zero duration** — they mark significant points or events). Use **rolling wave planning** for later work.

### Step 2: Sequence activities — the precedence diagramming method (PDM)

PDM uses **activity-on-node (AON)** diagrams with four relationship types:

| Relationship | Meaning | Example | Frequency |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Finish-to-start (FS)** | B cannot start until A finishes | Pour foundation, then build walls | **Most common** |
| **Start-to-start (SS)** | B cannot start until A starts | Start levelling concrete after pouring starts | Common |
| **Finish-to-finish (FF)** | B cannot finish until A finishes | Finish editing after writing finishes | Common |
| **Start-to-finish (SF)** | B cannot finish until A starts | Old security shift cannot end until the new shift starts | **Rare** |

**Dependency types:**

| Type | Description |
|---|---|
| **Mandatory (hard logic)** | Required by contract or inherent in the nature of the work (you can't test code that hasn't been written) |
| **Discretionary (preferred / soft logic)** | Based on best practice or preference; can be changed — **the first candidates for fast tracking** |
| **External** | Involve a relationship with non-project activities (permit from a regulator, vendor delivery) — usually outside the team's control |
| **Internal** | Between project activities, usually within the team's control |

A dependency can be both mandatory and external, etc.

**Leads and lags:**
- **Lead** = the successor can start *before* the predecessor finishes (FS −2 days) — an acceleration.
- **Lag** = a required *delay* between activities (FS +3 days: wait three days for concrete to cure).

### Step 3: Estimate resources and durations

| Technique | How it works | When to use |
|---|---|---|
| **Expert judgment** | Experience of people who did similar work | Always useful |
| **Analogous** (top-down) | Use the actual duration/cost of a similar past project, adjusted | Early, little detail; quick but less accurate |
| **Parametric** | Statistical relationship × quantity (e.g., 2 hours per m² × 500 m²) | When reliable unit rates exist; can be very accurate |
| **Three-point** | Optimistic (O), most likely (M), pessimistic (P) | When uncertainty is significant |
| **Bottom-up** | Estimate each lower-level component and aggregate | When detail is available; most accurate, most time-consuming |
| **Data analysis: alternatives, reserve analysis** | Compare options; add contingency reserves for identified risks | Throughout |
| **Decision-making (e.g., fist of five), Delphi** | Build consensus, reduce bias | Team estimates |

**Three-point formulas**

- **Triangular distribution:** E = (O + M + P) ÷ 3
- **Beta / PERT distribution:** E = (O + 4M + P) ÷ 6
- **Standard deviation (PERT):** σ = (P − O) ÷ 6
- Variance = σ²; for a path, add variances (not standard deviations), then take the square root.

*Example:* O = 4, M = 6, P = 14 days. PERT E = (4 + 24 + 14) ÷ 6 = **7 days**; σ = (14 − 4) ÷ 6 = **1.67 days**. Triangular E = 24 ÷ 3 = 8 days. Range at ±1σ: 5.33 to 8.67 days (≈68% confidence); ±2σ ≈ 95%; ±3σ ≈ 99.7%.

**Remember:** *effort* (person-hours of work) is not *duration* (calendar time). Adding people does not reduce duration linearly (law of diminishing returns, communication overhead — Brooks's law: adding people to a late software project makes it later). **Parkinson's law**: work expands to fill the time available. **Student syndrome**: people start at the last possible moment.

### Step 4: Build the schedule model — critical path method (CPM)

- **Forward pass** → early start (ES) and early finish (EF). Convention used here: ES of the first activity = 0; EF = ES + duration; ES of a successor = the **largest** EF of its predecessors.
- **Backward pass** → late finish (LF) and late start (LS). LF of the last activity = its EF (project end); LS = LF − duration; LF of a predecessor = the **smallest** LS of its successors.
- **Total float (slack)** = LS − ES = LF − EF — how long an activity can be delayed without delaying the **project end date**.
- **Free float** = ES of the earliest successor − EF of the activity — how long it can be delayed without delaying **any successor's early start**.
- **Critical path** = the **longest path** through the network; it determines the shortest possible project duration; activities on it usually have **zero total float** (negative float means the path is already late against a constraint date).
- **Near-critical path** = a path with little float; watch it closely. A project can have more than one critical path — which increases risk.

#### Worked example

| Activity | Duration (days) | Predecessor |
|---|---|---|
| A | 3 | — |
| B | 5 | A |
| C | 2 | A |
| D | 4 | C |
| E | 3 | B, D |

Paths: **A-B-E** = 3 + 5 + 3 = **11**; A-C-D-E = 3 + 2 + 4 + 3 = 12. Critical path = **A-C-D-E (12 days)**.

Forward pass: A (0–3); B (3–8); C (3–5); D (5–9); E starts at max(8, 9) = 9, finishes 12.
Backward pass: E (LS 9, LF 12); B (LF 9, LS 4); D (LF 9, LS 5); C (LF 5, LS 3); A (LF = min(4, 3) = 3, LS 0).
Float: A 0, C 0, D 0, E 0, **B: LS − ES = 4 − 3 = 1 day** (free float of B = ES of E − EF of B = 9 − 8 = 1).

### Step 5: Optimise — resource optimisation and compression

| Technique | What it does | Effect |
|---|---|---|
| **Resource levelling** | Adjusts start and finish dates to balance resource demand with supply (e.g., one specialist can't do two activities at once) | **Can change the critical path and lengthen the project** |
| **Resource smoothing** | Adjusts activities **within their float** so resource requirements don't exceed limits | Critical path and end date **do not change**; may not optimise all resources |
| **Crashing** | Adds resources (overtime, extra people, faster equipment) to critical-path activities to shorten duration at the **lowest additional cost** | **Increases cost**; works only on critical activities where extra resources help |
| **Fast tracking** | Performs activities in **parallel** that were planned in sequence (usually by changing discretionary dependencies or using leads) | **Increases risk and potential rework**; little or no added cost |

**Crashing logic:** crash the critical activity with the **lowest cost per day saved** first, one day at a time, re-checking the critical path because another path may become critical.

> **Exam tip.** "The sponsor wants the project to finish two weeks early, without increasing the budget" → **fast tracking**. "…without increasing risk, money is available" → **crashing**. Neither helps if applied to non-critical activities.

Other schedule-model tools: **what-if scenario analysis**, **Monte Carlo simulation** (probability distribution of completion dates, e.g., "80% chance of finishing by 30 June"), **agile release planning**, the **critical chain method** (resource-constrained critical path with **buffers**: project buffer at the end of the chain, feeding buffers where non-critical chains join, resource buffers; durations stripped of individual padding).

### Step 6: Present and baseline the schedule

- **Schedule baseline** — the approved version of the schedule model; changed only through change control.
- Presentations: **bar (Gantt) charts** (easy to read, show durations and dates), **milestone charts** (only key dates — good for executives), **project schedule network diagrams** (logic and critical path), **calendars**, **roadmaps**.
- **Schedule data** — milestones, activities, attributes, assumptions, constraints, resource requirements, alternative schedules, contingency reserves.

## 10.4 Scheduling in adaptive projects

| Concept | Description |
|---|---|
| **Timeboxing** | Fixed periods (sprints/iterations of 1–4 weeks, meetings with maximum durations) |
| **Release planning** | Determine features for each release based on the roadmap, priorities and velocity |
| **Iteration (sprint) planning** | The team selects backlog items it can complete in the iteration and plans the work |
| **Story points** | Relative estimates of size/complexity/uncertainty (often Fibonacci: 1, 2, 3, 5, 8, 13, 21) |
| **Planning poker** | Team estimation by simultaneously revealing cards; discuss outliers; re-vote |
| **Affinity estimating / T-shirt sizing** | Quickly group many items by relative size (S, M, L, XL) |
| **Velocity** | Story points completed per iteration; used by **the team** for forecasting — never to compare teams |
| **Capacity** | Available team time in an iteration (holidays, part-time members) |
| **On-demand scheduling (Kanban/pull)** | Work is pulled from the backlog when capacity is available, limited by WIP limits |

**Forecasting with velocity:** remaining backlog ÷ average velocity = number of iterations remaining. *Example:* 240 points remaining ÷ 30 points per sprint = 8 sprints; with 2-week sprints = 16 weeks.

### Tracking charts

- **Burndown chart** — remaining work vs time. A line above the ideal line means behind.
- **Burnup chart** — completed work vs time, plus a total-scope line; shows **scope changes** clearly (the scope line moves up).
- **Cumulative flow diagram (CFD)** — bands of work in each state over time; widening bands show **bottlenecks**; band thickness = WIP; horizontal distance ≈ cycle time.
- **Lead time** = time from request to delivery (customer view). **Cycle time** = time from work start to completion (team view). **Throughput** = items completed per period.
- **Little's Law**: average cycle time = WIP ÷ throughput. *Example:* 12 items in progress, 4 completed per week → cycle time = 3 weeks. To reduce cycle time, **reduce WIP**.

## 10.5 Monitor and Control Schedule

- Determine current status, compare with the baseline (variance analysis), assess trends, forecast completion.
- **Earned value schedule measures**: SV = EV − PV; SPI = EV ÷ PV (see Chapter 11).
- Techniques: performance reviews, trend analysis, **critical path analysis** (is the critical path changing?), what-if analysis, resource optimisation, leads and lags, schedule compression, **iteration burndown** and **velocity trend** in adaptive work.
- Outputs: work performance information, **schedule forecasts**, change requests (to the baseline), updates.
- Agile focus: are we delivering value at a sustainable pace? Retrospectives address impediments; the product owner adjusts scope to meet dates.

> **Trap.** A non-critical activity that is late is not necessarily a problem — check its **float** before taking action. Conversely, a critical activity that slips by one day delays the project by one day unless you recover it.

- **A.** Crashing
- **B.** Resource smoothing

**10.5** Activities: A (4 days, no predecessor), B (6 days, after A), C (3 days, after A), D (5 days, after C), E (2 days, after B and D). What is the length of the critical path?

- **A.** 12 days
- **B.** 13 days
- **C.** 14 days
- **D.** 20 days

**10.6** In the same network (A 4, B 6 after A, C 3 after A, D 5 after C, E 2 after B and D), what is the total float of activity B?

- **A.** 2 days

- **C.** 4 days
- **D.** 0 days

- **D.** Mandatory

- **B.** 15 weeks

- **B.** Burndown chart without a scope line

- **D.** Burnup chart with a total-scope line

**10.11** A manager wants to compare the productivity of two Scrum teams using their velocities. What should the project manager advise?

- **B.** Normalise both to hours and rank the developers
- **C.** Ask the teams to double their velocity

**10.12** Which two statements about the critical path are correct? (Choose two.)

- **B.** It is the path with the most activities
