# Chapter 11 — Finance Performance Domain

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe the four Finance processes and the financial management plan;
- choose estimating techniques and state typical accuracy ranges;
- build a budget with contingency and management reserves and explain the cost baseline;
- perform earned value calculations and forecasts, and interpret the results;
- evaluate projects with financial measures (NPV, IRR, ROI, payback, BCR) and explain funding concepts.

## 11.1 What the Finance domain covers

The Finance domain addresses **the planning, estimating, budgeting, funding and control of the project's financial resources**, and the link between money spent and value created. It replaces the Sixth Edition's Cost Management and broadens it to **funding, financial value and financial governance**.

| Process | Focus area | Key output |
|---|---|---|
| **Plan Financial Management** | Planning | Financial management plan |
| **Estimate Costs** | Planning | Cost estimates, basis of estimates |
| **Develop Budget** | Planning | Cost baseline, project funding requirements |
| **Monitor and Control Finances** | Monitoring and Controlling | Work performance information, cost forecasts, change requests |

**Desired outcomes:** costs are estimated realistically; funding is available when needed; spending is tracked against value delivered; variances are detected early and acted on; financial decisions support the business case.

## 11.2 Plan Financial Management

The **financial management plan** (formerly cost management plan) defines: **units of measure** (currency, staff-hours); **level of precision** (rounding, e.g., to the nearest $100); **level of accuracy** (acceptable range, e.g., ±10%, including contingency); **organisational procedure links** (control accounts in the WBS); **control thresholds** (variance that triggers action); **rules of performance measurement** (EVM techniques, how % complete is measured); funding sources and timing; currency and exchange-rate handling; reporting formats; approval authorities for spending.

## 11.3 Estimate Costs

### Cost concepts

| Concept | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Direct costs** | Attributable directly to the project (team salaries, materials) |
| **Indirect costs** | Shared across projects (overheads such as office rent, utilities) |
| **Fixed costs** | Do not change with volume (equipment set-up) |
| **Variable costs** | Change with volume (materials per unit, hourly labour) |
| **Sunk costs** | Already spent and cannot be recovered — **must not influence** future decisions |
| **Opportunity cost** | The value of the best alternative given up by choosing one option |
| **Life-cycle costing** | Total cost of ownership over the product's life (acquisition, operation, maintenance, disposal) |
| **Depreciation** | Allocation of an asset's cost over its useful life (straight-line or accelerated) |
| **Cost of quality** | Conformance + non-conformance costs (Chapter 9) |

### Estimating techniques

Same family as duration estimating: **analogous**, **parametric**, **bottom-up**, **three-point** (PERT), expert judgment, **reserve analysis**, **cost of quality**, **alternatives analysis**, decision-making, PMIS, and (in adaptive work) estimating by **team cost per iteration** (burn rate) or **cost per story point**.

### Accuracy ranges

| Estimate | Typical range | When |
|---|---|---|
| **Rough order of magnitude (ROM)** | **−25% to +75%** | Initiation |
| **Budget estimate** | −10% to +25% | Early planning |
| **Definitive estimate** | **−5% to +10%** | Detailed planning |

The output includes **cost estimates** and the **basis of estimates** (documentation of how the estimate was derived, assumptions, constraints, range, confidence level).

## 11.4 Develop Budget

**Cost aggregation:** activity estimates → work package estimates → control accounts → project.

| Layer | Contents |
|---|---|
| Activity and work package cost estimates | From Estimate Costs |
| **+ Contingency reserves** | For **identified** risks (known-unknowns); controlled by the PM within the plan's rules |
| **= Cost baseline** | Approved, time-phased budget (the **S-curve**); basis for EVM; = **BAC** |
| **+ Management reserves** | For **unidentified** risks (unknown-unknowns); controlled by management/sponsor; using them requires a change to the cost baseline |
| **= Project budget** | Total funds authorised |

> **Exam tip.** The **cost baseline includes contingency reserve but not management reserve**. Earned value is measured against the cost baseline (the PMB), so management reserve is **not** part of BAC.

**Funding limit reconciliation:** compare planned spending with funding limits (e.g., the organisation can release only $200,000 per quarter); reschedule work if necessary. **Project funding requirements** are derived from the cost baseline plus management reserve and are often provided in **incremental, step-like amounts**, while the expenditure curve is smooth.

**Funding sources** may include internal capital budgets, external financing, grants, customer payments, public–private partnerships. **CAPEX** (capital expenditures, creating assets) vs **OPEX** (operational expenditures, running costs) treatment matters to finance departments and can affect how agile work is funded.

**Adaptive funding:** many organisations fund **persistent teams or products** for a period (e.g., a quarter) and decide continuation based on value delivered (incremental or "venture capital" style funding).

## 11.5 Monitor and Control Finances — earned value management (EVM)

EVM integrates scope, schedule and cost to measure performance and forecast results.

### The three basic values

| Term | Definition | Question it answers |
|---|---|---|
| **PV — planned value** | Authorised budget assigned to the work **scheduled** to be done by now | How much work *should* be done? |
| **EV — earned value** | Budgeted value of the work **actually completed** by now | How much work *is* done (in budget terms)? |
| **AC — actual cost** | Actual cost incurred for the work completed by now | How much did the completed work *cost*? |
| **BAC — budget at completion** | Total planned value of the project (the cost baseline) | What is the total budget? |

### Variances and indices

| Measure | Formula | Interpretation |
|---|---|---|
| **Cost variance** | **CV = EV − AC** | Negative = over budget; positive = under budget |
| **Schedule variance** | **SV = EV − PV** | Negative = behind schedule; positive = ahead |
| **Cost performance index** | **CPI = EV ÷ AC** | < 1 = over budget (getting less than $1 of work per $1); > 1 = under budget |
| **Schedule performance index** | **SPI = EV ÷ PV** | < 1 = behind; > 1 = ahead |
| **Variance at completion** | **VAC = BAC − EAC** | Negative = expected overrun |

*Memory aid:* **EV comes first** in every variance and every index. Negative/less than 1 is bad.

### Forecasting: estimate at completion (EAC)

| Situation | Formula |
|---|---|
| Current cost performance will **continue** (most common default) | **EAC = BAC ÷ CPI** |
| Past variance was **atypical**; future work at the planned rate | **EAC = AC + (BAC − EV)** |
| Both cost and schedule performance influence the remaining work | **EAC = AC + [(BAC − EV) ÷ (CPI × SPI)]** |
| The original estimate is **fundamentally flawed**; re-estimate | **EAC = AC + bottom-up ETC** |

**Estimate to complete:** **ETC = EAC − AC** (how much more will we spend).

**To-complete performance index (TCPI)** — the cost performance needed on the remaining work to meet a target:
- To meet the **BAC**: **TCPI = (BAC − EV) ÷ (BAC − AC)**
- To meet a new approved **EAC**: TCPI = (BAC − EV) ÷ (EAC − AC)
- TCPI > 1 → you must be **more efficient** than planned for the rest of the project.

### Worked example

A project has BAC = $200,000, planned to finish in 10 months at an even spend rate. At the end of month 4, the project is 30% complete and has spent $70,000.

- PV = 40% × 200,000 = **$80,000** (4 of 10 months)
- EV = 30% × 200,000 = **$60,000**
- AC = **$70,000**
- CV = 60,000 − 70,000 = **−$10,000** (over budget)
- SV = 60,000 − 80,000 = **−$20,000** (behind schedule)
- CPI = 60,000 ÷ 70,000 = **0.857**
- SPI = 60,000 ÷ 80,000 = **0.75**
- EAC (typical) = 200,000 ÷ 0.857 ≈ **$233,333**
- ETC = 233,333 − 70,000 ≈ **$163,333**
- VAC = 200,000 − 233,333 ≈ **−$33,333**
- TCPI (to meet BAC) = (200,000 − 60,000) ÷ (200,000 − 70,000) = 140,000 ÷ 130,000 ≈ **1.077**

**Interpretation:** the project is over budget and behind schedule; at the current efficiency it will overrun by about $33,000; to finish within the original budget the team would need to work about 7.7% more efficiently than planned — unrealistic unless something changes. The PM should analyse root causes and present options (scope trade-offs, corrective actions, re-baseline through change control).

### Measuring percent complete (rules of performance measurement)

- **0/100** — credit only when complete (conservative; for short tasks).
- **50/50** or **20/80** — partial credit at start, the rest at completion.
- **Weighted milestones**, **physical % complete**, **units completed**.
- Avoid subjective "90% done" estimates — the 90% syndrome.

### Other control techniques

**Variance analysis**, **trend analysis**, **reserve analysis** (are remaining reserves adequate? release unused contingency when risks pass), **forecasting**, **burn rate** tracking in agile (team cost per sprint × sprints remaining), **agile EVM** (EV based on story points completed vs planned per release).

## 11.6 Financial measures for selecting and justifying projects

| Measure | Formula / meaning | Decision rule |
|---|---|---|
| **Present value (PV)** | PV = FV ÷ (1 + r)ⁿ | Converts future money into today's money |
| **Net present value (NPV)** | Sum of discounted inflows − discounted outflows (initial investment) | **Higher is better; NPV > 0 is acceptable.** NPV already accounts for the project's duration — do not adjust for years again |
| **Internal rate of return (IRR)** | Discount rate at which NPV = 0 | **Higher is better**; compare with the cost of capital (hurdle rate) |
| **Return on investment (ROI)** | (Net benefit ÷ cost) × 100% | Higher is better |
| **Payback period** | Time to recover the initial investment from net cash inflows | **Shorter is better**; ignores time value of money (unless discounted) and benefits after payback |
| **Benefit–cost ratio (BCR)** | Benefits ÷ costs (present values) | **> 1** means benefits exceed costs; higher is better |
| **Opportunity cost** | Value of the option not chosen | When choosing project A (NPV 50k) over B (NPV 40k), the opportunity cost is **40k** |
| **Economic value added (EVA)** | Net operating profit after tax − (capital × cost of capital) | Positive means value is created |

*PV example:* $121,000 received in 2 years at 10% → PV = 121,000 ÷ 1.21 = **$100,000**.

*Payback example:* invest $300,000; net inflows $75,000 per year → payback = **4 years**.

> **Trap.** **Sunk costs** are irrelevant: "We have already spent $2 million, so we must continue" is not a valid reason. Decide based on future costs and benefits.

# Chapter 12 — Stakeholders Performance Domain (including Communications)

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- identify, analyse and prioritise stakeholders using common models;
- plan, manage and monitor stakeholder engagement, including expectation alignment;
- plan, manage and monitor communications, and calculate communication channels;
- choose communication methods (interactive, push, pull) and technologies appropriate to the audience;
- engage stakeholders effectively in predictive, adaptive and hybrid projects.

## 12.1 What the Stakeholders domain covers

The Stakeholders domain addresses **identifying, understanding, engaging and communicating with the people and organisations who affect or are affected by the project**. In the Eighth Edition it contains both stakeholder engagement and communications (seven processes):

| Focus area | Processes |
|---|---|
| Initiating | **Identify Stakeholders** |
| Planning | **Plan Stakeholder Engagement**, **Plan Communications Management** |
| Executing | **Manage Stakeholder Engagement**, **Manage Communications** |
| Monitoring and Controlling | **Monitor Stakeholder Engagement**, **Monitor Communications** |

**Desired outcomes:** a productive working relationship with stakeholders; agreement on objectives; stakeholders who are supporters are satisfied; opposition does not undermine the project; information reaches the right people, at the right time, in the right format; expectations are aligned with what will be delivered.

> **Exam tip.** Stakeholder engagement is **continuous**. Identify stakeholders early and **re-identify** them throughout (at each phase, when the organisation changes, when someone new appears). A stakeholder who "suddenly appears" in the middle of a project is a sign that identification was incomplete — add them to the register and analyse them.

## 12.2 Identify Stakeholders *(Initiating)*

**Purpose:** identify stakeholders regularly and analyse their interests, involvement, interdependencies, influence and potential impact on success.

- **Inputs:** charter, business documents, agreements, EEFs, OPAs.
- **Techniques:** brainstorming, interviews, questionnaires and surveys, **stakeholder analysis**, document analysis, **data representation** (stakeholder mapping).
- **Output:** the **stakeholder register** — identification information (name, position, contact, role), **assessment information** (major requirements, expectations, potential for influence, phase of most interest), **stakeholder classification** (internal/external, impact, influence, power, interest, direction of influence).

### Stakeholder analysis models

| Model | Dimensions | Use |
|---|---|---|
| **Power/interest grid** | Power (authority) × interest (concern) | High power–high interest: **manage closely**; high power–low interest: **keep satisfied**; low power–high interest: **keep informed**; low power–low interest: **monitor** |
| **Power/influence grid** | Authority × active involvement | Similar quadrants |
| **Impact/influence grid** | Ability to cause changes × active involvement | Similar quadrants |
| **Salience model** | **Power** (ability to impose will), **urgency** (need for immediate attention — time-sensitive or high-stakes), **legitimacy** (appropriateness of involvement) | Stakeholders with all three ("definitive") get most attention; useful for large, complex communities |
| **Stakeholder cube** | Combines grid dimensions in 3-D | Refines classification |
| **Directions of influence** | **Upward** (senior management, sponsor), **downward** (team, specialists), **outward** (suppliers, regulators, public, customers), **sideward** (peers, other PMs) | Tailor communications and engagement |
| **Prioritisation** | Rank by impact, interest, power | When there are many stakeholders |

## 12.3 Plan Stakeholder Engagement *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** develop approaches to involve stakeholders based on their needs, expectations, interests and potential impact.

### Stakeholder engagement assessment matrix

Compares **current (C)** and **desired (D)** engagement levels:

| Stakeholder | Unaware | Resistant | Neutral | Supportive | Leading |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| Finance director | | C | | D | |
| Union representative | C | | D | | |
| Head of sales | | | | C | D |

- **Unaware** — not aware of the project and its potential impacts.
- **Resistant** — aware but resistant to the project or its changes.
- **Neutral** — aware but neither supportive nor resistant.
- **Supportive** — aware and supportive of the work and its outcomes.
- **Leading** — aware and actively engaged in ensuring success.

The gap between C and D drives engagement actions. Not every stakeholder needs to be "leading" — the desired level depends on their role.

**Output:** the **stakeholder engagement plan** — strategies and actions for productive involvement; may be formal or informal, detailed or broad. It can contain sensitive information (e.g., notes about resistant stakeholders), so distribution must be handled carefully.

## 12.4 Manage Stakeholder Engagement *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** communicate and work with stakeholders to meet their needs and expectations, address issues and foster appropriate involvement.

- Engage stakeholders at the right stages; manage expectations through negotiation and communication; address risks and concerns; clarify and resolve issues.
- **Techniques:** communication skills (feedback), **interpersonal skills** (conflict management, cultural awareness, negotiation, observation/conversation, political awareness), ground rules, meetings.
- **Outputs:** change requests, issue log updates, updates to the stakeholder register and engagement plan.

### Aligning and managing expectations (PMP People tasks 5 and 6)

- **Surface expectations early** — interviews, workshops, product vision sessions; record them (stakeholder register, requirements, assumptions).
- **Find common ground** — facilitate workshops; use objective criteria (business case, value, strategy).
- **Make trade-offs explicit** — show the impact of competing demands on scope, schedule, cost and risk.
- **Confirm agreement** — documented decisions, acceptance criteria, working agreements.
- **Monitor satisfaction** — feedback, surveys, demos, informal conversations.
- **Reset expectations transparently** when reality changes — early, honestly, with options.

## 12.5 Monitor Stakeholder Engagement *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** monitor relationships and tailor engagement strategies. Techniques: alternatives analysis, root-cause analysis, stakeholder analysis, **engagement assessment matrix** (has C moved toward D?), feedback, active listening, cultural awareness, leadership, networking, political awareness, meetings. Outputs: work performance information, change requests, updates.

## 12.6 Plan Communications Management *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** develop an appropriate approach and plan for communication activities based on the information needs of each stakeholder or group, available assets and the needs of the project.

### Communication requirements analysis

Determine the information needs of stakeholders: who needs what information, when, how often, in what format, who provides it, and why. Consider organisational charts, stakeholder responsibilities, disciplines involved, logistics (locations, time zones), internal and external information needs, legal requirements.

### Number of communication channels

**Channels = n (n − 1) ÷ 2**, where n = number of people (including the PM).

*Example:* a team of 10 has 10 × 9 ÷ 2 = **45** channels. If it grows to 15, there are 15 × 14 ÷ 2 = 105 channels — **60 more**. Communication complexity grows much faster than team size.

### Communication models

**Basic sender–receiver model:** the **sender** encodes the message (thoughts → words, symbols) and transmits it through a **medium/channel**; **noise** (distance, technology, culture, language, distractions) may interfere; the **receiver** decodes it and **acknowledges** receipt; then provides **feedback/response**. The sender is responsible for clear encoding and for confirming the message was understood; the receiver for decoding completely and acknowledging.

**Interactive model** adds the importance of feedback loops; **cross-cultural communication** recognises that the sender's and receiver's cultures, generations, experiences and emotional states affect understanding.

**Gulf of execution and evaluation** (Norman): the gap between what a user intends and what the system allows, and between what the system shows and what the user understands — relevant for designing communications and products.

### Communication methods

| Method | Description | Examples | Best for |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Interactive** | Real-time, multidirectional exchange | Meetings, phone/video calls, instant messaging, workshops | Immediate understanding, complex or sensitive topics, decisions |
| **Push** | Sent to specific recipients; ensures distribution but not understanding | Letters, memos, emails, reports, voicemails, press releases | Targeted information that must reach people |
| **Pull** | Recipients access the content at their own discretion | Intranet, wikis, knowledge repositories, e-learning, dashboards | Large volumes of information or large audiences |

### Other communication dimensions

- **Formal** (reports, briefings, contracts) vs **informal** (emails, ad-hoc conversations).
- **Written** vs **oral**; **verbal** vs **non-verbal** (most of the meaning in face-to-face communication is carried non-verbally).
- **Internal** vs **external**; **official** vs **unofficial**; **hierarchical** (upward, downward) vs **horizontal** (peers).
- **Communication technology** choices depend on urgency, availability, ease of use, project environment (co-located vs virtual), sensitivity and confidentiality.

### Communication skills

- **Active listening** — paying full attention, paraphrasing, asking clarifying questions.
- **Feedback** — timely, specific, constructive.
- **Presentations**, **meeting management** (agenda, timebox, facilitation, minutes, action items).
- The **5 Cs of written communication**: correct grammar and spelling, concise expression, clear purpose and expression for the reader, coherent logical flow, controlling flow of words and ideas.

**Output:** the **communications management plan** — stakeholder communication requirements; information to be communicated (language, format, content, detail); reason for distribution; timeframe and frequency; person responsible; person authorised to release confidential information; recipients; methods and technologies; resources (time and budget); escalation process; glossary; flow charts; constraints (legislation, regulation, technology).

## 12.7 Manage Communications *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** ensure timely and appropriate collection, creation, distribution, storage, retrieval, management, monitoring and disposition of project information.

- Uses: communication technology, methods, skills, PMIS, **project reporting** (status, progress, forecasts), interpersonal skills, meetings.
- Output: **project communications** (performance reports, deliverables status, schedule progress, costs incurred, presentations).
- In adaptive teams: **information radiators** (big visible charts, task boards, burn charts), daily stand-ups, sprint reviews, transparent backlogs, team chat channels, **osmotic communication** in co-located teams.

## 12.8 Monitor Communications *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** ensure the information needs of the project and its stakeholders are met. Check whether communications had the desired effect (Did people read and understand? Are stakeholders acting on it?). Techniques: stakeholder engagement assessment matrix, observation, surveys, feedback, meetings. Outputs: work performance information, change requests (e.g., to the communications plan).

> **Trap.** "Send more emails" is rarely the answer to a communication problem. Check **what** the stakeholder needs, **how** they prefer to receive it, and whether the communications plan should change.

## 12.9 Stakeholders in adaptive and hybrid projects

- The **product owner** represents stakeholders' interests and the business; stakeholders still participate directly in **sprint reviews**, backlog refinement and user research.
- **Frequent demonstrations** align expectations with reality better than documents.
- **Transparency** — anyone can see the backlog, the board and the burn charts.
- In hybrid projects, combine formal reporting for governance bodies with agile transparency for users and the team, and make sure **one consistent story** is told.

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 12](#ans-12) in Appendix B.*

- **B.** 28
- **C.** 38
- **D.** 66

- **B.** Keep informed
- **C.** Keep satisfied

- **A.** Power, urgency and legitimacy
- **B.** Urgency, budget and authority
- **C.** Impact, influence and interest
- **D.** Power, interest and influence

**12.4** The stakeholder engagement assessment matrix shows the IT security manager as "Resistant" now and "Supportive" as desired. What should the project manager do next?

- **A.** Escalate to the sponsor to override the manager
- **B.** Plan engagement actions to understand and address the manager's concerns
- **C.** Change the desired level to "Resistant" to close the gap
- **D.** Remove the IT security manager from the stakeholder register, since the manager is resistant

**12.5** A project manager needs to share a 300-page technical specification with 400 stakeholders worldwide who will consult it as needed. Which communication method is most appropriate?

- **A.** Interactive communication in a single meeting
- **B.** Push communication by sending the file to everyone by email
- **C.** Informal hallway conversations
- **D.** Pull communication via a shared repository

**12.6** An important decision about a scope trade-off must be made by several stakeholders who disagree. Which communication method is best?

- **A.** Interactive communication, such as a facilitated meeting
- **B.** Pull communication, such as a wiki page
- **C.** Push communication, such as a detailed memo sent to everyone involved
- **D.** A monthly newsletter

**12.7** Midway through a project, a regional manager the team had never heard of complains that the new process will affect her staff. What should the project manager do first?

- **A.** Submit a change request to remove her staff from scope
- **B.** Ignore the complaint because stakeholder identification ended in initiating
- **C.** Add her to the stakeholder register and analyse her interests
- **D.** Ask the sponsor to tell her the decision is final

**12.8** In the basic communication model, who is responsible for confirming that the message was understood correctly?

- **A.** The medium
- **B.** The project sponsor, who is accountable for all communication
- **C.** Only the receiver
- **D.** The sender, by checking for feedback

**12.9** Which two items would you expect in a communications management plan? (Choose two.)

- **A.** The contract's price adjustment formula
- **B.** The frequency and format of each communication
- **C.** The quality control measurements
- **D.** The person responsible for communicating each type of information
- **E.** The critical path of the schedule

**12.10** A stakeholder complains that she never reads the weekly status email because it is too long. What should the project manager do?

- **A.** Ask the sponsor to require her to read it
- **B.** Discuss her information needs and adjust the communication approach
- **C.** Send the weekly email twice a week instead, so that she is more likely to notice and read it
- **D.** Remove her from the distribution list

**12.11** In an agile team, which practice best supports transparent, low-effort communication of progress to anyone who walks by?

- **A.** A password-protected file on the PM's laptop
- **B.** Information radiators such as a visible task board and burndown chart
- **C.** Detailed minutes sent after each meeting
- **D.** A confidential monthly report sent only to the sponsor and the steering committee

**12.12** Which direction of influence describes communications with suppliers, regulators and the public?

- **A.** Upward
- **B.** Downward
- **C.** Sideward
- **D.** Outward

**12.13** Why should the stakeholder engagement plan sometimes have restricted distribution?

- **A.** It may contain sensitive assessments of stakeholders' attitudes
- **B.** It is the same document as the project charter and must be signed first by the sponsor
- **C.** Agile projects do not allow stakeholders to see plans
- **D.** It contains the project's bank account details

# Chapter 13 — Resources Performance Domain (including Procurement)

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- describe the five Resources processes and their key outputs;
- plan team and physical resources with RAMs/RACI charts, team charters and resource breakdown structures;
- acquire internal and external resources, including the main steps of conducting a procurement;
- lead the team (development, performance, recognition) — the detailed people skills are in Chapter 16;
- monitor and control physical resources and contracts, including claims and incentive-fee calculations.

## 13.1 What the Resources domain covers

The Resources domain addresses **identifying, acquiring, leading and managing the people, physical and virtual resources** needed to complete the project. It covers the team *and* equipment, materials, facilities, software and infrastructure, and — together with *Plan Sourcing Strategy* in Governance and Appendix X4 — the acquisition and control of externally sourced resources.

| Process | Focus area | Key outputs |
|---|---|---|
| **Plan Resource Management** | Planning | Resource management plan, team charter |
| **Estimate Resources** | Planning | Resource requirements, basis of estimates, resource breakdown structure |
| **Acquire Resources** | Executing | Physical resource assignments, project team assignments, resource calendars, (for external sourcing) selected sellers and agreements |
| **Lead the Team** | Executing | Team performance assessments, change requests, updates (formerly *Develop Team* and *Manage Team*) |
| **Monitor and Control Resourcing** | Monitoring and Controlling | Work performance information, change requests (physical/virtual resources, and contracts) |

**Desired outcomes:** the right resources are available at the right time and place; the team is high-performing, engaged and growing; resources are used efficiently and sustainably; suppliers deliver as agreed.

## 13.2 Plan Resource Management *(Planning)*

**Resource management plan** contents: identification of resources; how to acquire them; **roles and responsibilities** (role, authority, responsibility, competence); project organisation charts; project team resource management (how to define, staff, manage and release); training; team development methods; resource control; recognition plan.

### Data representation tools

| Tool | Description |
|---|---|
| **Hierarchical charts** — organisational breakdown structure (OBS), resource breakdown structure (RBS), WBS | Show reporting relationships, resource categories and types |
| **Responsibility assignment matrix (RAM)** | Grid linking work packages/activities to people or groups |
| **RACI chart** | A RAM with **R**esponsible (does the work), **A**ccountable (owns the outcome — **only one A per task**), **C**onsulted (two-way input), **I**nformed (one-way updates) |
| **Text-oriented formats** | Position descriptions, role-responsibility-authority forms |

### Team charter

A document created **by the team** that establishes values, agreements and operating guidelines: team values, communication guidelines, **decision-making criteria and process**, **conflict resolution process**, meeting guidelines, team agreements (ground rules), working hours across time zones. The team charter is most effective when the team writes it together and revisits it.

## 13.3 Estimate Resources *(Planning)*

Estimate team resources and the type and quantities of materials, equipment and supplies. Techniques: bottom-up, analogous and parametric estimating, alternatives analysis (in-house vs outsource, manual vs automated, different skill levels), PMIS. Outputs: **resource requirements**, **basis of estimates**, **resource breakdown structure** (hierarchy of resources by category — labour, material, equipment, supplies — and type).

## 13.4 Acquire Resources *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** obtain the team members, facilities, equipment, materials, supplies and other resources needed — from inside the organisation or from external sources.

### Internal acquisition

- **Pre-assignment** — team members selected in advance (e.g., named in the proposal or charter).
- **Negotiation** — with functional managers, other project managers, external organisations. The PM's influence is critical in matrix organisations.
- **Virtual teams** — expand the talent pool, reduce travel, enable people with mobility limitations or in different time zones; require strong communication planning and technology.
- **Multicriteria decision analysis** — weigh availability, cost, ability, experience, knowledge, skills, attitude, international factors.
- **Halo effect** — assuming someone is good at everything because they are good at one thing (e.g., promoting the best developer to PM). Avoid it in selection.
- If preferred resources are not available, use **alternative resources** (with lower competence or higher cost), document the impact and consider risk.

### External acquisition — conducting a procurement

When resources or work are sourced externally, the steps are typically:

1. **Advertise** and distribute **bid documents** (RFI/RFQ/RFP, procurement SOW) to prospective sellers.
2. Hold a **bidder conference** (pre-bid or vendor conference) so all prospective sellers receive the **same information** at the same time — fairness and transparency. Questions and answers are shared with all bidders.
3. **Receive proposals** and evaluate them against the **source selection criteria** using a **weighting system**, **screening** (must-meet criteria), independent estimates, and expert review — often by a proposal evaluation committee.
4. **Negotiate** — clarify structure, requirements and terms; the **procurement/contracts department** usually leads, and the PM supports.
5. **Award** the **agreement** (contract) — a mutually binding legal agreement that obligates the seller to provide the specified products/services and the buyer to pay.

**Typical contract components:** statement of work or deliverables; schedule and milestones; performance reporting; period of performance; roles and responsibilities; seller's place of performance; pricing and payment terms; inspection, quality and acceptance criteria; **warranty**; product support; **limitation of liability**; fees and **retainage** (amount withheld until completion); **penalties** and **liquidated damages** (pre-agreed compensation for specific breaches such as late delivery); **incentives**; insurance and performance bonds; subcontractor approvals; **change request handling**; **termination clause** and **alternative dispute resolution (ADR)** mechanisms; **force majeure** (unforeseeable circumstances that excuse non-performance); **indemnification**; confidentiality and **intellectual property** provisions.

> **Exam tip.** A contract is a **legal** document: changes to it must follow the **contract change control system** and usually require the authority of the procurement/contracts administrator. The PM cannot change a contract by informal agreement with the seller's project manager. Also, the **buyer's PM should not direct the seller's employees** as if they were their own staff — work through the contract and the seller's management.

## 13.5 Lead the Team *(Executing)*

This process merges the former *Develop Team* (improving competencies, interaction and environment) and *Manage Team* (tracking performance, providing feedback, resolving issues, managing changes). Key ideas — expanded in Chapter 16:

- **Team development** — training, team-building, recognition and rewards, co-location (war rooms, *tight matrix*) or effective virtual collaboration, communication technology, individual and team assessments (personality and style assessments, 360° feedback).
- **Stages of team development (Tuckman):** forming, storming, norming, performing, adjourning.
- **Motivation** — intrinsic and extrinsic, autonomy-mastery-purpose; recognition should be **timely**, **specific** and appropriate to the person and culture; win-lose (zero-sum) rewards that only a few can earn can hurt team cohesion.
- **Team performance assessments** — evaluate improvements in skills, competencies, team behaviour, turnover rate, cohesiveness.
- **Conflict management**, **decision-making**, **emotional intelligence**, **influencing**, **leadership**.
- **Self-organising teams** in adaptive contexts; the leader is a **servant leader** who removes impediments, shields the team, coaches and facilitates.
- **Performance problems** — address them **privately**, early, with specific feedback, focusing on behaviour and support; involve the functional manager when appropriate (it may affect their appraisal).

## 13.6 Monitor and Control Resourcing *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** ensure that the **physical and virtual resources** (equipment, materials, facilities, software, infrastructure) assigned and allocated are available as planned, monitor planned vs actual use, and take corrective action. It also covers monitoring **externally sourced** resources and contract performance. (Team performance itself is handled through *Lead the Team*.)

- Techniques: data analysis (alternatives, cost-benefit, performance reviews, trend analysis), problem solving, negotiation, influencing, PMIS.
- Physical resource issues: late deliveries, wrong quantities, defective materials, equipment failures, scarcity, price increases, environmental waste.
- **Just-in-time (JIT)** inventory reduces holding costs but requires reliable suppliers.

### Controlling contracts (procurement control)

- **Contract administration** — manage the relationship, monitor performance against the agreement, approve payments, manage changes.
- **Performance reviews** and **inspections/audits** — verify the seller's work and processes.
- **Earned value analysis** of seller performance; **trend analysis**.
- **Claims administration** — a **claim** (dispute, appeal) arises when buyer and seller cannot agree on compensation for a change or on whether a change occurred. Preferred resolution order: **negotiation** → **alternative dispute resolution (ADR)** such as **mediation** (a neutral third party helps the parties agree) or **arbitration** (a neutral third party decides; often binding) → **litigation** (courts) only as a last resort.
- **Records management** — maintain complete records of contract and procurement activities (they may be needed in disputes or audits).
- **Payment systems** — pay according to contract terms, upon verification of work.
- **Closing a contract** — see Chapter 7: verify deliverables accepted, settle claims, final payment, formal written notice of completion, archive records, lessons learned. A contract may also be terminated early **for cause** (breach) or **for convenience** (as allowed by the termination clause).

> **Trap.** If a seller is not performing, the first step is usually to **review the contract and the seller's performance data, then discuss with the seller** (through the formal channel) — not to terminate the contract or withhold all payments immediately.

## 13.7 Incentive contracts — calculations you may see

**Cost plus incentive fee (CPIF)** example: target cost $100,000; target fee $10,000; share ratio **80/20** (buyer/seller); actual cost **$90,000**.
- Underrun = $10,000; seller's share = 20% × 10,000 = $2,000.
- Fee = 10,000 + 2,000 = **$12,000**; total price paid = 90,000 + 12,000 = **$102,000**.
- If actual cost were $110,000: overrun $10,000; seller's share −$2,000; fee $8,000; total $118,000.

**Fixed price incentive fee (FPIF)** example: target cost $200,000; target profit $20,000 (target price $220,000); share ratio 70/30; **ceiling price $240,000**; actual cost $210,000.
- Overrun $10,000; seller absorbs 30% = $3,000; profit = 20,000 − 3,000 = $17,000; price = 210,000 + 17,000 = **$227,000** (below the ceiling, so payable).
- **Point of total assumption (PTA)** = ((ceiling price − target price) ÷ buyer's share ratio) + target cost = ((240,000 − 220,000) ÷ 0.70) + 200,000 ≈ **$228,571**. Above this cost, the seller bears **all** additional costs.

## 13.8 Resources in adaptive and hybrid projects

- Small (often 3–9 people), stable, **dedicated**, **cross-functional** teams; **T-shaped** people (deep in one skill, broad across others) and generalising specialists reduce bottlenecks.
- Teams are **self-organising**; work is pulled, not assigned.
- Co-location or strong virtual collaboration practices; **sustainable pace**.
- Vendors may join as part of blended teams under agile-friendly contracts (Chapter 7).
- In hybrid projects, physical resources for predictive components (heavy equipment, materials) are planned and controlled in the traditional way, while team capacity for adaptive components is managed through velocity and capacity planning.

# Chapter 14 — Risk Performance Domain

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- distinguish individual risks, overall project risk, issues and uncertainty;
- describe the six Risk processes and their outputs (risk management plan, risk register, risk report);
- analyse risks qualitatively and quantitatively (probability-impact, EMV, decision trees, Monte Carlo, sensitivity);
- select response strategies for threats and opportunities, including contingency and fallback plans;
- monitor risks and manage reserves in predictive and adaptive contexts.

## 14.1 Key concepts

| Term | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Risk** | An uncertain event or condition that, if it occurs, has a **positive or negative** effect on one or more objectives |
| **Threat** | A risk with a negative effect |
| **Opportunity** | A risk with a positive effect |
| **Individual project risk** | A specific uncertain event or condition |
| **Overall project risk** | The effect of uncertainty on the project **as a whole** — from all sources, including individual risks — representing the exposure of stakeholders to variations in outcome |
| **Issue** | A current condition or situation that is **already happening** and must be resolved (logged in the **issue log**) |
| **Risk appetite** | The degree of uncertainty an organisation or individual is willing to accept in anticipation of a reward |
| **Risk threshold** | The measure of acceptable variation around an objective (e.g., cost overrun > 5% is unacceptable) — reflects appetite |
| **Risk tolerance** | Older term close to threshold: the degree of risk that can be withstood |
| **Known-unknowns** | Identified risks → contingency reserve |
| **Unknown-unknowns** | Unidentified risks → management reserve |
| **Residual risk** | Risk remaining after responses are implemented |
| **Secondary risk** | A new risk that arises **as a direct result** of implementing a risk response |
| **Trigger condition** | An event or situation indicating a risk is about to occur |
| **Workaround** | An **unplanned** response to a risk that has occurred (or to an issue) — not planned in advance |
| **Contingency plan** | A planned response executed if a trigger occurs |
| **Fallback plan** | A plan B used if the contingency plan (primary response) is not effective |
| **Risk owner** | The person responsible for monitoring a risk and implementing its response |

Other forms of uncertainty the Guide asks you to recognise: **ambiguity** (unclear situation — use expert judgment, experiments, progressive elaboration), **complexity** (many interacting parts — use systems thinking, simulation, decoupling) and **volatility** (rapid, unpredictable change — use alternatives analysis, reserves). The acronym **VUCA** (volatility, uncertainty, complexity, ambiguity) summarises them. **Resilience** — the ability to absorb and recover from impacts — is built through reserves, flexibility, redundancy and learning.

## 14.2 Plan Risk Management *(Planning)*

**Risk management plan** contents: risk strategy; methodology; roles and responsibilities; funding (reserves); timing (when and how often risk activities happen); **risk categories** (often a **risk breakdown structure, RBS** — e.g., technical, management, commercial, external); **stakeholder risk appetite**; **definitions of risk probability and impact** (scales); **probability and impact matrix** (with rules for high/medium/low); reporting formats; tracking.

## 14.3 Identify Risks *(Planning)*

**Purpose:** identify individual risks and sources of overall project risk, and document their characteristics. Identification is **iterative** — everyone on the team should be encouraged to identify risks at any time.

**Techniques:** expert judgment; **brainstorming**; **checklists** (from historical information — useful but never exhaustive); **interviews**; **root-cause analysis**; **assumption and constraint analysis** (inaccurate, unstable or incomplete assumptions are risks); **SWOT analysis** (strengths, weaknesses, opportunities, threats); **document analysis**; **prompt lists** — e.g., **PESTLE** (political, economic, social, technological, legal, environmental), **TECOP** (technical, environmental, commercial, operational, political), **VUCA**; facilitation; meetings (risk workshops); **Delphi technique** (anonymous expert rounds to reach consensus, reducing bias).

**Risk statement (metalanguage):** *"Because of <cause>, <uncertain event> may occur, which would lead to <effect>."* Example: "Because the vendor is a new supplier, delivery of the servers may be late, which would delay testing by three weeks."

**Outputs:** the **risk register** (list of identified risks, potential risk owners, potential responses, later: causes, categories, probability, impact, priority, triggers, responses, residual and secondary risks) and the **risk report** (sources of overall project risk and summary information on individual risks — updated throughout).

## 14.4 Perform Risk Analysis *(Planning)*

In the Eighth Edition this single process covers both **qualitative** and **quantitative** analysis.

### Qualitative analysis (always performed; fast, subjective)

- **Probability and impact assessment** on defined scales (e.g., 1–5 or very low to very high).
- **Probability and impact matrix** — combine both to rank risks (high, medium, low).
- **Assessment of other risk parameters:** **urgency** (how soon a response is needed), **proximity** (how soon the risk could affect objectives), **dormancy** (time between occurrence and discovery of its impact), **manageability**, **controllability**, **detectability**, **connectivity** (relationships with other risks), **strategic impact**, **propinquity** (how much the risk matters to stakeholders).
- **Risk data quality assessment** — how accurate and reliable is the data about the risk?
- **Risk categorisation** — by source (RBS), affected WBS element, phase — reveals common root causes.
- **Data representation** — heat maps, hierarchical charts (bubble charts showing three parameters).
- Output: updated risk register (priorities, owners), assumption log, issue log, risk report.

### Quantitative analysis (only when justified — high-value, complex, high-risk projects)

| Technique | Use |
|---|---|
| **Monte Carlo simulation** | Run thousands of iterations of the schedule or cost model using probability distributions → probability of meeting targets (S-curve; e.g., "P80 completion date is 15 March") |
| **Sensitivity analysis (tornado diagram)** | Which individual risks or uncertain variables have the **most potential impact** on outcomes (longest bar at the top) |
| **Decision tree analysis** with **expected monetary value (EMV)** | Choose the best alternative when outcomes are uncertain |
| **Influence diagrams** | Graphical representation of decision situations, uncertainties and outcomes |
| **Criticality analysis** | How often an activity appears on the critical path across simulation iterations |

**Expected monetary value (EMV)** = probability × impact. Opportunities have **positive** values, threats **negative**. The EMV of a set of risks is the sum.

*Example:* Risk A: 30% chance of a $50,000 delay penalty → EMV = −$15,000. Opportunity B: 20% chance of a $40,000 early-completion bonus → EMV = +$8,000. Net EMV = **−$7,000** → a contingency reserve of about $7,000 could be justified for these two items.

**Decision tree example:** Option 1 — build in-house: cost $200,000; 40% chance of an extra $60,000 problem. Expected cost = 200,000 + (0.4 × 60,000) = **$224,000**. Option 2 — buy: cost $230,000; 10% chance of an extra $20,000. Expected cost = 230,000 + 2,000 = **$232,000**. → Choose **build** (lower expected cost).

## 14.5 Plan Risk Responses *(Planning)*

### Strategies for threats

| Strategy | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Escalate** | The threat is **outside the project's scope** or the PM's authority; pass it to the appropriate owner (program, portfolio, other part of the organisation). Once escalated, it is no longer monitored by the project (though it may be noted) | A risk to the company's market position spotted by the project |
| **Avoid** | Eliminate the threat or protect the project from its impact (change the plan, extend the schedule, reduce scope, use a proven approach) | Drop an unproven technology |
| **Transfer** | Shift ownership and impact to a **third party** (insurance, warranties, performance bonds, fixed-price contracts, outsourcing). Usually involves paying a premium. The risk is not eliminated | Buy insurance |
| **Mitigate** | **Reduce probability and/or impact** to an acceptable level | Extra testing, prototypes, redundant suppliers |
| **Accept** | Acknowledge the threat without proactive action. **Active acceptance** = establish a contingency reserve (time, money, resources); **passive acceptance** = no action other than periodic review | Accept small weather delays with a schedule reserve |

### Strategies for opportunities

| Strategy | Description | Example |
|---|---|---|
| **Escalate** | Opportunity outside the project's scope or authority → pass to the right owner | Savings for the whole company |
| **Exploit** | Make sure the opportunity **definitely happens** (raise probability to 100%) | Assign the most talented resources to finish early and earn a bonus |
| **Share** | Allocate ownership to a third party best able to capture the benefit (joint ventures, partnerships, teaming agreements) | Partner with a firm that has a complementary technology |
| **Enhance** | **Increase probability and/or impact** | Add resources to an activity to finish early |
| **Accept** | Take advantage if it occurs, without pursuing it actively | — |

### Overall project risk responses

Avoid (e.g., cancel or re-scope), exploit, transfer/share, mitigate/enhance, accept (active: overall contingency reserve).

### Other response elements

- **Contingent response strategies** — designed for use only if certain events occur (with **triggers**).
- **Fallback plans** — if the primary response fails.
- **Risk owner** for each risk, and **risk action owners** for specific actions.
- **Contingency reserves** (time and cost) calculated with EMV, simulation or percentages.
- **Secondary risks** and **residual risks** documented.
- **Cost-benefit analysis** of responses — a response should not cost more than the risk it addresses (unless required by compliance or values).

## 14.6 Implement Risk Responses *(Executing)*

**Purpose:** implement the agreed risk response plans. A common failure is that responses are planned but **never executed** — risk owners must act. Outputs: change requests, updates to the risk register and risk report, issue log, lessons learned.

## 14.7 Monitor Risks *(Monitoring and Controlling)*

**Purpose:** monitor implementation of responses, track identified risks, identify new risks, evaluate risk process effectiveness.

- **Risk reassessment** — regularly review existing risks, close outdated ones, identify new ones.
- **Risk audits** — examine the effectiveness of the risk management **process** and of responses.
- **Reserve analysis** — compare remaining reserves with remaining risk; release reserves when risks pass.
- **Technical performance analysis** — compare technical achievements with the plan (e.g., weight, transaction speed).
- **Trend and variance analysis**, **meetings** (risk reviews).
- When a **risk occurs**, implement the planned response; it becomes an **issue** to manage. If no response was planned, apply a **workaround** and then update the risk register and lessons learned.

> **Exam tip.** A trigger for an identified risk occurs → **implement the contingency plan** defined in the risk register (involving the risk owner). An unidentified problem appears → it is an **issue**: manage it (workaround, issue log, possibly a change request) and then **identify any new risks**.

## 14.8 Risk in adaptive and hybrid projects

- **Short iterations, frequent delivery and feedback** reduce risk by validating assumptions early.
- **Risk-adjusted backlog** — risk responses become backlog items that the product owner prioritises alongside features; high-risk items are often tackled early.
- **Spikes** — timeboxed research or experiments to reduce technical or design uncertainty.
- **Daily stand-ups and retrospectives** surface risks and impediments continuously; information radiators make them visible.
- **Cross-functional teams** and **sustainable pace** reduce people-related risks.
- **Incremental funding** limits financial exposure.
- In hybrid projects, maintain an integrated risk register across predictive and adaptive components, and watch the **interfaces** between them.


## Chapter 14 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 14](#ans-14) in Appendix B.*

**14.1** A risk has a 25% probability of causing a $80,000 loss. An opportunity has a 40% probability of producing a $30,000 saving. What is the combined expected monetary value?

- **A.** +$12,000
- **B.** −$32,000
- **C.** −$20,000
- **D.** −$8,000

**14.2** The project team buys insurance against fire damage to the construction site. Which risk response strategy is this?

- **A.** Transfer
- **B.** Mitigate
- **C.** Accept
- **D.** Avoid

**14.3** A team assigns its most experienced engineers to a module to make sure it is completed early, guaranteeing a client bonus. Which opportunity strategy is this?

- **A.** Enhance
- **B.** Exploit
- **C.** Accept
- **D.** Share

**14.4** A risk trigger for a previously identified risk has occurred. What should the project manager do?

- **A.** Close the risk in the register
- **B.** Begin identifying risks for the first time
- **C.** Perform a new Monte Carlo simulation of the whole project before doing anything else
- **D.** Ensure the risk owner implements the planned contingency response

**14.5** An unexpected problem occurs that was never identified as a risk. The team applies an unplanned fix to keep working. What is this fix called?

- **A.** Risk mitigation
- **B.** A fallback plan
- **C.** A workaround
- **D.** A contingency plan

**14.6** Which technique would best show which uncertain variables have the greatest effect on the project's total cost?

- **A.** Sensitivity analysis displayed as a tornado diagram
- **B.** A probability and impact matrix of the individual risks
- **C.** Risk breakdown structure
- **D.** Checklist analysis

**14.7** The project team identifies a risk that a new government policy could reduce the organisation's overall market share — far beyond the project's objectives. What should the project manager do?

- **A.** Accept the risk passively and ignore it
- **B.** Transfer it by buying insurance
- **C.** Escalate the risk to the appropriate owner in the organisation
- **D.** Mitigate the risk using the project's own contingency reserve and resources

**14.8** After implementing a response to reduce the chance of a server failure, a small chance of failure remains. What is this remaining risk called?

- **A.** Secondary risk
- **B.** Residual risk
- **C.** Trigger
- **D.** Issue

**14.9** Option X has an initial cost of $100,000 and a 30% chance of an additional $50,000 cost. Option Y has an initial cost of $110,000 and a 20% chance of an additional $20,000 cost. Based on expected cost, which option should be chosen?

- **A.** Option X, expected cost $115,000
- **B.** Option X, expected cost $100,000
- **C.** Option Y, expected cost $130,000
- **D.** Option Y, expected cost $114,000

**14.10** Which two practices help manage risk in adaptive projects? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Postponing all risk discussions to the final retrospective
- **B.** Including risk response activities in the backlog and prioritising them with features
- **C.** Eliminating demonstrations to save time
- **D.** Using timeboxed spikes to reduce technical uncertainty
- **E.** Freezing the backlog for the whole release

**14.11** What is the main purpose of a risk audit?

- **A.** To calculate the project's critical path
- **B.** To evaluate how effective the risk process and responses are
- **C.** To transfer risks to the auditor
- **D.** To identify every possible risk on the project for the first time, with the auditors

**14.12** Which statement best describes risk appetite and risk threshold?

- **A.** Appetite is the uncertainty accepted for reward; thresholds are measurable limits reflecting it
- **B.** They are identical terms
- **C.** Thresholds are set by the team alone without any stakeholder input, and the organisation's appetite is irrelevant
- **D.** Appetite applies only to opportunities and thresholds only to threats

**14.13** A Monte Carlo simulation shows a 50% probability of finishing by 1 May and an 85% probability of finishing by 20 May. The sponsor wants high confidence. What should the project manager communicate?

- **A.** The schedule must be crashed or fast-tracked until the 1 May date has a confidence level of 100%
- **B.** The simulation is irrelevant because it is probabilistic
- **C.** The project will certainly finish on 1 May
- **D.** A target near 20 May gives 85% confidence; 1 May leaves a 50% chance of being late

**14.14** In the Eighth Edition, which process covers both qualitative and quantitative risk analysis?

- **A.** Monitor Risks
- **B.** Plan Risk Management
- **C.** Perform Risk Analysis
- **D.** Identify Risks

# Chapter 15 — Agile and Hybrid Delivery in Depth

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- state the four values and twelve principles of the Agile Manifesto and apply them to scenarios;
- explain Scrum roles, events, artifacts and commitments, and the role of a servant leader;
- apply Kanban, Lean and XP practices;
- recognise scaling frameworks and their key ideas;
- estimate, plan, measure and contract in agile and hybrid environments;
- choose the "agile answer" in exam scenarios.

About 60% of PMP questions are agile or hybrid. This chapter collects the concepts most frequently tested.

## 15.1 The Agile Manifesto (2001)

**Four values** — *we value the items on the left more than the items on the right* (the right still has value):

1. **Individuals and interactions** over processes and tools
2. **Working software** (working product) over comprehensive documentation
3. **Customer collaboration** over contract negotiation
4. **Responding to change** over following a plan

**Twelve principles** (paraphrased):

1. Highest priority: satisfy the customer through **early and continuous delivery** of valuable software.
2. **Welcome changing requirements**, even late; harness change for the customer's competitive advantage.
3. **Deliver working software frequently**, from a couple of weeks to a couple of months, preferring the shorter timescale.
4. Business people and developers **work together daily**.
5. Build projects around **motivated individuals**; give them the environment and support they need and **trust** them.
6. **Face-to-face conversation** is the most efficient and effective method of conveying information.
7. **Working software is the primary measure of progress.**
8. Agile processes promote **sustainable development** — a constant pace indefinitely.
9. Continuous attention to **technical excellence and good design** enhances agility.
10. **Simplicity** — the art of maximising the amount of work *not* done — is essential.
11. The best architectures, requirements and designs emerge from **self-organising teams**.
12. At regular intervals the team **reflects** on how to become more effective and adjusts its behaviour.

**Agile mindset:** being agile (values and principles) matters more than doing agile (practices). Agile is an umbrella term for many approaches; **Lean** thinking is a superset that includes agile and Kanban.

## 15.2 Scrum

Scrum is a lightweight framework for developing complex products, based on **empiricism** (transparency, inspection, adaptation) and **lean thinking**. Scrum values: **commitment, focus, openness, respect, courage**.

### Scrum team (accountabilities)

| Role | Accountabilities |
|---|---|
| **Product owner (PO)** | Maximise the value of the product; develop and communicate the **product goal**; create, **order** and clearly communicate **product backlog** items; **accept** or reject work; the single voice of the stakeholders for the team. One person, not a committee |
| **Scrum master (SM)** | **Servant leader** and coach: helps everyone understand Scrum theory and practice; facilitates events as needed; **removes impediments**; shields the team from interruptions; helps the PO with backlog techniques; helps the organisation adopt Scrum |
| **Developers** | Everyone who creates the increment (any discipline); **self-managing**: decide **how** to do the work; create the **sprint backlog**; hold each other accountable; adhere to the **definition of done** |

A Scrum team is typically **10 or fewer** people, cross-functional, with no sub-teams or hierarchies.

### Events (all timeboxed)

| Event | Timebox (for a one-month sprint; shorter for shorter sprints) | Purpose |
|---|---|---|
| **Sprint** | 1 month or less (often 2 weeks); a container for all events | Create a valuable, useful "done" increment. No changes that endanger the **sprint goal**; quality does not decrease; scope may be clarified and renegotiated with the PO |
| **Sprint planning** | Max 8 hours | *Why* (sprint goal), *what* (selected items), *how* (plan) — produces the sprint backlog |
| **Daily scrum** | 15 minutes, same time and place | Developers inspect progress toward the sprint goal and adapt the plan. **Not** a status meeting for the PM. Detailed problem-solving happens after |
| **Sprint review** | Max 4 hours | Inspect the increment with **stakeholders**, gather feedback, adapt the product backlog — a working session, not just a demo |
| **Sprint retrospective** | Max 3 hours | Inspect how the last sprint went (people, interactions, processes, tools, DoD) and plan **improvements**, which may be added to the next sprint backlog |

**Sprint cancellation:** only the **product owner** can cancel a sprint, typically when the sprint goal becomes obsolete.

**Backlog refinement** is an ongoing activity (often ~10% of capacity) to break down, clarify, estimate and order items.

### Artifacts and their commitments

| Artifact | Commitment | Description |
|---|---|---|
| **Product backlog** | **Product goal** | Emergent, ordered list of what is needed to improve the product |
| **Sprint backlog** | **Sprint goal** | Selected items + the plan for delivering them; owned by developers |
| **Increment** | **Definition of done** | A concrete stepping stone toward the product goal; must be usable; multiple increments may be created in a sprint |

## 15.3 Kanban

Kanban (Japanese: "visual signal") is a method for managing flow of work. Core practices:

1. **Visualise the workflow** (Kanban board with columns such as To do / In progress / Review / Done).
2. **Limit work in progress (WIP)** — WIP limits per column create a **pull system** and expose bottlenecks.
3. **Manage flow** — measure and improve **lead time**, **cycle time** and **throughput** (cumulative flow diagrams).
4. **Make policies explicit** (e.g., definition of done per column).
5. **Implement feedback loops** (replenishment meetings, reviews).
6. **Improve collaboratively, evolve experimentally.**

Kanban has **no prescribed roles or iterations**; it starts with what you do now and evolves. It suits support/operations work with continuous incoming requests. **Scrumban** combines Scrum events with Kanban flow and WIP limits.

## 15.4 Lean

Lean thinking (from the Toyota Production System) focuses on **maximising customer value while minimising waste**. Principles: identify value, map the **value stream**, create **flow**, establish **pull**, pursue **perfection** (continuous improvement).

**Seven wastes in software development** (Poppendieck): partially done work, extra features (gold plating), relearning, handoffs, task switching, delays, defects. (Manufacturing equivalents: inventory, overproduction, extra processing, transportation, motion, waiting, defects — plus unused talent.)

**Value stream mapping** — visualise every step from request to delivery, distinguishing value-adding time from waiting time, to eliminate delays and waste.

## 15.5 Extreme Programming (XP)

XP focuses on technical excellence and frequent releases. Practices: **pair programming**, **test-driven development (TDD)** — write the test first (red–green–refactor), **continuous integration**, **refactoring**, **simple design**, **collective code ownership**, **coding standards**, **small releases**, **sustainable pace** (40-hour week), **whole team** (customer on-site), **planning game**, **system metaphor**. XP roles include coach, customer, programmer, tracker, tester. XP values: communication, simplicity, feedback, courage, respect.

## 15.6 Other approaches and scaling frameworks

| Approach | Key ideas |
|---|---|
| **Feature-driven development (FDD)** | Develop an overall model, build a features list, plan/design/build **by feature**; chief programmer, class owners |
| **DSDM** (Dynamic Systems Development Method) | Fixes **time, cost and quality**; scope varies (**MoSCoW**); eight principles; strong governance — popular for agile with corporate rigor |
| **Crystal** | A family of methodologies sized by team size and criticality (Clear, Yellow, Orange, Red…); emphasises people and communication |
| **Disciplined Agile (DA)** | PMI's toolkit for choosing your "way of working" (WoW); context-sensitive; principles such as *delight customers*, *be awesome*, *context counts*, *choice is good*, *optimise flow*, *enterprise awareness* |
| **SAFe® (Scaled Agile Framework)** | Organises many teams into **Agile Release Trains (ARTs)** of 50–125 people; **program increment (PI) planning** (typically every 8–12 weeks); roles such as Release Train Engineer; portfolio, large solution and essential configurations |
| **Scrum of Scrums** | Representatives of each team meet to coordinate dependencies and impediments across teams |
| **LeSS (Large-Scale Scrum)** | Many teams, **one product owner and one product backlog**; minimal extra roles |
| **Nexus** | Scrum.org's framework for 3–9 Scrum teams with a Nexus integration team |
| **Enterprise Scrum / Scrum@Scale** | Scaling Scrum principles to the enterprise |

## 15.7 Estimating and planning in agile

- **Relative estimating** with **story points** (size, complexity, uncertainty) — faster and more consistent than absolute hours.
- **Planning poker** (wideband Delphi): each estimator reveals a card simultaneously; discuss the highest and lowest; re-vote until convergence — avoids anchoring.
- **Affinity estimating** and **T-shirt sizing** for many items or early, coarse estimates.
- **Ideal days**, **#NoEstimates** (count items, use throughput).
- **Velocity** = points completed per iteration (only "done" items count); used to forecast; **not** a performance target.
- **Capacity planning** — adjust for holidays and availability.
- **Levels of planning (the planning onion)**: strategy → portfolio → product (vision, roadmap) → release → iteration → day.
- **Iteration zero / sprint zero** — set-up work (environment, architecture runway, team formation). Use with care: it should still deliver some value.
- **Spike** — a timeboxed research activity to answer a question or reduce risk; an **architectural spike** explores technical approach.

## 15.8 Agile metrics and visual management

| Metric / chart | Use | Caution |
|---|---|---|
| **Velocity** | Forecasting for the team | Not for comparing teams or evaluating individuals |
| **Burndown** | Remaining work in the sprint/release | Hides scope changes |
| **Burnup** | Completed work vs total scope | Shows scope growth |
| **Cumulative flow diagram** | Flow, WIP and bottlenecks | — |
| **Lead time / cycle time / throughput** | Predictability and speed | Reduce WIP to improve |
| **Escaped defects**, **technical debt** | Quality | — |
| **Business value delivered**, **customer satisfaction (NPS)**, **feature usage** | Outcomes | The most important measures |
| **Team happiness / health checks** | Sustainability | — |

**Information radiators** (big visible charts) make these transparent. **Earned value can be adapted** to agile by measuring planned vs completed story points against release budgets.

## 15.9 Servant leadership and the agile project manager

Servant leaders put the needs of the team first. Their behaviours:

- **Remove impediments** (or empower the team to remove them);
- **Shield the team** from interruptions and diversions;
- **Facilitate** collaboration, events and decisions — rather than direct;
- **Coach and mentor**; grow the team's capability; encourage self-organisation;
- **Promote transparency** and **psychological safety**;
- **Communicate the vision** and keep the team focused on value.

The project manager's role in agile environments may be to support the team, coordinate across teams and with the wider organisation, manage budgets and contracts, handle governance, and act as a servant leader — but **not** to assign tasks or make the product owner's decisions.

## 15.10 Agile contracts

Recap from Chapter 7: **fixed-price increments**, **not-to-exceed T&M**, **graduated fixed price**, **early cancellation option** (customer can stop when enough value is delivered, paying a fee), **dynamic scope option** (swap features of equal size), **team augmentation**, **multi-tiered structure** (master service agreement + statements of work for each release), **capacity-based** contracts. The agile value *customer collaboration over contract negotiation* means the contract should enable collaboration, not replace it.

## 15.11 Hybrid delivery — making it work

- Agree on **which parts** of the project use which approach, and why (tailoring).
- Integrate plans: **milestones** from the predictive plan linked to **releases** from the adaptive plan.
- Synchronise at **integration points** (e.g., hardware ready for software integration in sprint 6).
- Use **consistent governance and reporting** (e.g., a combined dashboard: milestone status + burnup + risks).
- Manage **dependencies** between components explicitly (dependency boards, Scrum of Scrums).
- Educate stakeholders about the differences (e.g., why the adaptive scope is flexible while the date is fixed).

## 15.12 The "agile answer" — common exam patterns

| Scenario | Usually the best answer |
|---|---|
| The team is in conflict over a technical approach | Let the team discuss and decide; facilitate if needed |
| A stakeholder asks a developer directly for a new feature | Redirect the request to the **product owner** for the backlog |
| The PO is not available to answer questions | Coach the PO on the importance of availability; agree on time slots; escalate to management only if it persists |
| A team member keeps talking about solutions in the daily scrum | Ask to take the discussion offline after the stand-up |
| Velocity dropped | Explore causes with the team in the **retrospective**; don't push for more points |
| Management wants the team to commit to more work | Explain capacity and velocity; protect sustainable pace; negotiate scope with the PO |
| Defects escaping to production | Strengthen the **definition of done**, automation and TDD; discuss in the retrospective |
| New team just formed | Facilitate a **team charter**/working agreements; coach Scrum practices |
| The team wants to change its process | Support experiments agreed in the retrospective |
| The organisation demands detailed upfront plans for an agile project | Educate on adaptive planning; provide roadmap/release plans and transparency; tailor reporting |


## Chapter 15 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 15](#ans-15) in Appendix B.*

**15.1** Which statement reflects the Agile Manifesto value "Working software over comprehensive documentation"?

- **A.** Documentation is forbidden in agile projects
- **B.** Working software is optional, as long as the documentation is complete and approved
- **C.** Only the product owner writes documentation
- **D.** Documentation still has value, but delivering a working product is valued more

**15.2** Who is accountable for ordering the product backlog and maximising the value of the product in Scrum?

- **A.** The scrum master
- **B.** The developers
- **C.** The project manager
- **D.** The product owner

**15.3** During the daily scrum, two developers start a detailed technical debate. What should the scrum master do?

- **A.** Extend the daily scrum until the technical debate has been fully resolved by the two developers
- **B.** Suggest they continue after the daily scrum so the event stays focused
- **C.** Decide the technical approach for them
- **D.** Cancel the daily scrum for the rest of the sprint

**15.4** Who can cancel a sprint in Scrum?

- **A.** The product owner
- **B.** The scrum master
- **C.** Any developer
- **D.** The sponsor, at any time without the product owner

**15.5** In Kanban, what is the main purpose of a work-in-progress (WIP) limit?

- **A.** To ensure that every team member is always busy with at least one work item
- **B.** To fix the length of each iteration
- **C.** To create a pull system that improves flow and exposes bottlenecks
- **D.** To estimate story points

**15.6** Which XP practice involves writing an automated test before writing the code that makes it pass?

- **A.** Test-driven development
- **B.** Refactoring
- **C.** Pair programming
- **D.** Continuous integration

**15.7** A large organisation coordinates eight agile teams working on one product with one product owner and one product backlog, adding as few extra roles as possible. Which framework does this describe?

- **A.** Kanban
- **B.** LeSS
- **C.** Crystal Clear
- **D.** Feature-driven development

**15.8** Which two behaviours are typical of a servant leader? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Removing impediments that block the team
- **B.** Reporting individual velocity to managers
- **C.** Shielding the team from interruptions so they can focus
- **D.** Assigning tasks to each developer every morning
- **E.** Deciding the technical design for the team

**15.9** Which Lean waste is created when developers frequently switch between several projects at once?

- **A.** Task switching
- **B.** Handoffs
- **C.** Relearning
- **D.** Extra features

**15.10** In DSDM, which elements are typically fixed while scope varies?

- **A.** Scope and cost
- **B.** Scope and time
- **C.** Time, cost and quality
- **D.** Scope, quality and team

**15.11** The sprint review is best described as:

- **A.** A status meeting for the project manager
- **B.** A working session where stakeholders inspect the increment and adapt the backlog
- **C.** The formal signing of the contract
- **D.** A meeting where the team reflects on its process and agrees improvements for the next sprint

**15.12** During planning poker, estimates for a story range from 2 to 13 points. What should happen next?

- **A.** Take the average of all estimates
- **B.** Use the highest estimate to be safe
- **C.** The scrum master chooses the final estimate, since the scrum master facilitates the session
- **D.** The lowest and highest estimators explain their reasoning, then the team re-estimates

**15.13** In a hybrid project, the hardware is delivered predictively and the software in sprints. Which practice best integrates the two?

- **A.** Forcing the software team to use a detailed Gantt chart for every task, so that both parts look the same
- **B.** Running the two parts with no coordination
- **C.** Aligning release plans with hardware milestones at defined integration points
- **D.** Delaying all software work until the hardware is complete

**15.14** What is the purpose of a spike in agile development?

- **A.** A defect found in production
- **B.** A short meeting held to celebrate a successful release with the stakeholders
- **C.** A timeboxed investigation to reduce uncertainty before committing
- **D.** A sudden increase in velocity
