# Chapter 16 — Leading People: Vision, Teams, Conflict, Motivation, Influence and Ethics

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- build a common vision and team agreements;
- choose a leadership style and source of power suited to the situation;
- apply motivation theories and team development models;
- select a conflict resolution approach based on the situation;
- use emotional intelligence, negotiation, decision-making and coaching techniques;
- apply the PMI Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct to dilemmas.

The People domain is 33% of the exam. Many of its questions have no "process" answer — they test judgment about human behaviour.

## 16.1 Developing a common vision (People task 1)

A shared vision aligns the team and stakeholders on **why** the project exists and **what success looks like**. Tools:

- **Project/product vision statement** — short, inspiring, memorable.
- **Elevator pitch** — *For* (target customer) *who* (need), *the* (product) *is a* (category) *that* (key benefit). *Unlike* (alternative), *our product* (differentiator).
- **Product box** — the team designs the "box" the product would be sold in, forcing focus on key benefits.
- **Project charter / team charter** — purpose, objectives, success criteria, values and working agreements.
- **Chartering workshops / inception** — collaborative sessions with stakeholders and team.
- **Roadmaps** — show how the vision will be reached over time.
- **Revisit the vision** regularly (sprint reviews, phase gates), especially when the team or context changes.

> **Exam tip.** If the team members disagree about priorities or "don't know why we are doing this", the best answer usually brings people back to the **vision, objectives and business value** — often by facilitating a session with the product owner or sponsor.

## 16.2 Leadership styles

| Style | Description | When useful |
|---|---|---|
| **Servant leadership** | Serves the team first: removes impediments, coaches, facilitates, empowers | Default for agile teams and knowledge work |
| **Transformational** | Inspires through vision, idealised attributes, individual consideration, intellectual stimulation | Change initiatives, motivating innovation |
| **Transactional** | Focuses on goals, feedback and rewards for performance (management by exception) | Routine work, clear targets |
| **Charismatic** | Inspires through energy and conviction | Rallying people; risk of dependence on the leader |
| **Interactional** | Combination of transactional, transformational and charismatic | Many situations |
| **Laissez-faire** | Hands-off; team makes decisions | Highly skilled, self-directed teams |
| **Directive / autocratic / command-and-control** | Leader decides and directs | Crises, safety emergencies, inexperienced teams in urgent situations |
| **Participative / democratic** | Involves the team in decisions | Building buy-in |
| **Situational leadership** (Hersey & Blanchard) | Adapt style to follower **competence** and **commitment** | Always — matching style to readiness |

**Situational Leadership® II** (Blanchard) maps four styles to four development levels:

| Development level | Typical state | Style |
|---|---|---|
| D1 — low competence, high commitment ("enthusiastic beginner") | New, eager | **S1 Directing** — high direction, low support |
| D2 — some competence, low commitment ("disillusioned learner") | Struggling | **S2 Coaching** — high direction, high support |
| D3 — moderate-high competence, variable commitment ("capable but cautious") | Able but hesitant | **S3 Supporting** — low direction, high support |
| D4 — high competence, high commitment ("self-reliant achiever") | Expert | **S4 Delegating** — low direction, low support |

**Leadership vs management:** management focuses on processes, systems, control and short-term results ("doing things right"); leadership focuses on people, vision, inspiration and change ("doing the right things"). Project managers need both.

## 16.3 Sources of power

| Power | Based on |
|---|---|
| **Formal / legitimate / positional** | The PM's assigned position (charter) |
| **Reward** | Ability to give rewards |
| **Penalty / coercive** | Ability to impose negative consequences — **least effective** in the long term |
| **Expert** | Knowledge, skills, experience — **among the most effective** |
| **Referent** | Respect, admiration, credibility — people want to follow you — **among the most effective** |
| **Informational** | Control over gathering or distribution of information |
| **Situational** | Gained because of unique circumstances (e.g., a crisis) |
| **Relational** | Networks, connections and alliances |
| **Personal / charismatic** | Charm, attraction |
| **Ingratiating** | Flattery or other common ground (manipulative — avoid) |
| **Guilt-based / persuasive / avoiding** | Other forms described in PMI materials |

> **Exam tip.** The PM's most effective forms of power are generally **expert** and **referent**; the least effective is **coercive/penalty**. In matrix organisations the PM has limited formal power, so influence, negotiation and relationship-building matter more.

## 16.4 Motivation theories

| Theory | Key idea | Practical meaning |
|---|---|---|
| **Maslow's hierarchy of needs** | Physiological → safety → social/belonging → esteem → self-actualisation. Lower needs must be met before higher ones motivate | A worried employee (job security) won't be motivated by an award ceremony |
| **Herzberg's two-factor theory** | **Hygiene factors** (salary, working conditions, policies, job security, supervision) prevent dissatisfaction but **do not motivate**; **motivators** (achievement, recognition, responsibility, growth, the work itself) create satisfaction | Fix hygiene problems, then use motivators |
| **McGregor's Theory X and Y** | X: people dislike work, must be controlled (micromanagement). Y: people are self-motivated and seek responsibility (empowerment) | Agile assumes Theory Y |
| **Ouchi's Theory Z** | Long-term employment, collective decision-making, concern for employee well-being, loyalty | Japanese management style |
| **McClelland's achievement (acquired needs) theory** | Needs for **achievement**, **affiliation** and **power** differ by person | Give achievers challenging goals, affiliators teamwork, power-seekers leadership opportunities |
| **Vroom's expectancy theory** | Motivation = **expectancy** (effort → performance) × **instrumentality** (performance → reward) × **valence** (value of reward) | Rewards must be achievable and valued |
| **Daniel Pink — Drive** | Intrinsic motivation from **autonomy, mastery and purpose**; extrinsic rewards work for simple tasks but can hurt creative work | Empower teams, support learning, connect work to purpose |
| **Equity theory** | People compare their input/outcome ratio with others' | Perceived unfairness demotivates |

**Recognition and rewards:** timely, specific, sincere, appropriate to the person and culture (some people dislike public praise). Avoid **win-lose** rewards that only one person can win when you want teamwork.

## 16.5 Team development

### Tuckman's ladder

1. **Forming** — team meets, learns about the project and roles; people are polite, independent, unsure. *Leader:* directive; clarify goals, roles, ground rules.
2. **Storming** — conflicts emerge over approaches and roles; if not handled constructively, the team can regress. *Leader:* coach, facilitate conflict resolution.
3. **Norming** — team adjusts behaviours, develops trust and working habits. *Leader:* support, facilitate.
4. **Performing** — high-functioning, interdependent, self-managing. *Leader:* delegate, remove impediments.
5. **Adjourning** — work completed, team released (also called *mourning*). *Leader:* recognise, celebrate, capture lessons, support transition.

Teams can move back to an earlier stage when membership or circumstances change.

### Drexler/Sibbet team performance model

Seven stages: **orientation** (why am I here?), **trust building** (who are you?), **goal clarification** (what are we doing?), **commitment** (how will we do it?), **implementation** (who does what, when, where?), **high performance** (wow!), **renewal** (why continue?).

### Building high-performing teams

- Clear vision and objectives, **shared ownership**, **trust**, **open communication**, **constructive disagreement**, **psychological safety**, **empowerment**, **recognition**, **collaboration**, accountability, **continuous learning** (training, cross-training, mentoring, pairing).
- **Team charter / working agreements**; **ground rules** (e.g., cameras on in decision meetings, response times).
- **Co-location** (war room) or effective **virtual team** practices: overlap hours, clear communication protocols, digital collaboration tools, occasional in-person meetings, inclusive meeting practices across time zones and cultures.
- **Diversity, equity and inclusion** — diverse teams make better decisions when everyone is heard; leaders ensure inclusive participation.

## 16.6 Conflict management

**Common sources of project conflict** (in descending frequency in classic research): **schedules**, **project priorities**, **resources**, technical opinions, administrative procedures, cost, personalities. Personality conflicts are usually the *least* frequent root cause.

**Conflict is normal** and can be healthy (constructive debate improves solutions). Handle it early, privately when possible, with focus on issues not personalities, and on the present and future, not the past.

### Five resolution techniques (Thomas–Kilmann / PMI)

| Technique | Description | Result | When appropriate |
|---|---|---|---|
| **Collaborate / problem-solve** | Incorporate multiple viewpoints; work openly to find a solution that satisfies all; requires trust and time | **Win-win**; consensus and commitment | Usually the **best** when time permits and the issue is important |
| **Compromise / reconcile** | Each party gives up something to reach a temporary or partial solution | **Lose-lose** (or partial win-win) | When collaboration fails, parties have equal power, or time is short |
| **Smooth / accommodate** | Emphasise areas of agreement, downplay differences; concede one's position | Lose-win; temporary peace | To preserve relationships, when the issue matters more to the other party |
| **Force / direct** | Push one viewpoint at the expense of others (using power) | Win-lose | Emergencies, safety, legal or ethical issues, when unpopular decisions are needed quickly |
| **Withdraw / avoid** | Retreat or postpone | Lose-lose (temporary) | When emotions are high and a cooling-off period helps, or the issue is trivial — never a long-term solution |

**Levels of conflict (Speed Leas):** 1 — problem to solve (information sharing, collaboration); 2 — disagreement (personal protection appears; support and empowerment help); 3 — contest (winning matters; use accommodation, negotiation); 4 — crusade/fight (protecting one's own group; diplomacy needed); 5 — world war (destroy the other; do whatever is necessary to prevent harm). The approach depends on the level.

> **Exam tip.** When two team members argue, the PM usually **first lets them try to resolve it themselves**, then **facilitates a private conversation** focused on the issue (collaborate). Escalating to functional managers or HR is for persistent or serious issues (harassment, ethics violations). In agile teams, the team is encouraged to resolve conflicts itself, with the scrum master/servant leader facilitating.

## 16.7 Emotional intelligence (EI)

The ability to identify, assess and manage one's own emotions and those of others. Goleman's model:

| Component | Focus |
|---|---|
| **Self-awareness** | Recognising one's own emotions, strengths, weaknesses |
| **Self-regulation (self-management)** | Controlling impulses, staying calm, adapting |
| **Motivation** | Inner drive toward goals |
| **Empathy** | Understanding others' feelings and perspectives |
| **Social skills** | Managing relationships, building networks, influencing |

Self-awareness and self-regulation are about **yourself**; empathy and social skills about **others**. High EI leaders handle stress, conflict and change better.

## 16.8 Decision-making

| Method | Description |
|---|---|
| **Unanimity** | Everyone agrees (Delphi can help) |
| **Majority** | More than 50% support |
| **Plurality** | The largest block decides even without a majority |
| **Autocratic** | One person decides (appropriate in emergencies or when it is clearly that person's decision) |
| **Consensus** | Everyone can live with and support the decision |
| **Fist of five / fist-to-five** | Each person shows 0–5 fingers; low counts trigger discussion |
| **Roman voting** | Thumbs up, sideways, down |
| **Multicriteria decision analysis** | Weighted criteria matrix to evaluate options |
| **Delphi** | Anonymous rounds with experts; reduces bias and groupthink |
| **Nominal group technique** | Ideas generated individually, then ranked |

**Divergent then convergent thinking:** generate options broadly (brainstorm), then narrow down. **Decide at the last responsible moment** for uncertain decisions (agile).

## 16.9 Negotiation and influencing

- Aim for **win-win** outcomes (principled negotiation — Fisher & Ury: separate people from the problem; focus on interests, not positions; invent options for mutual gain; insist on objective criteria).
- Know your **BATNA** (best alternative to a negotiated agreement) — your walk-away point; and the **ZOPA** (zone of possible agreement).
- **Influencing** without authority: build relationships and trust, understand others' interests, use data and logic, reciprocity, coalitions, credibility (expert/referent power).
- **Political awareness** — understand power structures and informal networks.

## 16.10 Coaching, mentoring and knowledge transfer (People task 7)

- **Coaching** — helps a person develop their own solutions through questions and feedback (performance-oriented, often short-term). **OSCAR model**: Outcome, Situation, Choices/consequences, Actions, Review.
- **Mentoring** — a more experienced person shares knowledge and guidance (career-oriented, longer-term).
- **Training** — formal learning (classroom, e-learning, on-the-job); planned in the resource management plan; costs included in the budget.
- **Knowledge transfer techniques** — pairing, job shadowing, communities of practice, lessons learned sessions, documentation and wikis, demos, "lunch and learn", handover workshops with operations, rotating roles, storytelling.
- Knowledge transfer happens **throughout** the project (not only at the end) and into **operations** and **future projects**.

## 16.11 Cultural awareness

- Cultures differ on power distance, individualism/collectivism, uncertainty avoidance, time orientation, communication style (direct/indirect, high-context/low-context) — e.g., Hofstede's dimensions.
- Practices: learn about team members' cultures, avoid assumptions and stereotypes, use clear language, confirm understanding, respect holidays and time zones, adapt recognition and feedback styles.

## 16.12 The PMI Code of Ethics and Professional Conduct

The Code describes expectations for PMI members and certification holders in four values. Each has **aspirational standards** (what we strive for) and **mandatory standards** (firm requirements; violations can lead to disciplinary action).

| Value | Aspirational examples | Mandatory examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Responsibility** | Make decisions in the best interests of society, safety and environment; accept only assignments consistent with our skills; fulfil commitments; own errors | **Follow policies, rules, laws**; **report unethical or illegal conduct** (backed by facts); bring violations to the attention of appropriate authorities |
| **Respect** | Learn about others' norms and customs; listen; approach conflicts directly; conduct ourselves professionally | **Negotiate in good faith**; do not exercise power to influence others for personal benefit; do not act abusively; respect others' property rights |
| **Fairness** | Be transparent in decision-making; examine impartiality; provide equal access to information; equal opportunities | **Disclose conflicts of interest** and refrain from decisions when conflicted; do not hire/fire, reward/punish based on personal considerations (favouritism, nepotism, bribery); do not discriminate |
| **Honesty** | Seek to understand the truth; be truthful in communications; provide accurate, timely information; make commitments in good faith | **Do not engage in deceptive behaviour** (false statements, half-truths, information out of context); do not act dishonestly for personal gain |

**Typical ethical scenarios and responses:**
- Gifts from vendors: follow company policy and the law; decline gifts that could influence decisions; disclose.
- Discovering a team member's falsified test results: address it, report through appropriate channels, correct the record.
- Being asked to misreport status: refuse; report accurately; discuss with the sponsor.
- Conflict of interest (e.g., a relative's company bids): **disclose** it and remove yourself from the decision.
- Local customs (e.g., facilitation payments) vs law: **follow the law** (anti-bribery laws apply even abroad) and company policy.
- Copyright/software licences: respect intellectual property.

# Chapter 17 — The Business Environment: Compliance, Issues, Change, Improvement, Sustainability, AI and PMOs

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- plan and manage compliance with laws, regulations, standards and policies;
- manage issues and remove impediments using the right escalation path;
- plan benefits realisation and transition to operations;
- support organisational change using recognised change models;
- evaluate and respond to changes in the external business environment;
- drive continuous improvement;
- integrate sustainability (ESG) into project decisions;
- use artificial intelligence responsibly in project work;
- describe the types and value of project management offices.

The Business Environment domain is now **26%** of the exam. Governance and change control were covered in Chapter 7, and risk in Chapter 14. This chapter covers the remaining tasks.

## 17.1 Compliance (Business Environment task 2)

**Compliance** = meeting the requirements imposed by laws, regulations, standards, contracts and organisational policies. Examples: data protection (e.g., GDPR), health and safety, environmental permits, accessibility, financial controls (e.g., SOX), industry standards (ISO, GMP in pharma, aviation regulations), labour law, anti-bribery and corruption laws, export controls, sustainability reporting rules.

**Managing compliance:**
1. **Identify** compliance requirements early (legal, regulatory, contractual, policy) with experts — legal, compliance, quality, security.
2. **Classify** them by category and risk (what happens if we fail?).
3. **Plan** how compliance will be achieved and demonstrated — build requirements into scope, acceptance criteria, the definition of done, the quality plan and the schedule (e.g., audit dates, regulatory submissions).
4. **Assign owners** and integrate into governance (compliance checkpoints at phase gates).
5. **Monitor and audit** — compliance audits, inspections, evidence collection.
6. **Analyse the consequences of non-compliance** (fines, shutdown, reputational damage, safety incidents) and treat them as risks.
7. **Address non-compliance immediately** — stop the non-compliant activity if needed, inform the right authorities internally, correct, and prevent recurrence.

> **Exam tip.** Compliance is **not negotiable** and not something to "trade off" for schedule. If a regulation changes during the project, assess the impact and process the needed changes — don't ignore it, and don't wait until the end. If you discover a violation, **don't hide it**: report it through the proper channels.

## 17.2 Issues and impediments (Business Environment task 4)

| | **Risk** | **Issue** | **Impediment (blocker)** |
|---|---|---|---|
| Definition | Uncertain future event | Current problem that must be resolved | Anything that slows or blocks the team's progress |
| Where recorded | Risk register | **Issue log** | Impediment board / issue log; raised in daily stand-ups |
| Typical owner | Risk owner | Issue owner (assigned) | Scrum master / servant leader, or whoever can remove it |

**Issue log** contents: issue type, who raised it and when, description, priority, **owner**, target resolution date, status, final solution.

**Handling issues and impediments:**
1. Make them **visible** (issue log, impediment board, stand-ups).
2. **Analyse** the root cause and impact.
3. **Resolve at the lowest appropriate level** — the team first, then the PM/scrum master.
4. **Escalate** through the agreed path when the resolution is outside the team's authority (functional manager, sponsor, steering committee) — with facts and options.
5. **Track** to closure and capture lessons learned; identify related risks.

> **Trap.** The servant leader removes impediments **the team cannot remove itself** — e.g., getting access to a test environment, resolving a dependency with another department. Solving every problem *for* a self-organising team (or ignoring impediments) are both wrong.

## 17.3 Benefits and value realisation

- **Benefits management plan** (Chapter 3): target benefits, owner, metrics, timeframe, assumptions, risks.
- **Value-based delivery (Process task 3)**: identify value components with stakeholders; **prioritise by value** (MoSCoW, WSJF, cost of delay); **deliver incrementally** (MVP, releases); **measure** benefits with KPIs and **OKRs** (objectives and key results); adjust based on feedback.
- **Leading vs lagging indicators**: leading indicators predict future results (e.g., feature adoption, pipeline of sales demos); lagging indicators confirm results after the fact (e.g., revenue, customer churn).
- **Transition to operations**: training, documentation, support model, service-level agreements, operational readiness reviews, hypercare period, handover of benefit tracking.
- Benefits are often realised **after** the project closes — the benefit owner continues measuring them.

## 17.4 Supporting organisational change (Business Environment task 7)

Projects create change; people must adopt it for benefits to occur. The PM supports **organisational change management (OCM)** — often with change managers and business leaders.

### Change models

| Model | Stages / elements |
|---|---|
| **Lewin** | **Unfreeze** (create readiness) → **Change** (move) → **Refreeze** (stabilise in new state) |
| **Kotter's 8 steps** | 1 Create urgency · 2 Form a powerful guiding coalition · 3 Create a vision for change · 4 Communicate the vision · 5 Remove obstacles (empower action) · 6 Create short-term wins · 7 Build on the change (consolidate) · 8 Anchor the changes in corporate culture |
| **ADKAR® (Prosci)** | **A**wareness of the need for change · **D**esire to participate · **K**nowledge of how to change · **A**bility to implement · **R**einforcement to sustain |
| **Bridges' transition model** | **Ending, losing, letting go** → **Neutral zone** → **New beginning** (focuses on people's psychological transition, not the change itself) |
| **Virginia Satir change model** | Late status quo → Introduction of a foreign element → **Chaos** → Transforming idea → **Practice and integration** → New status quo (performance dips before improving) |
| **Diffusion of innovations (Rogers)** | Innovators, early adopters, early majority, late majority, laggards — target early adopters as champions |

### Practical actions

- **Assess readiness** (culture, capacity for change, past change experiences, leadership support).
- **Engage stakeholders** early; identify **champions/change agents**; involve users in design.
- **Communicate** the *why*, *what* and *what's in it for me* — repeatedly, in multiple channels.
- **Train and support** (coaching, super-users, help desks).
- Expect and **address resistance** — listen to concerns; resistance often signals legitimate issues.
- **Celebrate quick wins**; **reinforce** with metrics, recognition, updated processes and policies.
- Avoid **change saturation** — too many simultaneous changes for the same people.

## 17.5 Evaluating external business environment changes (Business Environment task 8)

External changes — **regulatory**, **economic** (inflation, interest rates, exchange rates, tariffs), **market** (competitors, customer preferences), **technological** (new platforms, AI), **social** and **environmental** — can alter the project's value, scope, risks and constraints.

**Approach:**
1. **Scan** the environment continuously (PESTLE, market research, regulatory watch, supplier news).
2. **Assess the impact** on the business case, scope, schedule, budget, risks, compliance.
3. **Recommend options** (continue, adapt scope, re-plan, pause, cancel) and involve the decision-makers (sponsor, steering committee, product owner).
4. **Process changes** through governance (change requests, backlog reprioritisation, re-baselining).
5. **Communicate** changes and update the risk register, assumption log and business case.

## 17.6 Continuous improvement (Business Environment task 6)

- **Lessons learned** — captured throughout and shared (Chapter 7).
- **Retrospectives** — every iteration; produce **actionable** improvements that are tracked (often added to the backlog).
- **PDCA / Kaizen** — small, frequent improvements; **Six Sigma DMAIC** for process problems; **Lean** waste elimination; **value stream mapping**.
- **Root-cause analysis** (5 Whys, fishbone) — fix causes, not symptoms.
- **Experiments** — try a change for a limited time, measure, keep or discard.
- **Update OPAs** — templates, checklists, processes — so the organisation improves, not just the project.
- **Process audits** and **maturity models** (e.g., OPM3, CMMI) at organisational level.

## 17.7 Sustainability and ESG

The principle *Integrate sustainability within all project areas* (Chapter 4) plays out in the business environment as:

- **Triple bottom line** — people, planet, prosperity.
- **ESG** — environmental (emissions, energy, waste, water, biodiversity), social (labour practices, health and safety, community impact, diversity and inclusion, human rights in the supply chain), governance (ethics, transparency, anti-corruption, board oversight).
- **UN Sustainable Development Goals (SDGs)** — 17 global goals many organisations align to.
- **Sustainable procurement** — supplier codes of conduct, environmental and social criteria in source selection, local sourcing.
- **Life-cycle thinking** — design for durability, repair, reuse, recycling (**circular economy**); **life cycle assessment (LCA)** of environmental impact.
- **Carbon footprint** — measure and reduce project and product emissions (scope 1, 2, 3 emissions in corporate reporting).
- **Regulatory sustainability reporting** — organisations may be legally required to report sustainability data; projects may need to provide it.
- **Social sustainability of the team** — sustainable pace, well-being, fair treatment.

## 17.8 Artificial intelligence in project management (Appendix X3)

The Eighth Edition adds an appendix on AI. Key ideas:

**Common use cases**
- **Planning and scheduling** — draft WBS/backlogs, generate schedule options, estimate from historical data, optimise resources.
- **Risk management** — identify risks from documents and history, predict delays or overruns, analyse sentiment in communications.
- **Reporting and communications** — summarise meetings, draft status reports, translate, tailor messages for audiences.
- **Analytics and forecasting** — predictive analytics on cost and schedule, anomaly detection, scenario simulation.
- **Knowledge management** — search lessons learned, answer questions from project documents.
- **Administrative automation** — meeting notes, action tracking, document drafting, procurement document analysis.

**Responsible and ethical use**
- **Human oversight and accountability** — AI supports decisions; **people remain accountable** for them (principle: *Be an accountable leader*). Validate outputs before use.
- **Transparency** — disclose when content or decisions are AI-assisted; explainability of models.
- **Data privacy and confidentiality** — don't feed confidential, personal or client data into tools that are not approved; follow data protection laws and company policy.
- **Bias and fairness** — AI can replicate biases in its training data (e.g., in resource selection); check for fairness.
- **Accuracy** — AI can generate plausible but wrong outputs ("hallucinations"); verify facts and calculations.
- **Intellectual property** — respect copyright and licensing; clarify ownership of AI-generated content.
- **Security** — approved tools, access controls.
- **Sustainability** — consider the energy use of AI.

**Adopting AI**
- Start with a clear **purpose and value** (use cases with measurable benefits); assess **data readiness and quality**; run **pilots**; establish **AI governance** (policies, approved tools, roles); **train** people (AI literacy); manage the **organisational change** (fears about jobs, trust); monitor results and **improve**.

**AI projects themselves** have particular characteristics: experimental, data-dependent, iterative model development, uncertain outcomes (adaptive approaches fit well), plus ethical and regulatory risks.

> **Exam tip.** In AI scenarios, the best answer usually **uses AI to augment** the team while keeping **human judgment, transparency, data protection and compliance** — e.g., "validate the AI-generated schedule with the team before baselining", "use only the organisation's approved AI tools for confidential data".

## 17.9 The Project Management Office (Appendix X2)

A **PMO** is an organisational structure that standardises project-related governance processes and facilitates the sharing of resources, methodologies, tools and techniques.

| Type | Degree of control | Role |
|---|---|---|
| **Supportive** | **Low** | Consultative: templates, best practices, training, access to lessons learned |
| **Controlling** | **Moderate** | Support **and** require compliance (frameworks, methodologies, templates, governance reviews) |
| **Directive** | **High** | Directly **manages** the projects; project managers report to the PMO |

Other forms: **enterprise PMO (EPMO)** aligning portfolio to strategy; **program management office**; **agile PMO / value delivery office (VDO)** that coaches teams, supports agile practices and focuses on value rather than compliance; **centre of excellence**.

**PMO value proposition:** better alignment with strategy, consistent governance, improved decision-making (portfolio visibility), capability development, resource optimisation, knowledge management, and **customer focus** — a PMO must demonstrate value to its customers (executives, PMs, teams) and evolve, otherwise it is seen as bureaucracy. PMO **maturity models** help assess and improve capabilities.

- **B.** Send it to the customer as the final commitment

**17.7** Which situation is an issue rather than a risk?

- **A.** A supplier might go bankrupt next year
- **B.** The exchange rate may change before payment

**17.8** In Kotter's model, what comes right after creating a sense of urgency?

**17.9** Which two practices support sustainable procurement? (Choose two.)

**17.10** A retrospective identifies that code reviews are delaying delivery. What is the best way to ensure continuous improvement?

**17.11** Users of a new system resist the change and continue using old spreadsheets. What should the project manager do first?

- **B.** Report the users to HR

**17.12** A sharp rise in import tariffs will increase the cost of key materials by 20%. What is the best first step?

- **A.** Absorb the increase silently using the management reserve, so that the sponsor is not worried
- **B.** Analyse the impact on the business case and present options to decision-makers
- **C.** Immediately switch to an unqualified local supplier
- **D.** Wait until the materials are invoiced

**17.13** Which statement about leading and lagging indicators is correct?

- **A.** They are the same thing
- **B.** Leading indicators help predict results; lagging indicators measure them afterwards
- **C.** Lagging indicators are always better than leading indicators, because they are based on facts
- **D.** Leading indicators are only financial

# Chapter 18 — Tailoring

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- explain what tailoring is, why it matters and who does it;
- describe what can be tailored and the steps of the tailoring process;
- identify situational factors that drive tailoring decisions;
- recognise good and bad tailoring in scenarios.

## 18.1 What tailoring is

**Tailoring** is the deliberate adaptation of the approach, governance, processes, methods and artifacts to fit the project's context — its objectives, complexity, risks, stakeholders, team, organisation and environment. The PMBOK® Guide is a body of knowledge, not a methodology; **every project needs tailoring**.

**Why tailor?**
- Deliver **more value** by spending effort where it matters (and not on unnecessary documentation or meetings).
- **Increase commitment** from the team, who help shape the way of working.
- Improve **efficiency** and **responsiveness** to change.
- Meet **compliance** and **governance** needs proportionately (more rigour where risk is higher).
- Make the approach **customer- and context-oriented**.

**Who tailors?** The project manager collaborates with the **team**, **sponsor**, **PMO** and other governance bodies. Tailoring decisions are documented (often in the project management plan or the team's working agreement) and **revisited** as the project evolves. Tailoring must stay within **organisational policies and mandatory requirements** — you can't tailor away a legal obligation.

## 18.2 What can be tailored

| Element | Examples |
|---|---|
| **Life cycle and development approach** | Predictive, adaptive or hybrid; number and type of phases; iteration length; release cadence |
| **Processes** | Add (e.g., extra safety reviews), remove (e.g., formal procurement when nothing is bought), modify (e.g., lighter change control for low-impact changes), blend (combine processes), align (with organisational standards) |
| **Engagement** | Who is involved and how (people, empowerment, integration of contractors and partners) |
| **Tools** | Scheduling software, collaboration tools, AI tools — chosen by the team within organisational constraints |
| **Methods and artifacts** | Which techniques and documents are used, and how formal they are |
| **Governance** | Gate reviews, reporting frequency, approval thresholds |
| **Performance domains** | Depth of attention to each domain based on the project's needs |

## 18.3 The tailoring process

A common four-step sequence:

1. **Select the initial development approach** — using the factors in Chapter 5 (product, project, organisation). Assess suitability (e.g., agile suitability filter).
2. **Tailor for the organisation** — align with the organisation's policies, PMO requirements, governance, approved tools and processes; the PMO or value delivery office may review and approve tailoring.
3. **Tailor for the project** — consider the **product/deliverable** (compliance, criticality, type, industry, uncertainty, complexity), the **project team** (size, location, experience, culture, access to the customer) and the **culture** (buy-in, trust, empowerment, organisational culture).
4. **Implement ongoing improvement** — review tailoring at phase gates, retrospectives and lessons learned; adjust as the project and team learn.

## 18.4 Situational factors that drive tailoring

| Factor | Pushes toward more formality / predictive elements | Pushes toward lighter / adaptive elements |
|---|---|---|
| Regulatory and compliance requirements | High (medical devices, aviation, nuclear) | Low |
| Criticality / safety | Life-critical | Low impact |
| Requirements stability | Stable, well understood | Uncertain, emerging |
| Cost of change | High (physical construction) | Low (software) |
| Team size and distribution | Large, distributed, many vendors | Small, co-located |
| Team experience | Inexperienced with adaptive practices | Experienced, self-organising |
| Stakeholder availability | Limited, infrequent | Frequent, engaged product owner |
| Contract type | Fixed-price with defined scope | Collaborative, incremental contracts |
| Organisational culture | Hierarchical, low trust | Empowered, high trust |
| Project size and duration | Large, long | Small, short |

## 18.5 Tailoring the performance domains — examples

| Domain | Tailoring questions |
|---|---|
| Governance | How many gates? Who approves what? How formal is change control? What reporting does the steering committee need? |
| Scope | WBS, backlog or both? How detailed are requirements up front? How will acceptance work? |
| Schedule | Detailed CPM schedule, release plan, Kanban flow? Iteration length? |
| Finance | Fixed budget vs incremental funding? EVM required? Level of estimate accuracy? |
| Stakeholders | How many and how diverse? Formal communications plan or information radiators? |
| Resources | Internal team, vendors or blended? Co-located or virtual? Procurement rules? |
| Risk | Qualitative only, or quantitative analysis too? Risk-adjusted backlog? Size of reserves? |

## 18.6 Good vs bad tailoring

**Good tailoring** is intentional, justified by the context, agreed with the right people, documented, compliant, and revisited.

**Bad tailoring** includes:
- Skipping essential controls (e.g., no change control on a fixed-price contract; no testing for a safety-critical product).
- Copying another project's approach without considering differences.
- "Agile" as an excuse for no planning, no documentation and no accountability.
- Heavy documentation and approvals on a small, low-risk, fast-moving initiative.
- Tailoring that breaches organisational policy or the law.

> **Exam tip.** When a question asks what to do at the start of a project in an unfamiliar context (new client, new industry, new team), the best answer often involves **assessing the context and tailoring** the approach with the team and stakeholders — not applying a standard methodology blindly, and not abandoning all structure.

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 18](#ans-18) in Appendix B.*

**18.1** What is the primary purpose of tailoring?

- **A.** To adapt the approach, governance and processes to the project's context
- **B.** To remove all documentation from the project
- **C.** To apply every PMBOK Guide process to every project
- **D.** To allow the project manager to ignore any organisational policies that slow the project down

**18.2** Which is the first step of the tailoring process?

- **A.** Tailor for the project team's culture
- **B.** Archive the lessons learned
- **C.** Implement ongoing improvement of the approach
- **D.** Select the initial development approach

**18.3** A small internal project with low risk and no external suppliers is required by the PMO to complete a 40-page procurement plan template. What is the best course of action?

- **A.** Agree tailoring with the PMO, documenting that procurement is not applicable
- **B.** Complete the full template with invented content
- **C.** Ignore the PMO's requirement without telling anyone, since the project is small and low-risk
- **D.** Cancel the project

**18.4** A medical device project is being planned. The team wants to use agile practices for software development. What is the best tailoring decision?

- **A.** Let each developer choose a different approach
- **B.** Ban agile practices because medical devices are regulated
- **C.** Use adaptive practices for software, keeping the required regulatory controls
- **D.** Drop regulatory documentation, because agile values working software over comprehensive documentation

**18.5** Which two factors would typically push a project toward more formal, predictive tailoring? (Choose two.)

- **A.** High cost of change after construction starts
- **B.** Highly uncertain and emerging requirements
- **C.** Strict regulatory requirements for documented verification
- **D.** A small, co-located, experienced team
- **E.** Frequent availability of an engaged product owner

**18.6** When should tailoring decisions be revisited?

- **A.** Never; once approved, tailoring decisions are fixed for the whole of the project
- **B.** Only after project closure
- **C.** Throughout the project, such as at phase gates and retrospectives
- **D.** Only if the sponsor changes

**18.7** A project manager joins a new organisation and is assigned a project in an industry she does not know. What should she do first regarding the approach?

- **A.** Apply the methodology she used in her previous company unchanged, since it worked well there
- **B.** Assess the context, product, team and stakeholders, and tailor with them
- **C.** Avoid any structured approach until problems appear
- **D.** Ask the team to choose a framework by vote without analysis

**18.8** Which statement describes bad tailoring?

- **A.** Adding an extra safety review for a high-risk construction activity
- **B.** Removing change control from a fixed-price contract project to "be more agile"
- **C.** Shortening status reports for a small, low-risk project with the sponsor's agreement
- **D.** Using a backlog for the software component of a hybrid project

# Chapter 19 — Models, Methods and Artifacts: the Practitioner's Toolbox

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- distinguish models, methods and artifacts;
- select an appropriate model, method or artifact for a given purpose;
- recognise the purpose of the artifacts most often named in exam questions.

The Seventh Edition introduced the idea of a toolbox of **models, methods and artifacts (MMAs)** and the Eighth Edition keeps it. You do not need to memorise the list — but you must know **what each item is for**, because exam options often name them.

- **Model** — a thinking strategy to explain a process, framework or phenomenon (e.g., Tuckman's ladder).
- **Method** — a means of achieving an outcome, output, result or deliverable (e.g., a SWOT analysis, planning poker).
- **Artifact** — a template, document, output or project deliverable (e.g., the risk register).

## 19.1 Commonly used models

| Category | Models | Where explained |
|---|---|---|
| Leadership | Situational Leadership® II; OSCAR coaching model | Ch. 16 |
| Communication | Cross-cultural communication; effectiveness of communication channels (richness vs distance); gulf of execution and evaluation | Ch. 12 |
| Motivation | Hygiene and motivational factors (Herzberg); intrinsic vs extrinsic motivation (Pink); theory of needs (McClelland); Theory X, Y, Z | Ch. 16 |
| Change | Managing change in organisations; ADKAR®; 8-step process for leading change (Kotter); Virginia Satir change model; transition model (Bridges) | Ch. 17 |
| Complexity | Cynefin framework; Stacey matrix | Ch. 5 |
| Team development | Tuckman ladder; Drexler/Sibbet team performance model | Ch. 16 |
| Other | Conflict model (Thomas–Kilmann); negotiation (win-win, BATNA); planning; process groups/focus areas; salience model; Kano model | Ch. 8, 12, 16 |

## 19.2 Commonly used methods

### Data gathering and analysis

| Method | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **Alternatives analysis** | Evaluate options to choose the best way to do the work |
| **Assumption and constraint analysis** | Check that assumptions and constraints are valid and integrated |
| **Benchmarking** | Compare actual or planned practices with comparable organisations to find ideas and standards |
| **Business justification analysis** — payback, IRR, NPV, ROI, cost-benefit, BCR | Justify or select investments |
| **Check sheet** | Tally data during inspections |
| **Cost of quality** | Balance conformance vs non-conformance costs |
| **Decision tree analysis** | Evaluate alternatives under uncertainty (EMV) |
| **Earned value analysis** | Measure scope, schedule and cost performance |
| **Expected monetary value** | Quantify the value of uncertain outcomes |
| **Forecast** | Predict future performance (EAC, ETC, Monte Carlo) |
| **Influence diagram** | Graph the decisions, uncertainties and outcomes |
| **Life cycle assessment** | Evaluate total environmental impact of a product |
| **Make-or-buy analysis** | Decide whether to produce internally or purchase |
| **Probability and impact matrix** | Prioritise risks |
| **Process analysis** | Find process improvements |
| **Regression analysis** | Examine relationships among variables for forecasting |
| **Reserve analysis** | Evaluate remaining reserves vs remaining risk |
| **Root-cause analysis** | Find the underlying cause (5 Whys, fishbone) |
| **Sensitivity analysis** | Identify the variables with the most impact (tornado) |
| **Simulation** | Model combined effects of uncertainty (Monte Carlo) |
| **Stakeholder analysis** | Understand stakeholders' interests and influence |
| **SWOT analysis** | Strengths, weaknesses (internal), opportunities, threats (external) |
| **Trend analysis** | Is performance improving or deteriorating over time? |
| **Value stream mapping** | Visualise the flow of value and waste |
| **Variance analysis** | Determine cause and degree of difference between plan and actual |
| **What-if scenario analysis** | Assess the effect of scenarios on objectives |

### Estimating

Affinity grouping, analogous, function point (software size measurement), multipoint (three-point) estimating, parametric, relative estimating (story points), single-point estimating, story point estimating, wideband Delphi (planning poker), bottom-up.

### Meetings and events

| Event | Purpose |
|---|---|
| **Backlog refinement** | Clarify, split, estimate and order backlog items |
| **Bidder conference** | Give all prospective sellers the same information |
| **Change control board meeting** | Decide on change requests |
| **Daily stand-up** | Synchronise and identify impediments (15 min) |
| **Iteration planning / review / retrospective** | Plan the iteration; demonstrate the increment; improve the process |
| **Kickoff** | Launch the project/phase; align on objectives and roles |
| **Lessons learned meeting** | Capture knowledge to improve current and future projects |
| **Planning meeting** | Create, elaborate or review a plan and gain commitment |
| **Project review / phase gate** | Assess status and decide whether to continue |
| **Release planning** | Set the high-level plan for releases |
| **Risk review** | Analyse status of existing risks, identify new ones |
| **Status meeting** | Exchange and analyse progress information |
| **Steering committee** | Senior direction and decisions |
| **Wrap-up / close-out** | Obtain final acceptance, close the project |

### Other methods

Impact mapping (goals → actors → impacts → deliverables), modelling (prototypes, simulations), net promoter score (customer loyalty: % promoters − % detractors), prioritisation schema (MoSCoW, Kano, WSJF), timeboxing, design thinking (empathise, define, ideate, prototype, test), retrospectives formats (start-stop-continue, sailboat, 4Ls), customer journey mapping, personas.

## 19.3 Commonly used artifacts

| Category | Artifacts |
|---|---|
| **Strategy** | Business case, project brief, project charter, project vision statement, roadmap, benefits management plan |
| **Logs and registers** | Assumption log, backlog, change log, issue log, lessons learned register, risk-adjusted backlog, risk register, stakeholder register, decision log |
| **Plans** | Change control plan, communications management plan, financial (cost) management plan, iteration plan, sourcing (procurement) strategy plan, project management plan, quality management plan, release plan, requirements management plan, resource management plan, risk management plan, scope management plan, schedule management plan, stakeholder engagement plan, test plan |
| **Hierarchy charts** | Organisational breakdown structure, product breakdown structure, resource breakdown structure, risk breakdown structure, work breakdown structure |
| **Baselines** | Budget, milestone schedule, performance measurement baseline, project schedule, scope baseline |
| **Visual data and information** | Affinity diagram, burndown/burnup chart, cause-and-effect diagram, cumulative flow diagram, cycle time chart, dashboards, flowchart, Gantt chart, histogram, information radiator, lead time chart, prioritisation matrix, project schedule network diagram, requirements traceability matrix, RACI chart, scatter diagram, S-curve, stakeholder engagement assessment matrix, story map, throughput chart, use case, value stream map, velocity chart, control chart, heat map |
| **Reports** | Quality report, risk report, status report, final report |
| **Agreements and contracts** | Fixed-price, cost-reimbursable, T&M, indefinite delivery indefinite quantity (IDIQ), other agreements (MOU, MOA, SLA, BOA, letter of intent) |
| **Other** | Activity list, bid documents, metrics, project calendar, requirements documentation, project team charter, user story, definition of done, definition of ready, team agreements |

## 19.4 Quick "which artifact?" guide

| You need to… | Use… |
|---|---|
| Record a current problem and its owner | **Issue log** |
| Record an uncertain event | **Risk register** |
| Record something believed true for planning | **Assumption log** |
| Track all change requests and their status | **Change log** |
| Record decisions and their rationale | **Decision log** |
| See who does what on each activity | **RACI chart / RAM** |
| Link requirements to their source and tests | **Requirements traceability matrix** |
| See stakeholders' current vs desired engagement | **Stakeholder engagement assessment matrix** |
| Show the project's total scope as deliverables | **WBS** (predictive) / **product backlog, story map** (adaptive) |
| Describe each WBS element in detail | **WBS dictionary** |
| Show key dates to executives | **Milestone chart / roadmap** |
| Show remaining work in a sprint | **Burndown chart** |
| Show scope changes and progress together | **Burnup chart** |
| Detect bottlenecks in flow | **Cumulative flow diagram** |
| Show cumulative planned spending over time | **S-curve** (cost baseline) |
| Show the logical sequence and critical path | **Project schedule network diagram** |
| Formalise the team's values and agreements | **Team charter** |
| Prioritise threats by probability and impact | **Probability and impact matrix / heat map** |

# Chapter 20 — Formulas and Calculations Master Sheet

**After this chapter you should be able to:** perform every calculation that commonly appears on the PMP exam, quickly and accurately, and interpret the result.

> **Exam tip.** The exam has relatively few pure calculation questions, but it often gives you a number (e.g., CPI = 0.8) and asks what it means or what to do. Interpretation matters as much as arithmetic. You will have an on-screen calculator.

## 20.1 Earned value management

| Measure | Formula | Good if… |
|---|---|---|
| Cost variance | **CV = EV − AC** | ≥ 0 |
| Schedule variance | **SV = EV − PV** | ≥ 0 |
| Cost performance index | **CPI = EV ÷ AC** | ≥ 1 |
| Schedule performance index | **SPI = EV ÷ PV** | ≥ 1 |
| Estimate at completion (typical: current CPI continues) | **EAC = BAC ÷ CPI** | ≤ BAC |
| EAC (atypical: rest at budgeted rate) | **EAC = AC + (BAC − EV)** | |
| EAC (CPI and SPI both affect remaining work) | **EAC = AC + (BAC − EV) ÷ (CPI × SPI)** | |
| EAC (re-estimate) | **EAC = AC + bottom-up ETC** | |
| Estimate to complete | **ETC = EAC − AC** | |
| Variance at completion | **VAC = BAC − EAC** | ≥ 0 |
| To-complete performance index (to meet BAC) | **TCPI = (BAC − EV) ÷ (BAC − AC)** | ≤ 1 (easier) |
| TCPI (to meet EAC) | **TCPI = (BAC − EV) ÷ (EAC − AC)** | |
| Percent complete | **EV ÷ BAC** | |
| Percent spent | **AC ÷ BAC** | |
| Planned value at a point (linear plan) | **PV = BAC × (elapsed time ÷ total time)** | |

**Interpretation quick table**

| CPI | SPI | Status |
|---|---|---|
| > 1 | > 1 | Under budget, ahead of schedule |
| > 1 | < 1 | Under budget, behind schedule |
| < 1 | > 1 | Over budget, ahead of schedule |
| < 1 | < 1 | Over budget, behind schedule |

**Drill:** BAC $100,000; PV $50,000; EV $40,000; AC $45,000.
CV = −5,000 · SV = −10,000 · CPI = 0.889 · SPI = 0.80 · EAC = 100,000 ÷ 0.889 ≈ 112,500 · ETC ≈ 67,500 · VAC ≈ −12,500 · TCPI = 60,000 ÷ 55,000 ≈ 1.09.

## 20.2 Estimating

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Triangular (simple average) | **E = (O + M + P) ÷ 3** |
| Beta / PERT | **E = (O + 4M + P) ÷ 6** |
| PERT standard deviation | **σ = (P − O) ÷ 6** |
| PERT variance | **σ² = [(P − O) ÷ 6]²** |
| Path standard deviation | **√(sum of activity variances)** |
| Confidence ranges | ±1σ ≈ 68.3% · ±2σ ≈ 95.5% · ±3σ ≈ 99.7% |
| Parametric | **Quantity × unit rate** |
| ROM / budget / definitive ranges | −25% to +75% · −10% to +25% · −5% to +10% |

**Drill:** O = 10, M = 16, P = 28 → PERT E = (10 + 64 + 28) ÷ 6 = **17**; σ = 18 ÷ 6 = **3**; 95% range ≈ 17 ± 6 = **11 to 23**.

## 20.3 Schedule network

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Early finish | **EF = ES + duration** (with ES of first activity = 0) |
| Late start | **LS = LF − duration** |
| Total float | **TF = LS − ES = LF − EF** |
| Free float | **FF = ES (earliest successor) − EF (activity)** |
| Critical path | Longest path; usually zero total float |

(If your course uses "day 1" conventions — EF = ES + duration − 1 — the float results are the same. Be consistent.)

## 20.4 Agile and flow

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Velocity | Story points of **done** items per iteration |
| Iterations remaining | **Remaining story points ÷ average velocity** (round up) |
| Little's Law | **Cycle time = WIP ÷ throughput** |
| Throughput | Items completed ÷ time period |
| Lead time | Request → delivery |
| Cycle time | Start of work → completion |

**Drill:** 180 points remaining, velocity 24 → 7.5 → **8 iterations**.

## 20.5 Communication

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Communication channels | **n (n − 1) ÷ 2** |

**Drill:** 6 people → 15 channels; adding 3 people (9) → 36 channels (+21).

## 20.6 Risk

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Expected monetary value | **EMV = probability × impact** (threats negative, opportunities positive) |
| Risk score (qualitative) | **probability × impact** (on the scales defined in the risk management plan) |
| Decision tree | Expected value of each branch = cost/value + Σ(probability × outcome); choose the best expected value |

**Drill:** 10% chance of $200,000 loss and 30% chance of $50,000 gain → EMV = −20,000 + 15,000 = **−$5,000**.

## 20.7 Finance and selection

| Measure | Formula / rule |
|---|---|
| Present value | **PV = FV ÷ (1 + r)ⁿ** |
| Future value | **FV = PV × (1 + r)ⁿ** |
| NPV | Σ PV of inflows − Σ PV of outflows; choose the **highest** (> 0) |
| IRR | Rate where NPV = 0; choose the **highest** |
| ROI | **(Benefit − cost) ÷ cost × 100%** |
| Payback period | **Investment ÷ annual net cash inflow** (uneven flows: accumulate until recovered) |
| Benefit–cost ratio | **Benefits ÷ costs**; > 1 is favourable |
| Opportunity cost | Value of the **best alternative not chosen** |
| Straight-line depreciation | **(Cost − salvage value) ÷ useful life** |

**Drill:** invest $50,000 in a tool that saves $20,000 per year for 4 years → ROI over 4 years = (80,000 − 50,000) ÷ 50,000 = **60%**; payback = **2.5 years**.

## 20.8 Procurement

| Measure | Formula |
|---|---|
| Make-or-buy break-even (rent vs buy) | Purchase cost + (daily operating cost × d) = daily rental × d → **d = purchase cost ÷ (rental − operating cost)** |
| CPIF / FPIF fee adjustment | **Fee = target fee + seller share × (target cost − actual cost)** |
| Total price (CPIF) | **Actual cost + fee** |
| FPIF price | **Actual cost + adjusted profit**, capped at the **ceiling price** |
| Point of total assumption | **PTA = (ceiling price − target price) ÷ buyer's share ratio + target cost** |

## 20.9 Quality

| Measure | Formula / rule |
|---|---|
| Control limits | Mean ± 3σ (process data) |
| Rule of seven | 7 consecutive points on one side of the mean → investigate |
| Cost of quality | Conformance (prevention + appraisal) + non-conformance (internal + external failure) |
| Six Sigma | 3.4 defects per million opportunities |

## 20.10 Mixed drills (answers below)

1. EV = 120, AC = 100, PV = 150. What are CPI and SPI?
2. BAC = 500, CPI = 1.25. EAC (typical)?
3. A team of 12 adds 4 people. How many channels are added?
4. O = 2, M = 5, P = 14. PERT estimate and σ?
5. Project A: NPV $80k over 4 years; Project B: NPV $95k over 6 years. Which one, and opportunity cost?
6. 25 items in progress, throughput 5 per week. Cycle time?
7. Option 1 costs $40k with a 50% chance of $10k extra; option 2 costs $44k with no risk. Better option by expected cost?
8. FPIF: target cost $80k, target profit $8k, ceiling $96k, share 60/40, actual cost $90k. Price paid?

**Answers:** 1) CPI 1.2, SPI 0.8 (under budget, behind schedule). 2) 400. 3) 12 people → 66 channels; 16 people → 120 channels; **54 added**. 4) E = (2 + 20 + 14) ÷ 6 = **6**; σ = 12 ÷ 6 = **2**. 5) **Project B** (higher NPV — duration already accounted for); opportunity cost **$80k**. 6) 25 ÷ 5 = **5 weeks**. 7) Option 1: 40 + 5 = **$45k**; option 2: $44k → **option 2**. 8) Overrun $10k; seller absorbs 40% = $4k; profit = 8 − 4 = $4k; price = 90 + 4 = **$94k** (below the ceiling of $96k).

# Chapter 21 — Exam Strategy and the PMI Mindset

**After this chapter you should be able to:**
- apply the "PMI mindset" to choose the best answer among several plausible options;
- use a repeatable technique to read and answer situational questions;
- manage time, breaks and stress on exam day;
- avoid the most common traps.

## 21.1 The PMI mindset — 25 rules of thumb

These are not laws, but they describe how the "best" answer is usually chosen.

**Leadership and people**
1. **Be a servant leader.** Support, coach, facilitate and remove impediments; don't command and control unless there is an emergency.
2. **Empower the team.** Let self-organising teams decide *how* to do their work; involve the team in estimates and plans.
3. **Talk to people directly.** Address problems face-to-face (or the closest equivalent) and privately, before escalating.
4. **Collaborate to resolve conflict.** Problem-solving is the default; forcing only for emergencies or ethics.
5. **Recognise and develop people.** Training, mentoring and recognition are good investments.
6. **Respect culture and diversity.** Adapt communication; include everyone.

**Analysis and action**
7. **Assess before acting.** When something happens, first understand it: gather facts, review the plan, analyse impact and root cause.
8. **But don't just analyse — act.** Among options, prefer the one that *does something* appropriate after analysis, rather than waiting or ignoring.
9. **Fix root causes, not symptoms.**
10. **Be proactive.** Anticipate risks, engage stakeholders early, plan communication, prevent problems.
11. **Prevention over inspection; quality is built in.**

**Process and governance**
12. **Follow the agreed process.** Changes to baselines go through change control; contracts through contract change control; agile changes through the backlog and product owner.
13. **Know who decides.** The product owner decides priorities; the sponsor/steering committee decides on business case and major changes; the CCB on changes per thresholds; the team on how to build. The PM facilitates and recommends.
14. **Escalate only when needed** — when the issue exceeds your authority or the team's ability, and after trying to resolve it yourself — and escalate with **options**, not just problems.
15. **Keep documents current.** Update the risk register, issue log, lessons learned, stakeholder register as things happen.
16. **Never implement unapproved changes**, and don't gold-plate.
17. **Close properly.** Formal acceptance, lessons learned, contract closure, archiving, then release resources.

**Value, ethics and environment**
18. **Focus on value and outcomes**, not just outputs or the triple constraint.
19. **Be honest and transparent.** Report bad news early with options; never hide problems or falsify data.
20. **Follow laws, regulations, policies and the Code of Ethics.** Disclose conflicts of interest; refuse bribes; report illegal conduct.
21. **Consider sustainability and the wider impact** of decisions.
22. **Use AI responsibly** — as an aid, with human validation, transparency and data protection.

**Context**
23. **Identify the approach** (predictive, agile, hybrid) and answer in that context.
24. **Tailor** — the right amount of process for the situation.
25. **Assume you are in a well-run organisation** with the usual artifacts (charter, plans, registers) unless the question says otherwise — and that the PM has the authority the question describes.

## 21.2 A repeatable technique for situational questions

1. **Read the last sentence first** — what is being asked? (*first*, *next*, *best*, *most likely*, *least*, *EXCEPT*, *what should have been done to prevent…*)
2. **Read the scenario and underline the key facts**: the approach (sprints? baseline?), the phase (planning, executing, closing?), who is involved, what has already been done, the real problem (often not the one stated).
3. **Predict the answer** before reading the options.
4. **Eliminate** clearly wrong options — usually two:
   - options that ignore the problem, delay without reason, or are passive;
   - options that escalate prematurely or hand the problem to someone else;
   - options that break rules (unapproved changes, ethics violations, bypassing the product owner);
   - options that use the wrong approach for the context (formal CCB for a backlog item in Scrum; "just start coding" in a regulated predictive project).
5. **Choose between the remaining two** by asking: *Which one happens first? Which one addresses the root cause? Which one is more aligned with servant leadership, value and the agreed process?*
6. **Watch for "first" vs "next".** "First" usually means analysis or understanding (review, assess, discuss). "Next" assumes something has already been done — read carefully what that is.
7. **Don't add facts.** Answer the question as written, not the situation you imagine.
8. **Flag and move on** if you're stuck for more than ~2 minutes. Never leave a question blank — there is no penalty for guessing.

## 21.3 Typical question patterns and their usual answers

| Pattern | Usually best |
|---|---|
| A stakeholder asks for a change in a predictive project | Assess impact → change request → CCB decision → update plan → communicate |
| A stakeholder asks for a change in an agile project | Add to the backlog; product owner prioritises |
| A team member is underperforming | Private conversation to understand causes; coaching/training; then involve functional manager if needed |
| Two team members are in conflict | Let them try first; then facilitate a collaborative discussion |
| The sponsor wants a delivery date the team believes is impossible | Analyse with the team; present data and options (scope, resources, phasing); negotiate |
| A risk occurs that was identified | Implement the planned response (risk owner); update the register |
| An unidentified problem occurs | Treat it as an issue: assess, workaround, issue log, then identify new risks |
| A new stakeholder appears | Add to the stakeholder register, analyse, plan engagement |
| The team is missing sprint commitments | Discuss in the retrospective; look at capacity, WIP, impediments, estimation |
| A vendor is late | Review the contract and performance; communicate with the vendor through the agreed channel |
| The project is cancelled | Close the project: document, transfer, close contracts, lessons learned, release resources |
| A team member asks the PM to decide a technical approach (agile) | Coach the team to decide |
| The product owner is unavailable | Coach on the role; agree availability; escalate if needed |
| A regulatory change appears mid-project | Assess impact with experts; process changes; compliance is mandatory |
| A request to hide bad news | Refuse; report transparently with options |

## 21.4 Time management and exam day

- **Pacing:** ~80 seconds per question. Checkpoints: Q60 at ~80 min, Q120 at ~160 min, Q180 at ~235 min.
- **Breaks:** take both. Before each break, review flagged questions in that section — you can't return after the break.
- **Calculations:** write the formula, then plug in numbers. Re-check signs (negative CV = over budget).
- **Case-study sets:** read the case once carefully, then refer back for each question; the questions may test different domains.
- **Multiple-response:** the question tells you how many to choose; partial credit is not given.
- **Matching/drag-and-drop:** start with the pairs you are sure of; use elimination for the rest.
- **Online proctored exams:** test your system in advance; clear your desk; have ID ready; no notes, phones or watches.
- **Physical readiness:** sleep, hydration, a light meal. Arrive early (or log in early).
- **Mental readiness:** expect hard questions — the exam feels difficult for almost everyone. Many unscored pretest items are deliberately difficult; don't let them shake you.

## 21.5 The final two weeks

- Take at least **three full-length timed simulations** (this package has ten).
- Review **every** explanation, especially where you chose the second-best answer — those reveal mindset gaps.
- Keep an **error log**: question, your answer, correct answer, why, which principle or process it tests.
- Re-read Chapters 4 (principles), 7 (governance/change), 15 (agile), 16 (people) and this chapter.
- Memorise the formula sheet (Chapter 20) and practise ten calculations per day.
- Stop heavy studying the day before the exam; review the mindset rules and rest.


## Chapter 21 Review Questions

*Answer all questions first, then check the [answer key for Chapter 21](#ans-21) in Appendix B.*

**21.1** A question asks what the project manager should do FIRST when a team member reports that a critical supplier may deliver late. Which type of answer is most likely to be correct?

- **A.** Ignoring the report until the delay has been confirmed in writing by the supplier's management
- **B.** An action that gathers information and assesses the situation first
- **C.** Terminating the supplier's contract
- **D.** Escalating immediately to the sponsor

**21.2** In an agile project, a senior manager asks the project manager to add a feature to the current sprint. What is the best response?

- **A.** Submit the request to the change control board, since all scope changes need formal approval
- **B.** Refuse the request because the sprint is locked
- **C.** Add the feature to the current sprint immediately
- **D.** Explain that the product owner prioritises new work, and connect them

**21.3** Which answer pattern is usually WRONG on the PMP exam?

- **A.** Escalating to the sponsor before trying to understand or resolve the problem
- **B.** Meeting privately with a team member to discuss the concerns about their recent performance
- **C.** Updating the risk register when new risks are identified
- **D.** Facilitating a collaborative discussion to resolve conflict

**21.4** Before taking the first optional break, what should a candidate do?

- **A.** Skip remaining questions to save time
- **B.** Nothing; breaks have no effect on navigation
- **C.** Review the flagged questions in the first section, since they cannot be revisited
- **D.** Leave the flagged questions to review at the end of the exam, when there is more time

**21.5** The sponsor asks the project manager to report the project as "green" to the board although it is significantly behind schedule. What should the project manager do?

- **A.** Report the true status, with recovery options, and discuss the concern with the sponsor
- **B.** Report green as requested
- **C.** Avoid sending any report this month
- **D.** Report green as requested, but tell the team the truth so that they can recover the delay

**21.6** A scenario describes a project that uses a WBS, a schedule baseline and a change control board. Which mechanism is most appropriate for handling a requested scope change?

- **A.** Add the request to the product backlog for the product owner to prioritise in the next sprint
- **B.** Integrated change control: assess the impact and submit a change request
- **C.** Implement it if it takes less than one day
- **D.** Ask the team to vote on it

**21.7** Which two techniques help you choose between two remaining plausible answers? (Choose two.)

- **A.** Ask which option addresses the root cause rather than a symptom
- **B.** Choose the option that involves the most senior person
- **C.** Ask which option would logically happen first in the sequence
- **D.** Choose the longest option
- **E.** Choose the option with the most technical terms

**21.8** What is a good pacing target for the PMP exam (180 questions, 240 minutes)?

- **A.** Pacing does not matter, because the time allowed for the PMP exam is effectively unlimited
- **B.** About 2 minutes per question
- **C.** About 80 seconds per question, reaching question 60 around the 80-minute mark
- **D.** About 30 seconds per question
