# Appendix A — Glossary of Key Terms

Definitions are written for exam preparation (in plain language). Terms are listed alphabetically.

| Term | Definition |
|---|---|
| **Acceptance criteria** | Conditions that must be met before a deliverable or user story is accepted. |
| **Accepted deliverables** | Deliverables formally accepted by the customer or sponsor (output of Validate Scope). |
| **Accuracy** | How close a measured value is to the true value. |
| **Activity** | A distinct, scheduled portion of work performed during a project; decomposed from work packages. |
| **Actual cost (AC)** | The realised cost incurred for the work performed during a specific period. |
| **Adaptive approach** | A development approach in which requirements are elaborated and validated frequently during delivery (agile). |
| **Affinity diagram** | A technique that groups many ideas into themes. |
| **Agreement** | Any document or communication defining the initial intentions of a project, e.g., contract, MOU, SLA. |
| **Analogous estimating** | Estimating using historical data from a similar activity or project. |
| **Assumption** | A factor considered true, real or certain without proof, for planning purposes. |
| **Assumption log** | Record of assumptions and constraints throughout the project. |
| **Backlog refinement** | Ongoing clarification, splitting, estimation and ordering of backlog items. |
| **BAC (budget at completion)** | The sum of all budgets established for the work to be performed; equals the cost baseline total. |
| **Baseline** | The approved version of a work product, changed only through formal change control. |
| **Benefits management plan** | Describes how and when benefits will be delivered and measured, with a benefit owner. |
| **Bidder conference** | Meeting with prospective sellers before proposals are prepared, so all receive the same information. |
| **Bottom-up estimating** | Estimating by aggregating estimates of lower-level components. |
| **Burndown / burnup chart** | Graphs of remaining work / completed work over time. |
| **Business case** | Documented economic feasibility study used to justify a project and assess its continuing validity. |
| **Change control board (CCB)** | A group responsible for reviewing, evaluating, approving, deferring or rejecting changes. |
| **Change log** | A list of changes submitted and their status. |
| **Change request** | A formal proposal to modify a document, deliverable or baseline (corrective, preventive, defect repair, update). |
| **Charter (project)** | Document issued by the sponsor that authorises the project and gives the PM authority. |
| **Code of accounts** | Numbering system that uniquely identifies each WBS element. |
| **Configuration management** | Managing the specifications of product and documents: identification, status accounting, verification and audit. |
| **Constraint** | A limiting factor that affects the execution of a project. |
| **Contingency reserve** | Time or money for identified risks (known-unknowns); part of the baseline. |
| **Control account** | A management control point where scope, budget and schedule are integrated for performance measurement. |
| **Control chart** | Graph of process data over time against control limits, used to determine stability. |
| **Cost baseline** | Approved time-phased budget, excluding management reserve. |
| **Cost of quality (COQ)** | Costs of conformance (prevention, appraisal) plus costs of non-conformance (internal, external failure). |
| **Crashing** | Shortening the schedule by adding resources to critical-path activities at the least cost. |
| **Critical path** | The longest path through a schedule network; determines the shortest project duration. |
| **Cumulative flow diagram (CFD)** | Chart showing work in each state over time; reveals bottlenecks. |
| **Cycle time** | Time from starting work on an item to completing it. |
| **Decomposition** | Dividing scope and deliverables into smaller, more manageable parts. |
| **Definition of done (DoD)** | Team checklist of criteria every increment must meet to be considered done. |
| **Definition of ready (DoR)** | Criteria a backlog item must meet before the team can start it. |
| **Deliverable** | Any unique, verifiable product, result or capability produced to complete a process, phase or project. |
| **Development approach** | Method used to create and evolve the product: predictive, iterative, incremental, adaptive, hybrid. |
| **Earned value (EV)** | The measure of work performed expressed in terms of the budget authorised for that work. |
| **Emotional intelligence** | Ability to identify, assess and manage one's own emotions and those of others. |
| **Enterprise environmental factors (EEFs)** | Conditions, not under the team's immediate control, that influence the project. |
| **Escalate (risk strategy)** | Passing a risk outside the project's scope or authority to the appropriate owner. |
| **Estimate at completion (EAC)** | Expected total cost of completing all work. |
| **Expected monetary value (EMV)** | Probability × impact; sum across outcomes. |
| **Fast tracking** | Performing activities in parallel that were planned in sequence. |
| **Float (slack)** | Time an activity can be delayed: total float (without delaying the project), free float (without delaying successors). |
| **Focus area** | Eighth-Edition name for a process group: Initiating, Planning, Executing, Monitoring and Controlling, Closing. |
| **Gold plating** | Adding features or quality beyond requirements, not requested by the customer. |
| **Governance** | Framework of authority, accountability, decision rights and oversight directing the project. |
| **Grade** | Category assigned to products with the same use but different technical characteristics. |
| **Hybrid approach** | A combination of predictive and adaptive elements. |
| **Increment** | A usable, potentially releasable piece of the product. |
| **Information radiator** | A large, visible display of project information (task board, burn chart). |
| **Issue** | A current condition or situation that may affect objectives and must be resolved. |
| **Issue log** | Document recording issues, owners, actions and status. |
| **Iteration** | A timeboxed cycle of development (sprint). |
| **Kanban** | Method for managing flow by visualising work and limiting WIP. |
| **Lag / Lead** | Required delay / allowed acceleration between a predecessor and a successor. |
| **Lead time** | Time from a request to its delivery. |
| **Lessons learned register** | Document recording knowledge gained during the project, updated throughout. |
| **Management reserve** | Budget withheld for unidentified risks (unknown-unknowns); outside the cost baseline. |
| **Milestone** | A significant point or event; zero duration. |
| **Minimum viable product (MVP)** | Smallest product that allows learning whether the solution delivers value. |
| **Monte Carlo simulation** | Computer simulation that uses probability distributions to model possible outcomes. |
| **Net present value (NPV)** | Present value of inflows minus present value of outflows. |
| **Organisational process assets (OPAs)** | Plans, processes, policies, procedures and knowledge bases of the performing organisation. |
| **Parametric estimating** | Estimating using a statistical relationship between historical data and other variables. |
| **Performance domain** | A group of related activities critical for the effective delivery of outcomes (seven in the Eighth Edition). |
| **Performance measurement baseline (PMB)** | Integrated scope, schedule and cost baselines used for earned value comparisons. |
| **Planned value (PV)** | The authorised budget assigned to scheduled work. |
| **Portfolio** | Projects, programs and operations managed as a group to achieve strategic objectives. |
| **Precision** | How close repeated measurements are to each other. |
| **Predictive approach** | A development approach in which scope, time and cost are determined early in the life cycle. |
| **Principle** | A fundamental norm, truth or value guiding behaviour (six in the Eighth Edition). |
| **Product backlog** | Ordered list of everything that might be needed in the product; owned by the product owner. |
| **Product owner** | Person accountable for maximising the value of the product and ordering the backlog. |
| **Program** | Related projects and activities managed in a coordinated way to obtain benefits. |
| **Progressive elaboration** | Iteratively increasing the level of detail as more information becomes available. |
| **Project** | A temporary endeavour undertaken to create a unique product, service or result. |
| **Project management plan** | Document describing how the project will be executed, monitored and controlled, and closed. |
| **Quality** | Degree to which inherent characteristics fulfil requirements. |
| **RACI chart** | Responsibility matrix: Responsible, Accountable, Consulted, Informed. |
| **Requirements traceability matrix** | Grid linking requirements from their origin to the deliverables and tests that satisfy them. |
| **Residual risk** | Risk that remains after responses are implemented. |
| **Resource levelling / smoothing** | Adjusting dates to balance resource demand (may change the end date) / within float (does not). |
| **Risk** | An uncertain event or condition that, if it occurs, has a positive or negative effect on objectives. |
| **Risk appetite / threshold** | Willingness to accept uncertainty / measurable level of acceptable variation. |
| **Risk register** | Repository of identified risks, their analysis and responses. |
| **Rolling wave planning** | Planning near-term work in detail and future work at a higher level. |
| **Scope baseline** | Approved scope statement, WBS and WBS dictionary. |
| **Scope creep** | Uncontrolled expansion of scope without adjustments to time, cost and resources. |
| **Secondary risk** | Risk arising as a direct result of implementing a risk response. |
| **Servant leadership** | Leadership that focuses on serving the team: removing impediments, coaching, empowering. |
| **Sourcing strategy plan** | Eighth-Edition plan describing what will be acquired externally, how and from whom. |
| **Sponsor** | Person or group providing resources and support for the project and accountable for enabling success. |
| **Sprint** | Scrum's timeboxed iteration of one month or less. |
| **Stakeholder** | Anyone who may affect, be affected by, or perceive itself to be affected by the project. |
| **Stakeholder register** | Document with identification, assessment and classification of stakeholders. |
| **Story points** | Relative units estimating the size and complexity of backlog items. |
| **Sustainability** | Meeting present needs without compromising the future — economic, environmental and social. |
| **Tailoring** | Deliberate adaptation of approach, governance and processes to the context. |
| **Team charter** | Document of team values, agreements and operating guidelines. |
| **Throughput** | Number of items completed per unit of time. |
| **Timebox** | A fixed period of time for an activity. |
| **To-complete performance index (TCPI)** | Cost efficiency needed on remaining work to meet a target (BAC or EAC). |
| **Trigger condition** | Event or situation indicating a risk is about to occur. |
| **User story** | Short description of value for a user: "As a… I want… so that…". |
| **Validate Scope** | Process of formalising acceptance of completed deliverables. |
| **Value** | The worth, importance or usefulness of something to stakeholders. |
| **Velocity** | Amount of work (story points) a team completes per iteration. |
| **Work breakdown structure (WBS)** | Hierarchical decomposition of the total scope of work. |
| **Work package** | Lowest level of the WBS, for which cost and duration are estimated and managed. |
| **Work performance data / information / reports** | Raw observations / analysed data compared with plans / compiled documents for decisions. |
| **Workaround** | An unplanned response to a risk that occurred or an issue. |

# Appendix B — Answer Keys for the Chapter Review Questions

Each answer includes a short explanation. If you missed a question, re-read the related section before moving on.

<a id="ans-1"></a>

### Chapter 1 — The PMP® Exam (2026) and How the PMBOK® Guide Fits

**1.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. 170

The exam contains 180 questions, of which 170 are scored and 10 are unscored pretest items. The figures 175 scored and 230 minutes belong to the earlier format.

**1.2 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 26%

Business Environment rose from 8% to 26%. People is 33% and Process is 41%.

**1.3 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Business Environment

In the 2026 outline, risk planning and management is a Business Environment task, together with governance, compliance, change control, issues and impediments, continuous improvement, organisational change and the external environment.

**1.4 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 36 months

With a four-year degree the requirement is 36 months of experience leading projects within the last eight years. 24 months applies to GAC-accredited degrees and 60 months to a secondary diploma or associate's degree.

**1.5 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 40%

About 40% of items reflect predictive approaches; the remaining ~60% reflect agile or hybrid approaches.

**1.6 — Answer: D, E**

*Correct:* D. Align stakeholder expectations / E. Help ensure knowledge transfer

People contains: develop a common vision, manage conflicts, lead the team, engage stakeholders, align stakeholder expectations, manage stakeholder expectations, help ensure knowledge transfer, and plan and manage communication. Evaluate project status and procurement are Process tasks; organisational change is a Business Environment task.

**1.7 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. The exam follows the content outline; the PMBOK Guide is a core reference for questions

The PMP is based on the ECO. The PMBOK Guide is a key reference among several, and questions test the application of its concepts in realistic situations rather than memorisation of text.

**1.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. A performance rating, such as Above Target or Needs Improvement

PMI reports an overall Pass/Fail result plus a performance rating per domain. It does not publish raw scores or a fixed pass mark.

**1.9 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. They can no longer be reviewed or changed

Once you start a break, the previous block of questions is locked. Review flagged questions before taking the break.

**1.10 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The best action may differ by context, e.g. backlog reprioritisation instead of a change request

The values behind the answers are the same, but the mechanisms differ: in an agile context new work usually goes to the product backlog for the product owner to prioritise, while in a predictive context a change to a baseline goes through integrated change control.

**1.11 — Answer: 1→A, 2→B, 3→D, 4→C**

*Correct pairs:* 1. Scored questions → 170; 2. Exam duration → 240 minutes; 3. Weight of the People domain → 33%; 4. Weight of the Process domain → 41%

180 questions are delivered (170 scored + 10 pretest) in 240 minutes. People is 33%, Process 41% and Business Environment 26%.


<a id="ans-2"></a>

### Chapter 2 — PMBOK® Guide 8 at a Glance: Structure, Evolution and Core Vocabulary

**2.1 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 6 principles, 7 performance domains, 5 focus areas and 40 processes

The Eighth Edition is organised around 6 principles, 7 performance domains, 5 focus areas and 40 processes. Twelve principles and eight domains describe the Seventh Edition; ten knowledge areas and 49 processes describe the Sixth.

**2.2 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Quality

The seven domains are Governance, Scope, Schedule, Finance, Stakeholders, Resources and Risk. Quality is embedded across domains: planning in Define Scope, assurance in Governance and control in Monitor and Control Scope.

**2.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Higher customer retention and lower processing costs

The output is the system, the outcome is the faster processing, and the benefit is the gain the organisation realises from that outcome, such as retention and cost savings.

**2.4 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. A program coordinates related components for added benefits; a portfolio groups components to meet strategy

Programs are about coordination of related work for benefits; portfolios are about selecting and prioritising investments for strategy. Portfolio components need not be related.

**2.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The lessons learned repository from previous projects

OPAs are plans, processes, policies, procedures and knowledge bases owned by the performing organisation, such as historical information and lessons learned. Regulations, culture and market conditions are EEFs.

**2.6 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Stakeholders performance domain

Plan Communications Management, Manage Communications and Monitor Communications are processes of the Stakeholders domain, reflecting the close link between communication and engagement.

**2.7 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Temporary means a definite beginning and end, not a short duration

Temporary refers to a definite start and end. A project can run for years, and the product or outcome it creates can be long-lasting.

**2.8 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Principles alone were too abstract, so processes returned as non-prescriptive guidance

The Eighth Edition keeps the principles and domains of the Seventh Edition but reconnects them with practical, non-prescriptive processes that apply to predictive, adaptive and hybrid approaches.

**2.9 — Answer: B, C**

*Correct:* B. Artificial Intelligence / C. Procurement

The Eighth Edition appendices include the PMO (X2), Artificial Intelligence (X3) and Procurement (X4). Earned value, the Agile Manifesto and business analysis are covered elsewhere, not as these appendices.

**2.10 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Replacing the software is a project; running monthly payroll is operations

The replacement is temporary and unique, so it is a project. Monthly payroll is ongoing and repetitive, so it is operations, which the project changed.

**2.11 — Answer: 1→C, 2→A, 3→B, 4→D**

*Correct pairs:* 1. Principles → Why — which beliefs and behaviours should guide decisions?; 2. Performance domains → What — which areas of activity must be managed effectively?; 3. Processes and focus areas → How — which work is performed, in what order, with which tools?; 4. Tailoring → Fit — how should the approach be adapted to this context?

Principles give the why, domains the what, and processes the how; tailoring adapts all of them to the specific project, organisation and environment.

**2.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. An enterprise environmental factor

External regulations are conditions not under the immediate control of the team that constrain or influence the project, which defines an enterprise environmental factor.


<a id="ans-3"></a>

### Chapter 3 — The Standard: A System for Value Delivery, Governance and the Project Environment

**3.1 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Project charter

The charter, issued by the sponsor or initiator, formally authorises the project and documents the PM's authority. The business case justifies the investment and the benefits plan describes how benefits will be realised.

**3.2 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The business case

The business case contains the justification and cost-benefit analysis for the investment. Phase gates are the natural point to confirm it is still valid and decide to continue, change or stop the project.

**3.3 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The sponsor or the designated benefit owner

The benefits management plan names a benefit owner, often the sponsor or an operational manager, who is accountable for realising benefits after transition. The PM's accountability normally ends at project closure.

**3.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Functional

In a functional structure the functional manager controls resources and budget; the PM role is part-time with little or no authority.

**3.5 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Negotiate with the functional manager for the engineer's time

In a matrix the functional manager controls the resource, so the PM must negotiate and use influence. Escalation is reserved for when negotiation fails; bypassing the manager breaks governance.

**3.6 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The organisation's culture and appetite for risk

Culture and risk appetite influence the project but are not under the team's immediate control, so they are EEFs. Templates, repositories and procedures are organisational process assets.

**3.7 — Answer: B, C**

*Correct:* B. The benefit owner responsible for realisation / C. The timeframe for realising target benefits

A benefits management plan describes target benefits, strategic alignment, timeframe, benefit owner, metrics, assumptions and risks. Critical path, ground rules and WBS details belong to other artifacts.

**3.8 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Strategy guides the work, and performance and benefit information lets leaders adjust strategy

Leadership sets direction downward; projects and operations feed back performance, lessons and benefit data upward. Without honest upward feedback, the organisation cannot rebalance its portfolio.

**3.9 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. A product-oriented approach that delivers value continuously

Long-lived product teams that deliver value continuously reflect product management considerations described in the Standard, distinct from temporary project teams.

**3.10 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Power Skills

Power Skills are the interpersonal skills; Ways of Working covers delivery approaches and techniques; Business Acumen covers strategy, finance and market understanding.

**3.11 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Develop it with the sponsor, who issues and signs it

The PM often helps develop the charter, but it is issued by the sponsor or initiator at a level appropriate to fund and authorise the project. The PM cannot authorise their own project.

**3.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Operations

Once deliverables are transitioned, operations run and sustain them, enabling benefits over time. Projects create the outputs; operations keep them working.


<a id="ans-4"></a>

### Chapter 4 — The Six Principles of Project Management

**4.1 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Adopt a holistic view

Considering the ripple effects of one decision on several areas and on the wider environment is systems thinking, the core of adopting a holistic view.

**4.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Re-evaluate the business case with the sponsor and consider changing direction

Value, not completion, is the measure of success. Continuing only because of money already spent is sunk-cost thinking; the right action is to revisit the business case with the decision-maker.

**4.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Agreeing on a definition of done and automated tests before development starts

Embedding quality means building it in from the start through clear criteria and preventive practices. Final inspection alone is detection, gold plating adds risk without value, and quality is everyone's responsibility.

**4.4 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Inform the sponsor promptly, correct the report and explain the planned actions

Accountable leaders are transparent and own their mistakes. Correcting the record promptly preserves trust; hiding or shifting blame violates the Code of Ethics.

**4.5 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Integrate sustainability within all project areas

Sustainability requires considering environmental and social impacts in sourcing decisions. Value is broader than price, and a holistic view would also consider reputational and regulatory risk.

**4.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Encourage the team to decide the breakdown and offer support

Self-organising teams decide how to do their work. The PM supports and coaches rather than dictating, which builds ownership and capability.

**4.7 — Answer: B, C**

*Correct:* B. Reporting a project as green although critical milestones are slipping / C. Accepting a valuable gift from a bidder during an open tender

Misleading status reports and conflicts of interest breach transparency and integrity. Escalating through the agreed path, admitting mistakes and seeking input are accountable behaviours.

**4.8 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Because embedding quality makes it part of every domain, especially Governance and Scope

Quality is integrated rather than isolated: it is planned with scope, assured through governance and controlled with scope, reflecting that it is everyone's responsibility.

**4.9 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Integrate sustainability, including the team's social sustainability and a sustainable pace

Social sustainability includes people's well-being and a sustainable pace. Persistent overtime also damages quality and engagement, so it threatens several principles, but sustainability is the most direct.

**4.10 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Optimise risk responses

Optimise risk responses was absorbed into the Risk performance domain. The other three map to the principles Focus on value, Be an accountable leader and Build an empowered culture.

**4.11 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Lower call-centre volume and higher customer satisfaction after launch

Value-oriented metrics measure outcomes and benefits for the organisation and customers. Code volume, spend and meeting counts measure activity, not value.

**4.12 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. They are non-prescriptive and guide behaviour in every type of project

Principles are fundamental norms that guide behaviour; they do not prescribe specific procedures and they are consistent with, not a replacement for, the Code of Ethics.

### Chapter 5 — Life Cycles and Development Approaches

**5.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Iterative

Repeated cycles that improve the same solution through feedback describe an iterative approach. Incremental delivers usable pieces over time; predictive plans once and builds once.

**5.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Incremental delivery

Delivering usable portions of the product over time is incremental. Agile approaches combine incremental delivery with iterative refinement.

**5.3 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. A phase gate

A phase gate (stage gate, kill point) is a decision point at the end of a phase. The decision can be to continue, continue with modifications, repeat the phase or end the project.

**5.4 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Well-understood requirements, and changes after construction starts are very expensive

Stable, clear requirements and a high cost of change favour planning up front. The other characteristics favour adaptive approaches.

**5.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Hybrid

Combining adaptive delivery for the uncertain UI with a predictive approach for the regulated, sequential integration is a hybrid approach.

**5.6 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Probe, sense, respond — run safe-to-fail experiments and adapt

Complex situations have emergent cause and effect, so experimentation and feedback (as in agile) are appropriate. Best practice suits clear situations, expert analysis suits complicated ones, and acting first suits chaos.

**5.7 — Answer: A, C**

*Correct:* A. The likelihood of change in the project / C. The team's access to the customer or business representative

The filter assesses culture (buy-in, trust, decision-making), team (size, experience, access to the business) and project factors (likelihood of change, criticality, incremental delivery).

**5.8 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Changes are possible but go through formal change control against the baselines

Predictive approaches constrain change through baselines and change control, but changes still occur and are evaluated formally.

**5.9 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Rolling wave planning

Rolling wave planning is a form of progressive elaboration: near-term work is planned in detail, future work at a higher level.

**5.10 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Continuous delivery

Releasing features as soon as they are ready through automation is continuous delivery, typical of DevOps environments.

**5.11 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Predictive

High agreement and high certainty represent a simple situation in which detailed up-front planning works well.

**5.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Release plan or product roadmap

Roadmaps and release plans show features over time at a high level; iteration plans then add detail for each sprint.

### Chapter 6 — Focus Areas and the 40 Processes

**6.1 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 19

The distribution is Initiating 2, Planning 19, Executing 8, Monitoring and Controlling 10, Closing 1, totalling 40 processes.

**6.2 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Initiate Project or Phase and Identify Stakeholders

Initiating contains only Initiate Project or Phase (the charter) and Identify Stakeholders.

**6.3 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Assess and Implement Changes

Assess and Implement Changes, in the Governance domain, is the integrated change control process.

**6.4 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Develop Schedule

The Schedule domain has three processes. Develop Schedule absorbs activity definition, sequencing and duration estimating together with building the schedule model.

**6.5 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. None; the risk register is a project document updated as needed

Project documents such as the risk register, issue log and lessons learned register are updated as the project progresses. Changes to baselines and the project management plan require formal change control.

**6.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The WBS dictionary

The scope baseline consists of the approved project scope statement, the WBS (scope structure) and the WBS dictionary.

**6.7 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Work performance data

Raw observations from execution are work performance data. Once compared with the plan and analysed, they become information; compiled for stakeholders, they become reports.

**6.8 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Corrective action

Corrective action realigns current performance with the plan. Preventive action addresses future performance, defect repair fixes non-conforming products, and an update changes controlled documents.

**6.9 — Answer: B, E**

*Correct:* B. In adaptive approaches the focus areas occur within each iteration / E. Monitoring and Controlling activities occur throughout the project

Focus areas group work by its nature; they repeat in every phase or iteration and monitoring runs throughout. Closing applies to phases and contracts as well, and everything can be tailored.

**6.10 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Governance

Governance has Initiate Project or Phase (initiating), two planning processes, three executing, two monitoring and controlling, and Close Project or Phase (closing).

**6.11 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. It includes contingency reserves but excludes management reserves

The cost baseline is the approved time-phased budget including contingency reserves for identified risks. Management reserves, for unknown-unknowns, are part of the project budget but not the baseline.

**6.12 — Answer: 1→C, 2→A, 3→D, 4→B**

*Correct pairs:* 1. Create WBS → Develop Scope Structure; 2. Develop Team and Manage Team → Lead the Team; 3. Plan Cost Management → Plan Financial Management; 4. Collect Requirements → Elicit and Analyze Requirements

The Eighth Edition renames and merges several processes: Create WBS became Develop Scope Structure, the two team processes became Lead the Team, Plan Cost Management became Plan Financial Management and Collect Requirements became Elicit and Analyze Requirements.

**6.13 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Monitor and Control Project Performance

Monitor and Control Project Performance consolidates work performance information from the control processes into work performance reports. Manage Communications then distributes them.

### Chapter 7 — Governance Performance Domain

**7.1 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Time and materials with a not-to-exceed limit

When scope cannot yet be defined but work must start, T&M (ideally capped) or cost-reimbursable contracts are appropriate. Fixed-price contracts require a well-defined scope.

**7.2 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Firm fixed price

In an FFP contract the seller must deliver for the agreed price regardless of actual costs. Cost-reimbursable contracts shift most risk to the buyer, and T&M sits in between.

**7.3 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Scope creep; it should go through integrated change control

Uncontrolled additions requested by the customer are scope creep. Gold plating is when the team adds extras on its own initiative. The request should be captured as a change request and evaluated before implementation.

**7.4 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Evaluate its impact on scope, schedule, cost, quality, risk and value

Before a decision, the PM analyses the impact and options so the decision-maker can make an informed choice. Implementing before approval and rejecting without analysis are both wrong.

**7.5 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Explicit knowledge can be codified; tacit knowledge is personal and shared through interaction

Explicit knowledge is easily written down; tacit knowledge such as experience and judgment is transferred through conversation, observation and collaboration.

**7.6 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Throughout the project, then transferred to the organisation's repository at closure

Lessons learned are captured continuously so they can improve the current project as well as future ones; at closure they are transferred to the organisational lessons learned repository.

**7.7 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Obtain formal acceptance before completing closure

Closure requires formal acceptance, administrative closure, contract closure and lessons learned. Releasing resources before formal acceptance risks being unable to address remaining issues.

**7.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Manage Quality Assurance

Manage Quality Assurance in the Governance domain focuses on processes, using audits and process analysis. Inspecting deliverables is quality control within Monitor and Control Scope; formal acceptance is Validate Scope.

**7.9 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Fixed price incentive fee

FPIF combines a fixed-price structure with a target cost, target profit, share ratio and ceiling price, rewarding efficient performance.

**7.10 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Request for quotation

An RFQ is used when the item is well defined and price is the main criterion. RFPs suit complex solutions; RFIs gather market information early.

**7.11 — Answer: D, E**

*Correct:* D. Settling open claims and making final payments / E. Providing formal written notice that the contract is complete

Contract closure includes verifying deliverables, settling claims, final payments, formal notice of completion and archiving. RFPs, SOWs and bidder conferences belong to earlier procurement activities.

**7.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Perform closure: document the status, transfer completed work, close contracts and capture lessons

Even when a project is terminated early, closure is needed to protect the organisation, settle obligations and preserve knowledge.

**7.13 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Add it to the product backlog for the product owner to prioritise

In adaptive work, change is welcome but flows through the backlog, which the product owner orders by value. The sprint goal is normally protected from mid-sprint changes.

**7.14 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Configuration verification and audit

Identification defines controlled items, status accounting records and reports their state, and verification and audit confirm correctness and implementation of approved changes.

**7.15 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 40 days

Break-even: 12,000 + 100d = 400d, so 300d = 12,000 and d = 40. For more than 40 days, buying is cheaper.

### Chapter 8 — Scope Performance Domain

**8.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The WBS includes all of the project's work, and only that work

The 100% rule means the total of the work at the lowest levels rolls up to the total scope of the project, with nothing missing and nothing extra.

**8.2 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Work package

Work packages are the lowest level of the WBS. Activities are decomposed from work packages in schedule development and are not part of the WBS itself.

**8.3 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Validate Scope

Validate Scope takes verified deliverables (which passed quality control) and obtains formal acceptance from the customer or sponsor.

**8.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Gold plating

Gold plating is the team adding unrequested features on its own initiative. Scope creep is uncontrolled expansion, often from customer requests, without adjusting time and cost.

**8.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Requirements traceability matrix

The RTM traces requirements backward to their origin and forward to design, deliverables and tests, helping to ensure value and assess change impact.

**8.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Facilitate a workshop with both stakeholders to reach agreement

Facilitated workshops reconcile differences and build consensus. Choosing by seniority or doing both ignores value and creates waste.

**8.7 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Acceptance criteria are story-specific; the definition of done applies to every item

Acceptance criteria describe what a particular story must do to be accepted. The DoD is a shared checklist of quality conditions that every increment must meet.

**8.8 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Project exclusions

Explicitly stating what is out of scope manages expectations and prevents misunderstandings later.

**8.9 — Answer: B, E**

*Correct:* B. Negotiable / E. Testable

INVEST stands for Independent, Negotiable, Valuable, Estimable, Small and Testable. Value must be to the user or customer.

**8.10 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Non-functional requirement

Performance, capacity, security and usability qualities are non-functional requirements. Functional requirements describe behaviours of the product.

**8.11 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. They become expected basic features as customers get used to them

Delighters lose their novelty and become performance or basic expectations; yesterday's innovation becomes today's standard.

**8.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. The product owner

The product owner is accountable for maximising value and ordering the backlog, informed by stakeholders and the developers' input on risks and dependencies.

**8.13 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Within Monitor and Control Scope

Quality control merged into Monitor and Control Scope, producing verified deliverables. Assurance of processes is Manage Quality Assurance in Governance, and formal acceptance is Validate Scope.

**8.14 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. A minimum viable product

An MVP is the smallest product that validates the key assumption with real users, supporting the principle of focusing on value.

### Chapter 9 — Quality: Embedded Across Governance and Scope

**9.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The first is low grade but high quality; the second is high grade but low quality

Grade refers to the category of features; quality refers to how well requirements are met. Low grade can be acceptable; low quality is always a problem.

**9.2 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. External failure costs

Failures discovered by the customer, such as warranty work and liability, are external failure costs and are usually the most expensive.

**9.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. The process may be out of control and the cause should be investigated

Under the rule of seven, seven consecutive points on one side of the mean indicate a non-random pattern, so the process should be investigated even though no point is outside the limits.

**9.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Pareto diagram

A Pareto diagram ranks causes by frequency, highlighting the vital few that produce the majority of problems.

**9.5 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Auditing whether the team follows the agreed testing process

Assurance focuses on processes, for example quality audits. Inspecting and testing deliverables are quality control activities.

**9.6 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Precise but not accurate

Tightly clustered results are precise; being consistently off the true value means they are not accurate.

**9.7 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. The customer or the requirements

Specification limits reflect customer requirements. Control limits are calculated from process data, typically at plus or minus three sigma.

**9.8 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Keep the definition of done and adjust the sprint scope with the product owner

Quality is not negotiable within the definition of done. When time is short, the scope of the sprint is negotiated with the product owner rather than lowering quality.

**9.9 — Answer: C, D**

*Correct:* C. Training the team in a new coding standard / D. Design reviews before construction

Prevention costs are incurred to avoid defects, such as training and design reviews. Rework is internal failure, recalls are external failure and inspection is appraisal.

**9.10 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Scatter diagram

A scatter diagram plots two variables to reveal correlation. It does not by itself prove causation.

**9.11 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. As part of Define Scope

Quality planning has been incorporated into Define Scope, where quality requirements, metrics and the approach to verification are defined alongside the scope.

**9.12 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Philip Crosby

Crosby emphasised conformance to requirements, zero defects and the idea that the cost of non-conformance makes investment in quality pay for itself.

### Chapter 10 — Schedule Performance Domain

**10.1 — Answer: 10**

PERT expected duration = (O + 4M + P) / 6 = (6 + 36 + 18) / 6 = 60 / 6 = 10 days.

**10.2 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 2 days

Standard deviation = (P − O) / 6 = (18 − 6) / 6 = 2 days.

**10.3 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Fast tracking critical-path activities

Fast tracking overlaps activities that were planned in sequence. It adds risk and potential rework but little or no cost. Crashing adds cost; smoothing does not shorten the project.

**10.4 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Resource smoothing

Resource smoothing works only within float, so the end date does not change. Resource levelling may extend the schedule and change the critical path.

**10.5 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 14 days

Paths: A-B-E = 4 + 6 + 2 = 12; A-C-D-E = 4 + 3 + 5 + 2 = 14. The longest path, A-C-D-E, is critical at 14 days.

**10.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. 2 days

B can start at day 4 and finish at day 10. E cannot start before day 12 (after D). B's late finish is 12, late start 6, so total float = 6 − 4 = 2 days.

**10.7 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Mandatory

A mandatory (hard logic) dependency is inherent in the nature of the work. Discretionary dependencies reflect preferences and are the usual targets of fast tracking.

**10.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 4 weeks

Cycle time = WIP / throughput = 12 / 3 = 4 weeks. Reducing WIP is the most direct way to reduce cycle time.

**10.9 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 24 weeks

300 / 25 = 12 sprints; 12 sprints × 2 weeks = 24 weeks, assuming velocity and scope remain stable.

**10.10 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Burnup chart with a total-scope line

A burnup chart shows completed work against a separate total-scope line, so additions to scope are visible as the scope line rises.

**10.11 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Velocity is team-specific and should not be used to compare teams

Story points are relative to each team, so velocities are not comparable. Using velocity for comparison encourages point inflation and undermines trust.

**10.12 — Answer: D, E**

*Correct:* D. It is the longest path through the schedule network / E. Activities on it usually have zero total float

The critical path is the longest path and determines the shortest project duration; its activities normally have zero float. It can change during the project as activities are delayed or completed.

**10.13 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. A bottleneck in testing

A widening band means work is accumulating in that state faster than it leaves it, indicating a bottleneck that limits flow.

**10.14 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Start-to-finish

Finish-to-start is the most common; start-to-finish is rarely used, for example in shift handovers.

### Chapter 11 — Finance Performance Domain

**11.1 — Answer: 0.90**

CPI = EV / AC = 45,000 / 50,000 = 0.90. The project gets 90 cents of work for every dollar spent, so it is over budget.

**11.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. $625,000

CPI = 200,000 / 250,000 = 0.8. EAC = BAC / CPI = 500,000 / 0.8 = $625,000.

**11.3 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. −$20,000

SV = EV − PV = 200,000 − 220,000 = −$20,000, so the project is behind schedule.

**11.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 1.20

TCPI = (BAC − EV) / (BAC − AC) = 300,000 / 250,000 = 1.20. The team would need to be 20% more efficient than planned on the remaining work.

**11.5 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Contingency reserve

The cost baseline includes contingency reserves for identified risks. Management reserves for unknown-unknowns are added to obtain the project budget but are excluded from the baseline.

**11.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Ahead of schedule and over budget

SPI above 1 means more work was done than planned (ahead). CPI below 1 means the work cost more than budgeted (over budget).

**11.7 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. −25% to +75%

Early rough order of magnitude estimates are typically −25% to +75%. Definitive estimates later in planning are −5% to +10%.

**11.8 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Project A; opportunity cost $95,000

NPV already accounts for timing, so choose the higher NPV. The opportunity cost is the value of the option not chosen: $95,000.

**11.9 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. $310,000

For atypical variance, EAC = AC + (BAC − EV) = 80,000 + 230,000 = $310,000.

**11.10 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Sunk costs should not drive decisions about future investment

Money already spent cannot be recovered regardless of the decision. The decision should be based on future costs, benefits and value.

**11.11 — Answer: C, E**

*Correct:* C. It addresses unknown-unknowns / E. Using it normally requires approval from management and a change to the cost baseline

Management reserve covers unidentified risks; it is outside the cost baseline and its use requires management approval and a baseline change.

**11.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 4 years

Payback = initial investment / annual net inflow = 400,000 / 100,000 = 4 years.

**11.13 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. $200,000

PV = FV / (1 + r)^n = 242,000 / 1.21 = $200,000.

**11.14 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Perform funding limit reconciliation and adjust the schedule of work if needed

Funding limit reconciliation compares planned expenditure with funding constraints and may require rescheduling work so spending does not exceed available funds.

**12.1 — Answer: C**

Channels = n(n − 1)/2. With 8 people: 28. With 12 people: 66. The increase is 66 − 28 = 38.

High power, low interest stakeholders should be kept satisfied so they do not become obstacles. High–high is manage closely; low power–high interest is keep informed.

The salience model uses power, urgency and legitimacy. Grids use pairs such as power/interest or impact/influence.

The gap between current and desired engagement drives targeted engagement actions. Understanding the concerns is the first step to moving the stakeholder toward support.

Pull communication suits large volumes of information and large audiences who access it at their discretion.

**12.6 — Answer: A**

Complex or sensitive topics that require mutual understanding and decisions are best handled through real-time, multidirectional interactive communication.

Stakeholder identification is continuous. A newly discovered stakeholder must be recorded and analysed so that engagement can be planned.

**12.8 — Answer: D**

The sender encodes the message clearly and confirms it was understood; the receiver decodes it, acknowledges receipt and provides feedback.

The plan defines what, when, how often, in what format, by whom, to whom and through which methods information is communicated, plus escalation and constraints.

Monitoring communications means checking whether information needs are met and adapting the approach, for example an executive summary or a dashboard.

Information radiators make progress, impediments and work in progress highly visible and support transparency in adaptive teams.

**12.12 — Answer: D**

Outward refers to stakeholders outside the project team and organisation. Upward is senior management, downward is the team and sideward is peers.

Notes on resistance and strategies to change attitudes can be politically sensitive, so the PM uses judgment about how widely to share them.

**13.1 — Answer: D**

There should be only one Accountable person per activity to avoid confusion about ownership. Several people can be Responsible, Consulted or Informed.

**13.2 — Answer: B**

The team charter captures values, agreements and operating guidelines. It is most effective when the team develops it collaboratively and revisits it.

Fairness requires that all bidders receive the same information at the same time. Clarifications are shared with all prospective sellers through the formal channel.

Claims are resolved preferably through negotiation, then alternative dispute resolution; litigation is the last resort.

*Correct:* C. Discuss it privately with the team member to understand the causes

Performance issues are addressed early, privately and respectfully, focusing on causes and support. Escalation or replacement come later if needed.

Monitor and Control Resourcing covers physical and virtual resources and externally sourced resources. People performance is managed through Lead the Team.

The buyer manages the seller through the contract and the seller's management, not by directing the seller's employees. Concerns are raised formally and documented.

Bidder conferences are held before proposals are submitted so all prospective sellers receive the same information, which supports fairness.

Team charters include values, communication guidelines, decision-making and conflict resolution processes, meeting guidelines and team agreements.

The halo effect is assuming someone will be good at everything because they excel at one thing.

Retainage protects the buyer by holding back part of each payment until completion. Pre-agreed compensation for late delivery is liquidated damages.

### Chapter 14 — Risk Performance Domain

**14.1 — Answer: D**

EMV of the threat = 0.25 × −80,000 = −20,000. EMV of the opportunity = 0.40 × 30,000 = +12,000. Net = −$8,000.

Insurance transfers the financial impact to a third party in exchange for a premium. The risk itself is not eliminated.

**14.3 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* C. A workaround

A workaround is an unplanned response to an event or issue that occurs. Contingency and fallback plans are prepared in advance.

**14.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Sensitivity analysis displayed as a tornado diagram

Sensitivity analysis shows the relative impact of each uncertain variable; the tornado diagram displays the largest effects at the top.

**14.7 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Escalate the risk to the appropriate owner in the organisation

Risks outside the project's scope or authority should be escalated to the program, portfolio or relevant business owner, who takes ownership.

**14.8 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Residual risk

Residual risk remains after responses have been implemented. Secondary risk is a new risk created by the response itself.

**14.9 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Option Y, expected cost $114,000

X: 100,000 + 0.3 × 50,000 = $115,000. Y: 110,000 + 0.2 × 20,000 = $114,000. Y has the lower expected cost.

**14.10 — Answer: B, D**

*Correct:* B. Including risk response activities in the backlog and prioritising them with features / D. Using timeboxed spikes to reduce technical uncertainty

A risk-adjusted backlog and spikes reduce uncertainty early. Freezing the backlog, postponing risk discussion and eliminating demos increase risk.

**14.11 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. To evaluate how effective the risk process and responses are

Risk audits assess whether risk processes and responses are working. Identification and reassessment happen in other activities.

**14.12 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Appetite is the uncertainty accepted for reward; thresholds are measurable limits reflecting it

Appetite is a general willingness to take risk, and thresholds translate it into measurable limits, such as "no more than 5% cost variance".

**14.13 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. A target near 20 May gives 85% confidence; 1 May leaves a 50% chance of being late

Simulation results give confidence levels for dates. Communicating the probability helps stakeholders choose a target that matches their risk appetite.

**14.14 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Perform Risk Analysis

The Sixth Edition's two analysis processes were merged into Perform Risk Analysis.

### Chapter 15 — Agile and Hybrid Delivery in Depth

**15.1 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Documentation still has value, but delivering a working product is valued more

The manifesto values items on the left more than those on the right; items on the right still have value. Teams produce documentation that is useful, not documentation for its own sake.

**15.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The product owner

The product owner orders the backlog and maximises value. The scrum master serves the team and the organisation; developers decide how to build the increment.

**15.3 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Suggest they continue after the daily scrum so the event stays focused

The daily scrum is a 15-minute inspection of progress toward the sprint goal. Detailed problem-solving happens afterwards with the people involved.

**15.4 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The product owner

Only the product owner has the authority to cancel a sprint, usually when the sprint goal becomes obsolete.

**15.5 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. To create a pull system that improves flow and exposes bottlenecks

Limiting WIP reduces multitasking, shortens cycle time and reveals bottlenecks. Keeping everyone busy is not the goal; flow is.

**15.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Test-driven development

TDD follows a red-green-refactor cycle: write a failing test, write code to pass it, then improve the design.

**15.7 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. LeSS

Large-Scale Scrum (LeSS) scales Scrum with one product owner and one product backlog for many teams and minimal additional roles.

**15.8 — Answer: A, C**

*Correct:* A. Removing impediments that block the team / C. Shielding the team from interruptions so they can focus

Servant leaders remove impediments, protect focus, facilitate and coach. Assigning tasks, deciding designs and reporting individual metrics undermine self-organisation.

**15.9 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Task switching

Task switching causes time loss from context changes. Limiting WIP and dedicating people to teams reduce this waste.

**15.10 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Time, cost and quality

DSDM fixes time, cost and quality and flexes the scope using MoSCoW prioritisation.

**15.11 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. A working session where stakeholders inspect the increment and adapt the backlog

The sprint review inspects the product increment with stakeholders and adapts the backlog. The retrospective focuses on process improvement.

**15.12 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The lowest and highest estimators explain their reasoning, then the team re-estimates

Discussing outliers surfaces different assumptions and knowledge; the team then votes again to converge.

**15.13 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Aligning release plans with hardware milestones at defined integration points

Hybrid delivery requires explicit synchronisation of milestones and releases, managed dependencies and consistent governance and reporting.

**15.14 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. A timeboxed investigation to reduce uncertainty before committing

Spikes are short, timeboxed investigations that answer technical or design questions and reduce risk.

### Chapter 16 — Leading People: Vision, Teams, Conflict, Motivation, Influence and Ethics

**16.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Meet with both to understand their positions and help them find a solution

Collaborating or problem-solving addresses the root cause and builds commitment. Forcing may be needed in emergencies; withdrawing leaves the problem unresolved; escalation is premature.

**16.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Force/direct

In emergencies, safety, legal or ethical situations, a direct decision may be necessary. Collaboration is preferred when time and the situation allow.

**16.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Salary and working conditions

Hygiene factors prevent dissatisfaction but do not motivate on their own. Recognition, growth and responsibility are motivators.

**16.4 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Forming; clarify goals, roles and ground rules

In forming, people are getting oriented. The leader provides direction, clarifies objectives and roles, and helps the team establish agreements such as a team charter.

**16.5 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Delegating

High competence and high commitment call for a delegating style with low direction and low support.

**16.6 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Expert and referent power

Expert power (knowledge) and referent power (respect, credibility) build lasting influence. Coercive power tends to damage relationships.

**16.7 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Disclose the conflict and stay out of the selection decision

The Code of Ethics requires disclosure of real or potential conflicts of interest and refraining from influencing the decision unless the stakeholders approve after full disclosure.

**16.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Empathy

Empathy is the ability to understand others' emotions. Self-awareness and self-regulation concern one's own emotions.

**16.9 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. The team believes the target is unachievable, so expectancy is low

Motivation depends on expectancy, instrumentality and valence. If the team does not believe effort can achieve the target, a valuable reward will not motivate.

**16.10 — Answer: B, E**

*Correct:* B. Pairing operations staff with developers during the final iterations / E. Job shadowing and joint handover workshops before go-live

Tacit knowledge transfers best through interaction such as shadowing, pairing and workshops, which should start before closure. Sending documents at the end and single points of knowledge are weak practices.

**16.11 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Facilitate a session with the product owner and team to clarify the vision

A shared vision aligns priorities. Revisiting the vision and objectives with the product owner builds a common understanding without taking over the PO's decisions.

**16.12 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The best alternative to a negotiated agreement, i.e. the walk-away option

BATNA is your best option if negotiation fails. Knowing it prevents accepting a worse deal. ZOPA is the overlap between parties' acceptable ranges.

**16.13 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Offer ways to contribute that suit the person, such as one-to-one or written input

Cultural awareness and inclusion mean adapting how input is gathered so all voices are heard, without embarrassing anyone.

**16.14 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Coaching helps a person find their own solutions; mentoring shares the mentor's experience

Coaching is performance-focused and question-driven; mentoring is relationship- and experience-based, often career-oriented.

### Chapter 17 — The Business Environment: Compliance, Issues, Change, Improvement, Sustainability, AI and PMOs

**17.1 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Assess the regulation's impact with compliance or legal experts

Compliance is mandatory, so the PM first assesses the impact with experts, then processes the necessary changes through governance and updates plans and risks.

**17.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. The scrum master or servant-leader project manager

Servant leaders remove impediments the team cannot remove itself, such as access to environments or cross-department dependencies.

**17.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. ADKAR

ADKAR is Prosci's individual change model: awareness, desire, knowledge, ability and reinforcement.

**17.4 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Supportive

Supportive PMOs are consultative. Controlling PMOs require compliance, and directive PMOs manage projects directly.

**17.5 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Use only an approved tool for confidential data, follow policy and verify the summary

Responsible AI use protects confidentiality and complies with policy while keeping human verification of outputs.

**17.6 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Review and validate it with the team, checking assumptions and estimates

AI augments planning, but people remain accountable. Validating outputs with those who will do the work ensures accuracy and buy-in.

**17.7 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. A key supplier has announced it will stop production next week

An issue is a current condition that must be resolved now. The other options describe uncertain future events, which are risks.

**17.8 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Forming a powerful guiding coalition

The order is urgency, coalition, vision, communicate the vision, remove obstacles, short-term wins, build on the change, anchor it in the culture.

**17.9 — Answer: C, D**

*Correct:* C. Requiring suppliers to follow a code of conduct on labour practices / D. Including environmental and social criteria in the source selection criteria

Sustainable procurement considers environmental and social performance in selection and contracts, not price alone.

**17.10 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Agree a specific improvement experiment with an owner, and review its effect

Effective continuous improvement turns retrospective findings into concrete, owned actions that are tried, measured and reviewed.

**17.11 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Understand the reasons for resistance by engaging the users

Resistance often signals real concerns (training, usability, workload). Understanding them enables targeted actions such as training, communication or design changes.

**17.12 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Analyse the impact on the business case and present options to decision-makers

External environment changes should be assessed for impact and handled through governance with options for the sponsor or steering committee.

**17.13 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Leading indicators help predict results; lagging indicators measure them afterwards

For example, adoption rate during a pilot is a leading indicator of future revenue, which is a lagging indicator.


<a id="ans-18"></a>

### Chapter 18 — Tailoring

**18.1 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. To adapt the approach, governance and processes to the project's context

Tailoring adapts the way of working to the context. It neither applies everything blindly nor removes necessary controls or policies.

**18.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Select the initial development approach

The usual sequence is: select the initial development approach, tailor for the organisation, tailor for the project, then implement ongoing improvement.

**18.3 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Agree tailoring with the PMO, documenting that procurement is not applicable

Tailoring for the organisation means working with the PMO to adapt requirements to the context, documenting the rationale, rather than ignoring policy or producing waste.

**18.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Use adaptive practices for software, keeping the required regulatory controls

Tailoring can combine adaptive delivery with the rigour required by regulation. Compliance obligations cannot be tailored away.

**18.5 — Answer: A, C**

*Correct:* A. High cost of change after construction starts / C. Strict regulatory requirements for documented verification

High cost of change and strict regulation favour planning and formal controls. Engaged product owners, uncertain requirements and small experienced teams favour adaptive approaches.

**18.6 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Throughout the project, such as at phase gates and retrospectives

Implementing ongoing improvement means tailoring is reviewed and adjusted as the team and project learn.

**18.7 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Assess the context, product, team and stakeholders, and tailor with them

Tailoring starts with understanding the context. Copying an approach without considering differences is poor tailoring.

**18.8 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Removing change control from a fixed-price contract project to "be more agile"

Removing essential controls in a context that needs them, such as contract change control, is poor tailoring. The other examples adapt the approach to the context sensibly.

### Chapter 19 — Models, Methods and Artifacts: the Practitioner's Toolbox

**19.1 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Tuckman ladder

A model is a thinking strategy that explains a phenomenon, such as team development. Planning poker is a method; the risk register and burnup chart are artifacts.

**19.2 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Decision log

A decision log captures decisions and their rationale. Issue, assumption and change logs serve other purposes.

**19.3 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Make-or-buy analysis

Make-or-buy analysis compares the costs, risks and benefits of internal development versus purchase.

**19.4 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. S-curve

The time-phased cost baseline, plotted cumulatively, typically forms an S-curve.

**19.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Value stream mapping

Value stream mapping shows value-adding and non-value-adding time across the whole flow, highlighting waste such as waiting.

**19.6 — Answer: 1→A, 2→C, 3→B, 4→D**

*Correct pairs:* 1. Record a current problem with an owner and due date → Issue log; 2. Record a condition believed to be true for planning purposes → Assumption log; 3. Record an uncertain event with probability and impact → Risk register; 4. Track submitted changes and their approval status → Change log

Issues are current problems, assumptions are beliefs used for planning, risks are uncertain events, and the change log tracks change requests and decisions.

**19.7 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Customer loyalty and willingness to recommend

NPS subtracts the percentage of detractors from the percentage of promoters, measuring customer loyalty.

**19.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Story map

Story mapping arranges stories by user activities horizontally and by priority vertically, helping plan releases.

**19.9 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Regression analysis

Regression analysis examines relationships among variables to support forecasting.

**19.10 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. A dashboard

Dashboards summarise key performance information visually for decision-makers.

### Chapter 20 — Formulas and Calculations Master Sheet

**20.1 — Answer: 0.9**

SPI = EV / PV = 54,000 / 60,000 = 0.9. The project has completed 90% of the work planned to date.

**20.2 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. $500,000

With atypical variance, EAC = AC + (BAC − EV) = 400,000 + 500,000 = 900,000. ETC = EAC − AC = $500,000.

**20.3 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. 45

7 people: 21 channels. 12 people: 66 channels. The increase is 45.

**20.4 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. AC + bottom-up ETC

When the original estimating assumptions are no longer valid, the team re-estimates the remaining work bottom-up and adds it to the actual cost.

**20.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. 16 to 24 days

About 95% of outcomes fall within ±2 standard deviations: 20 ± 4 = 16 to 24 days.

**20.6 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. 4 years

Payback = 60,000 / 15,000 = 4 years.

**20.7 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. The remaining work must be done 35% more efficiently than planned to meet the budget

A TCPI above 1 means future performance must exceed planned efficiency. A value such as 1.35 often signals that the BAC is no longer achievable without changes.

**20.8 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. $5,000

(Cost − salvage) / life = (25,000 − 5,000) / 4 = $5,000 per year.

**20.9 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. More than 90 days

Break-even d = 54,000 / (900 − 300) = 90 days. Beyond 90 days, buying is cheaper.

**20.10 — Answer: 6**

160 / 30 = 5.33, rounded up to 6 sprints, because a partial sprint still has to be scheduled.

### Chapter 21 — Exam Strategy and the PMI Mindset

**21.1 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. An action that gathers information and assesses the situation first

"First" usually points to understanding the situation and its impact before taking larger actions such as escalation or termination.

**21.2 — Answer: D**

*Correct:* D. Explain that the product owner prioritises new work, and connect them

In agile contexts, the product owner prioritises backlog items. The PM facilitates the connection and protects the sprint goal rather than refusing outright.

**21.3 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Escalating to the sponsor before trying to understand or resolve the problem

Premature escalation is a common wrong answer. Escalation is appropriate when an issue exceeds the PM's authority or the team's ability, ideally with options.

**21.4 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. Review the flagged questions in the first section, since they cannot be revisited

Once a break begins, the previous section is locked. Reviewing flagged questions beforehand prevents losing the chance to change answers.

**21.5 — Answer: A**

*Correct:* A. Report the true status, with recovery options, and discuss the concern with the sponsor

Honesty is a mandatory standard of the Code of Ethics. The PM reports accurately and provides options, while working with the sponsor.

**21.6 — Answer: B**

*Correct:* B. Integrated change control: assess the impact and submit a change request

The scenario indicates a predictive context, so changes to the baseline go through integrated change control.

**21.7 — Answer: A, C**

*Correct:* A. Ask which option addresses the root cause rather than a symptom / C. Ask which option would logically happen first in the sequence

Root cause and sequence are reliable tie-breakers. Length, jargon and seniority are not indicators of the best answer.

**21.8 — Answer: C**

*Correct:* C. About 80 seconds per question, reaching question 60 around the 80-minute mark

240 minutes for 180 questions is about 1.33 minutes per question, so 60 questions take about 80 minutes.
